# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 33

## Page 15965

Part Thirty-Three
The Book of Divorce

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise belongs to Allah alone, and blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islam Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise belongs to Allah. We seek His help, guidance and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bestow upon him blessings and abundant peace.

Chapter: Divorce in Accordance with the Sunnah and Innovated Divorce

Section

A brief account of “which forms of divorce are lawful and which are prohibited,” and whether a prohibited divorce takes effect or not.

We say:

Some forms of divorce are prohibited by the Quran, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus, while others are not prohibited. Thus, “permissible divorce,” by scholarly consensus, is for a man to divorce

---

## Page 15966

his wife with a single pronouncement of divorce once her menstruation has ended, after she has performed the ritual bath and before he has intercourse with her. He must then leave her without pronouncing another divorce until her waiting period has ended. This is called “divorce in accordance with the Sunnah.” If he wishes to take her back during the waiting period, he may do so without her consent or that of her guardian, and without a new dower.

If he leaves her until her waiting period has ended, he must release her with kindness, for the marriage bond between them has now been severed. If he wishes to marry her after the waiting period has ended, he may do so, but a marriage contract is required, just as when marrying her for the first time or when another man marries her. Should he then take her back during the waiting period, or marry her after it has ended, and wish to divorce her, he must divorce her as described above. Then, if he takes her back or marries her a second time and wishes to divorce her, he must again divorce her as described above.

Once he has divorced her for the third time, she becomes unlawful to him until she marries another husband, as Allah and His Messenger have ordained. She does not then become lawful to him unless another man marries her in the customary manner in which people marry, wishing to be married to the woman, and subsequently separates from her. If, however, he marries her with the intention of making her lawful for another man, this is prohibited according to most scholars, as has been transmitted from the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and others, and as indicated by the Prophetic texts and the evidence of the Sharia. Some scholars have permitted this, as has been explained elsewhere.

---

## Page 15967

If the woman does not menstruate because of her young or advanced age, he may divorce her whenever he wishes, whether or not he has had intercourse with her, for her waiting period is three months. Thus, whenever he divorces her, the timing is appropriate for the commencement of her waiting period, since her waiting period is reckoned neither by menstrual cycles nor by pregnancy. Some scholars call this “divorce in accordance with the Sunnah,” while others classify it as neither “divorce in accordance with the Sunnah” nor “innovated divorce.”

If he divorces her during menstruation, or after having intercourse with her and before her pregnancy becomes evident, this divorce is prohibited and is called “innovated divorce.” It is forbidden by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus.

If her pregnancy has become evident and he wishes to divorce her, he may do so. Is this called divorce in accordance with the Sunnah, or is it classified as neither divorce in accordance with the Sunnah nor innovated divorce? The disagreement here is merely terminological. As for the prohibited divorce pronounced during menstruation, or after intercourse and before pregnancy becomes evident, does it take effect or not, whether pronounced once or three times? There are two well-known views on this among the earlier and later scholars.

If he divorces her three times during a single period of purity, whether in one statement or several—for example, by saying, “You are divorced three times”; or, “You are divorced, and divorced, and divorced”; or, “You are divorced, then divorced, then divorced”; or by saying, “You are divorced,” then saying, “You are divorced,” then saying, “You are divorced”; or by saying, “You are divorced three times,” or “ten times,” or “a hundred times,” or

---

## Page 15968

“a thousand times,” or similar expressions—scholars, both earlier and later, hold three views on this, whether or not the marriage has been consummated. Some early scholars distinguished between consummated and unconsummated marriages. There is also a fourth view, which is a later innovation.

The first view is that such a divorce is permissible and takes effect. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and the earlier position reported from Ahmad, which al-Khiraqī adopted.

The second view is that such a divorce is prohibited but takes effect. This is the view of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, and the later position reported from Ahmad, which most scholars of his school adopted. This view is transmitted from many early scholars among the Companions and Successors, while the preceding view is transmitted from some of them.

The third view is that it is prohibited, but only a single divorce takes effect. This view is transmitted from a number of earlier and later scholars, including Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, such as al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām and ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf. Both views are reported from ʿAlī, Ibn Masʿūd, and Ibn ʿAbbās. It is also the view of many Successors and those who came after them, such as Tāwūs, Khilās ibn ʿAmr, and Muhammad ibn Ishāq, as well as Dāwūd and most of his followers. It is also reported from Abū Jaʿfar Muhammad ibn ʿAlī ibn al-Husayn and his son Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad; this is why some of the Shia adopted it. It is also the view of some followers of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal.

---

## Page 15969

As for the “fourth view,” held by some Muʿtazilites and Shia—that no divorce takes effect at all—it is not known to have been held by any of the early generations.

The “third view” is the one supported by the Quran and Sunnah. Every divorce that Allah has prescribed in the Quran for a woman whose marriage has been consummated is a revocable divorce. Allah has not prescribed that anyone pronounce all three divorces at once, nor has He prescribed that a man irrevocably divorce a woman whose marriage he has consummated. Rather, she becomes irrevocably separated from him if he divorces her before consummation, and she becomes irrevocably separated from him when her waiting period expires.

By agreement of the Muslims, divorce is of “three types”:

“Revocable divorce”: the husband may take her back without her consent, and if either spouse dies during the waiting period, the other inherits from the deceased.

“Irrevocable divorce”: the former husband becomes merely one suitor among others, and she is not lawful for him except through a new marriage contract.

“Divorce that makes her unlawful to him”: she is not lawful for him until she marries another husband. This occurs when he divorces her three times in the manner permitted by Allah and His Messenger: he divorces her, then takes her back during the waiting period or remarries her; he then divorces her again, then takes her back or remarries her; and then he pronounces the third divorce. By agreement of the scholars, this is the divorce that makes her unlawful to him until she marries another husband.

Neither the Book of Allah nor the Sunnah of His Messenger provides for an irrevocable divorce of a woman whose marriage has been consummated that counts as one of the three divorces. This is why the position held by the hadith jurists—such as Imam Ahmad, according to the apparent position of his school, al-Shāfiʿī in one of his two views, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Abū Thawr, and Ibn al-Mundhir

---

## Page 15970

and Dāwūd, Ibn Khuzaymah, and others—is that khulʿ (compensated marital separation) dissolves the marriage and irrevocably separates the spouses, without counting as one of the three divorces. This is what is established from the Companions, such as Ibn ʿAbbās. It is likewise established from ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, Ibn ʿAbbās, and others that a woman who obtains khulʿ need not observe a waiting period of three monthly courses; rather, she need only wait for one menstrual period. This is the view of Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Ibn al-Mundhir, and others, and it is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

Well-known hadiths concerning this have been narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the Sunan collections, corroborating one another. He explained that this is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said that it had been reported from a group of Companions that they regarded khulʿ as divorce. However, leading hadith scholars—including Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ibn Khuzaymah, Ibn al-Mundhir, al-Bayhaqī, and others—judged that report weak, as has been transmitted from them concerning this matter.

Khulʿ is when a woman offers her husband compensation so that he will separate from her. Allah Most High says:

“Divorced women shall wait for three monthly courses. It is not lawful for them to conceal what Allah has created in their wombs, if they believe in Allah and the Last Day. Their husbands have a greater right to take them back during this period, if they desire reconciliation. Women have rights similar to their obligations, according to what is reasonable, but men have a degree above them. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.”

“Divorce is twice; then either retain her on reasonable terms or release her with kindness. It is not lawful for you to take back anything you have given them, unless both fear that they cannot keep within Allah’s limits. If you fear that they cannot keep within Allah’s limits, there is no blame on either of them concerning what she gives to secure her release. These are Allah’s limits, so do not transgress them. Whoever transgresses Allah’s limits—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

“Then if he divorces her, she is not lawful to him

---

## Page 15971

thereafter until she marries another husband. If he then divorces her, there is no blame on the two of them if they return to one another, provided they believe they can observe Allah’s limits. These are Allah’s limits, which He makes clear to people who know.”

“When you divorce women and they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them on reasonable terms or release them on reasonable terms. Do not retain them to harm them and thereby transgress. Whoever does so has wronged himself. Do not make a mockery of Allah’s revelations. Remember Allah’s blessing upon you, and the Book and wisdom He has sent down to you, through which He admonishes you. Fear Allah, and know that Allah has knowledge of all things.”

He, glorified be He, thus explained that women divorced after consummation must wait for three monthly courses. According to most of the Companions—including ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Ibn Mas‘ūd, Abū Mūsā, and others—a “monthly course” means a menstrual period. A woman therefore remains in her waiting period until her third menstrual period has ended. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad, according to the better-known of the two reports from him. Ibn ‘Umar, ‘Ā’ishah, and others held that her waiting period ends when her third menstrual period begins. This is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī.

As for a woman divorced before consummation, Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, when you marry believing women and then divorce them before you have touched them, you have no waiting period to count for them. So provide for them and release them graciously.”

Then He said:

“Their husbands have a greater right to take them back during this period”—that is, during that waiting period.

Then He said:

“Divorce is twice.”

He thus explained that the divorce He mentioned—the revocable divorce during which the husband has a greater right to take his wife back—occurs (twice, once after another. It is like telling a man, “Glorify Allah twice,” or “Glorify Allah three times,” or “a hundred times.” He must then say, “Glory be to Allah. Glory be to Allah,” until he has completed the required number. But if he wished to summarize

---

## Page 15972

this by saying, “Glory be to Allah twice,” or “a hundred times,” he would have glorified Allah only once. Allah Most High did not say, “Divorce consists of two pronouncements.” Rather, He said, “Twice.” Thus, if a man tells his wife, “You are divorced twice,” or “three times,” or “ten times,” or “a thousand times,” he has divorced her only once.

As for the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) to the Mother of the Believers, Juwayriyyah:

“Since leaving you, I have said four phrases which, if weighed against everything you have said today, would equal them in weight: Glory be to Allah to the number of His creation. Glory be to Allah to the weight of His Throne. Glory be to Allah to the measure of His pleasure. Glory be to Allah to the measure of the ink of His words.”

Muslim recorded this in his Sahīh. Its meaning is that He, glorified be He, deserves glorification in such measure. It is like the Prophet’s saying (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Our Lord, to You belongs all praise, enough to fill the heavens, to fill the earth, to fill all that is between them, and to fill whatever else You will.”

It does not mean that the person has actually glorified Him to that extent. A measure may describe the servant’s action, which is finite; or it may describe what the Lord deserves, and that is what is immeasurably great. Otherwise, if someone praying were to say during his prayer, “Glory be to Allah to the number of His creation,” he would still have glorified Allah only once.

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prescribed glorifying Allah thirty-three times after every prayer, praising Him thirty-three times, and declaring His greatness thirty-three times. If someone were to say, “Glory be to Allah, praise be to Allah, and Allah is greatest—to the number of His creation,” he would have glorified Allah only once.

We know of no instance during the Prophet’s lifetime in which a man divorced his wife three times in a single utterance and the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) held him bound by all three divorces. Nor has any authentic or good hadith been transmitted to that effect, nor have the authors of the authoritative books transmitted anything to that effect;

---

## Page 15973

rather, reports have been transmitted on this matter, but the hadith scholars agree that they are all weak—indeed, fabricated.

What is recorded in Sahīh Muslim and other Sunan and Musnad collections, however, is the report transmitted by Tāwūs from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), during Abū Bakr’s caliphate, and for two years of ʿUmar’s caliphate, a triple divorce counted as one. Then ʿUmar said, ‘People have been hasty in a matter in which they had time to proceed deliberately. What if we were to hold them to it?’ So he held them to it.”

In another version recorded by Muslim and others, Tāwūs reported that Abū al-Sahbāʾ said to Ibn ʿAbbās:

“Do you know that three divorces were counted as one during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), during Abū Bakr’s caliphate, and for three years of ʿUmar’s rule?”

Ibn ʿAbbās replied, “Yes.”

In another version, Abū al-Sahbāʾ said to Ibn ʿAbbās:

“Tell me something from your reports: did not a triple divorce count as one during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and during Abū Bakr’s caliphate?”

He replied:

“That was indeed the case. But during ʿUmar’s time, people began resorting to divorce in rapid succession, so he made it binding upon them.”

Imam Ahmad narrated in his Musnad: Saʿīd ibn Ibrāhīm told us: My father told us, from Muhammad ibn Ishāq, who said: Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn told me, from ʿIkrimah, the freedman of Ibn ʿAbbās, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said:

“Rukānah ibn ʿAbd Yazīd, a member of Banū al-Muttalib, divorced his wife three times in a single sitting and grieved deeply over losing her. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) asked him, ‘How did you divorce her?’

“He replied, ‘I divorced her three times.’

“He asked, ‘In a single sitting?’

“He replied, ‘Yes.’

“He said, ‘That counts only as one divorce, so take her back if you wish.’ So he took her back.”

Ibn ʿAbbās therefore held that divorce was to be pronounced in each period of purity. Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Maqdisī also included this report in his book al-Mukhtārah, which is more authentic than Sahīh al-Hākim. Abū Dāwūd and others likewise transmitted it through the report of

---

## Page 15974

And the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), “In a single sitting,” imply that, had the pronouncements not occurred in a single sitting, the ruling would not have been the same. For if they had occurred in separate sittings, he might ordinarily have taken her back between them, since she was living with him; and a divorce pronounced after taking one’s wife back takes effect. An implication, however, does not apply universally to every case left unstated; rather, such cases may require further distinctions. Consider his words:

“When water reaches two large jarfuls, it does not admit impurity,” or, “Nothing renders it impure.”

When it reaches two large jarfuls, it may or may not admit impurity. Likewise, he said:

“Zakat is due on grazing camels.”

If they are not grazing camels, zakat may still be due on them—as trade goods—or it may not. Similarly, he said:

“Whoever stands in prayer on the Night of Decree with faith and seeking reward will have his past sins forgiven.”

Someone who does not stand in prayer that night may nevertheless be forgiven for another reason. Likewise, he said:

“Whoever fasts Ramadan with faith and seeking reward will have his past sins forgiven.”

And Allah, exalted is He, says:

“Indeed, those who have believed and those who have emigrated and striven in the cause of Allah—it is they who hope for Allah’s mercy.”

Someone who does not meet that description may, alongside his faith, perform another deed through which he hopes for Allah’s mercy, or he may not.

Thus, if the divorce pronouncements occurred in separate sittings, he may or may not have taken her back between them. This differs from a single sitting, during which a man does not ordinarily take his wife back between pronouncements. In that case, he retains the right to take her back, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Take her back if you wish.”

He did not say what he said in the hadith of Ibn ʿUmar:

“Tell him to resume marital life with her.”

He thus instructed him to revoke the divorce, which the husband can do unilaterally, unlike resuming marital life together. Abū Dāwūd and others also narrated:

“Rukānah divorced his wife using the word ‘absolutely.’ The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) asked him, ‘By Allah, did you intend only one divorce?’ He replied, ‘I intended only one divorce by it.’

---

## Page 15975

So the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) restored her to him.”

Abū Dāwūd, who did not narrate in his Sunan the hadith that Ahmad included in his Musnad, said:

“The hadith mentioning ‘absolutely’ is more authentic than Ibn Jurayj’s report that ‘Rukānah pronounced three divorces upon his wife,’ because his own family knew better.”

However, the leading imams versed in the hidden defects of hadith and their legal interpretation—such as Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, al-Bukhārī and others, as well as Abū ʿUbayd, Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm and others—judged the hadith mentioning ‘absolutely’ to be weak. They explained that its narrators were unknown persons whose integrity and accuracy had not been established. Ahmad affirmed the authenticity of the report mentioning three divorces and explained that it was the correct version. For example, he said:

“It is not established in the hadith of Rukānah that he divorced his wife using the word ‘absolutely.’”

He also said:

“The hadith of Rukānah mentioning ‘absolutely’ carries no weight, because Ibn Ishāq narrates it from Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn, from ʿIkrimah, from Ibn ʿAbbās, stating that ‘Rukānah pronounced three divorces upon his wife.’ The people of Medina describe someone who pronounces three divorces as having divorced ‘absolutely.’”

Ahmad initially set aside Ibn ʿAbbās’s hadith only because he considered pronouncing three divorces permissible, agreeing with al-Shāfiʿī. It was therefore possible to say that Rukānah’s hadith had been abrogated. Then, when he retracted that position and it became clear that the Quran and Sunnah permit no divorce other than a revocable divorce, he set aside Ibn ʿAbbās’s hadith because Ibn ʿAbbās had issued a ruling contrary to it. According to one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, this constitutes a defect in the report. According to the other position, however—which his followers adopted—it does not. It therefore follows that his position entails acting upon Ibn ʿAbbās’s hadith.

Elsewhere, he has explained the grounds excusing the imams who exercised independent legal judgment—may Allah be pleased with them—and held those who pronounced all three divorces together bound by all three. One such imam was ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him). For when he saw that people were frequently committing what Allah had forbidden them—combining three divorces—and would not desist without punishment,

---

## Page 15976

he decided to punish them by holding them bound by all three divorces, to deter them from doing so. This was either a temporary discretionary punishment imposed when needed—just as he would administer eighty lashes for drinking intoxicants, shave the offender’s head and exile him, and just as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prohibited the three who had stayed behind from being with their wives—or because he thought that counting the three as one had depended upon a condition that no longer existed. He took a similar view concerning combining ‘umrah with Hajj, either generally or specifically by converting Hajj into ‘umrah.

Compelling separation when someone fails to fulfil his obligations is a matter open to independent legal judgment. Sometimes separation is a right belonging to the wife, as in the case of an impotent husband or one who has sworn to abstain from marital relations, according to the majority of scholars, and a husband unable to provide maintenance, according to those who hold this view.

At other times, it is said to be a right of Allah. Examples include the two arbiters’ separation of a married couple, according to the majority, when the arbiters are not treated as agents; and divorce taking effect against a husband who has sworn to abstain from marital relations if he does not resume them within the waiting period, according to those among the earlier and later scholars who hold this view. Likewise, some jurists among Ahmad’s followers and others have stated that if both spouses willingly engage in anal intercourse, they are to be separated.

If a righteous father orders his son to divorce because he considers it to be in his son’s best interests, the son must obey him, as Ahmad and others have stated, just as:

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) instructed ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar to obey his father when his father ordered him to divorce his wife.”

Thus, compelling separation—whether by the Lawgiver or by the ruler—when the husband fails to fulfil his obligations is a matter open to independent legal judgment. Since people would commit the forbidden act unless they were held bound by all three divorces, ‘Umar decided to hold them bound by them, because they had failed to remain obedient to Allah and His Messenger while the marriage remained intact. Yet many of the Companions

---

## Page 15977

and the Successors disputed this view, either because they did not approve of such a punitive measure or because the Lawgiver had not imposed such a punishment. This concerns those who deserve punishment. As for someone who does not deserve it because of ignorance or an interpretation he has adopted, there is no basis for holding him bound by all three divorces.

This is a ruling prescribed by the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), just as he prescribed comparable rulings; he did not restrict its application. Thus, some earlier and later scholars have stated that what the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prescribed concerning converting Hajj into ‘umrah—performing ‘umrah followed by Hajj, as he instructed his Companions during the Farewell Pilgrimage—is a generally applicable ruling, as he explained:

“When he was asked, ‘Is this ‘umrah of ours for this year only, or forever?’ he replied, ‘No; rather, forever and ever. ‘Umrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.’”

The claim that this was prescribed only for the elders, for a reason peculiar to them—such as demonstrating the permissibility of performing ‘umrah during the months of Hajj—is unsound, for reasons explained at length elsewhere.

Allah Most High has said:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and leads to the best outcome.”

He thus commanded the believers, when they disagree, to refer their dispute to Allah and the Messenger. Whatever the earlier and later generations have disputed must therefore be referred to the Quran and Sunnah. Nothing in the Quran or Sunnah requires holding someone bound by all three divorces when he pronounces them together, whether in a single utterance or several utterances, without an intervening resumption of the marriage or a new marriage contract. Rather, the Quran and Sunnah require holding someone bound by them only when he has divorced in the manner permitted by Allah and His Messenger. And in support of this view

---

## Page 15978

are legal analogy and reasoning based on the other foundational principles of Islamic law. Any contract that is permissible in some circumstances and forbidden in others—such as a sale or marriage—does not, when concluded in a forbidden manner, have the binding legal effect of a lawful contract permitted by Allah and His Messenger. Thus, Muslims agree that marriages forbidden by Allah, such as marriages within the prohibited degrees or marriage to a woman during her waiting period, are void and nonbinding. The same applies to the sale of things Allah has forbidden, such as wine, swine, and carrion.

This differs from acts that are forbidden by their very nature, such as zihār (declaring one’s wife forbidden by likening her to a prohibited female relative), accusations of unchastity, lying, false testimony, and the like. Whoever commits such an act deserves punishment in accordance with the rulings Allah has prescribed. These acts are not lawful in some circumstances and unlawful in others, such that they might sometimes be valid and sometimes invalid.

Other transactions are forbidden for one party but permissible for the other. Examples include ransoming a captive; purchasing someone whose emancipation is denied; bribing an oppressor to ward off his oppression or secure a right he is obliged to grant; buying an animal whose milk has deliberately been allowed to accumulate in its udder, or an item whose defect has been concealed; giving money to those whose hearts are to be won over so that they fulfil an obligation or refrain from something forbidden; and an incoming trader selling to someone who intercepts him. In such cases and others like them, the wronged party may permissibly enter into the transaction and may either rescind the contract or uphold it. By contrast, the wrongdoer’s act is not binding.

Divorce is among the things Allah has permitted in some circumstances and forbidden in others. When it is carried out in a manner forbidden by Allah and His Messenger, it does not have the binding legal effect of what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful. As is reported in the two Sahīh collections from ‘Ā’ishah (may Allah be pleased with her), the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

---

## Page 15979

“Whoever performs a deed that is not in accordance with our command will have it rejected.”

Allah Most High has said:

“Divorce is twice; then either retain her honorably or release her graciously.”

He thus made clear that the divorce Allah has prescribed for a woman with whom the marriage has been consummated—namely, revocable divorce—may occur twice. After these two divorces, there is either “retaining her honorably,” by taking her back so that she remains his wife, with one divorce remaining; or “releasing her graciously,” by letting her go when her waiting period ends. As Allah Most High has said:

“O you who believe, when you marry believing women and then divorce them before you have touched them, you have no waiting period to count for them. So provide for them and release them graciously.”

Then, after that, He said:

“It is not lawful for you to take back anything you have given them, unless both fear that they cannot keep within Allah’s limits. If you fear that they cannot keep within Allah’s limits, there is no blame upon either of them concerning what she gives to secure her release.”

This is khulʿ (divorce in return for compensation). He called it “ransoming,” because the woman ransoms herself from her husband’s captivity, just as a captive or slave secures his release by paying his captor or master.

Allah Most High said:

“Then if he divorces her”—meaning the third divorce—“she is not lawful for him thereafter until she marries another husband.”

“Then if he divorces her”—meaning this second husband—“there is no blame upon either of them”—meaning her and her first husband—“if they return to one another, provided they believe that they can keep within Allah’s limits.”

Likewise, Allah Most High said:

“O Prophet, when you divorce women, divorce them at the proper time for their waiting period, and keep count of the waiting period. Fear Allah, your Lord. Do not turn them out of their homes, nor should they leave, unless they commit a manifest indecency. These are Allah’s limits. Whoever transgresses Allah’s limits has wronged himself. You do not know: perhaps Allah will bring about a new situation thereafter.”

“Then, when they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honorably or part with them honorably. Call two just men from among you as witnesses, and bear witness for Allah. This is an admonition for whoever believes in Allah and the Day

---

## Page 15980

of Judgment. Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him.”

“And He will provide for him from where he does not expect. Whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him. Allah will surely accomplish His purpose. Allah has appointed a measure for everything.”

The authentic hadith collection, along with the Sunan and Musnad collections, reports from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar:

“He divorced his wife while she was menstruating. ʿUmar mentioned this to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Prophet became angry with him. He said: ‘Tell him to take her back until she menstruates and then becomes pure. Thereafter, if he wishes, he may keep her, and if he wishes, he may divorce her before having intercourse with her. That is the waiting period for which Allah has commanded that women be divorced.’”

Another narration in the authentic collection states:

“He instructed him to divorce her when she was free from menstruation or pregnant.”

Another narration in the authentic collection states:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited: ‘When you divorce women, divorce them at the proper time for their waiting period.’”

It is reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and other Companions:

“Divorce takes four forms: two are lawful and two are unlawful. The two lawful forms are to divorce one’s wife during a period of purity in which no intercourse has occurred, or to divorce her while she is pregnant and her pregnancy has become evident. The two unlawful forms are to divorce her while she is menstruating, or to divorce her after intercourse without knowing whether her womb contains a child.”

This was reported by al-Dāraqutnī and others.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made clear that a man may not divorce his wife unless she has become pure after menstruation and before he has intercourse with her. This is divorce timed for the waiting period—that is, for its commencement—for that is the period of purity or the waiting period. If he divorces her before the waiting period, he has divorced her before the time Allah has permitted, prolonged her waiting, and divorced her without having any need for

---

## Page 15981

divorcing her. Divorce is, in principle, something Allah dislikes; it is the most disliked of lawful things to Allah. He has permitted it only insofar as people need it, just as prohibited things may be permitted because of need. Thus, after the third divorce, He has forbidden her to him until she marries another husband, as a punishment intended to deter him from repeatedly divorcing her. Once he divorces her, she remains in the waiting period for three cycles, while he retains his marital rights over her: he inherits from her, and she inherits from him. He gains nothing by hastening divorce before its proper time, just as nothing is gained by anticipating the imam’s movements in prayer. Accordingly, whatever he does before the imam does not count; indeed, according to one of the two scholarly views, his prayer becomes invalid if he does so deliberately. Yet he still remains with the imam in prayer until the final salutation.

For this reason, most scholars have permitted khulʿ (marital dissolution in return for compensation) during menstruation. According to the jurists of the hadith tradition, it is not divorce but an irrevocable separation. According to one of their two views, her freedom from pregnancy is established by a single menstrual period, and she has no prescribed waiting period. This is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. Moreover, through khulʿ she gains control over her own marital affairs, so both spouses benefit from hastening the irrevocable separation to end the harm between them. This differs from revocable divorce, which offers no benefit when hastened before its proper time; rather, that brings harm without any good.

It has also been said:

“Such a divorce occurs when he feels no desire for her and may have no need to divorce her. By contrast, divorce at a time when he desires her can only be prompted by need.”

Scholars have differed over what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended by his instruction in Ibn ʿUmar’s case: “Tell him to take her back.” One group of scholars understood this to mean that the divorce had taken effect, so he was instructed to take her back and then, if he wished, divorce her during a period of purity. There was also disagreement

---

## Page 15982

among these scholars: Is taking her back obligatory or recommended? May he take her back during the first or second period of purity? And what is the wisdom behind this prohibition? There are several views, which we have discussed, together with their grounds, elsewhere.

Another group understood that the divorce had not taken effect. Rather, he had physically separated from her, as customarily happens when a man divorces his wife: he withdraws from her, and she withdraws from him. Thus, the Prophet “said to ʿUmar: ‘Tell him to resume life with her.’” He did not say, “Tell him to take her back.” The term murājaʿah (returning to one another) has a reciprocal form involving both parties: she returns to him physically, and they are reunited as before, because his divorce had not taken effect. Then, when the time at which Allah permits divorce arrives, he may divorce her if he wishes.

These scholars argued: Had the divorce taken effect, there would have been no benefit in commanding him to take her back so that he could divorce her a second time. Rather, this would harm them both. Explicit scriptural evidence and scholarly consensus establish that he may divorce her after taking her back. In that case, adding this divorce to the first would only increase the number of divorces, prolong the waiting period, and torment both spouses. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not oblige him to have intercourse with her before divorcing her. Indeed, if he had intercourse with her, he would not be permitted to divorce her until her pregnancy became apparent or she reached the second period of purity. He might have no desire for her and be reluctant to have intercourse with her lest she conceive by him. How, then, could he be obliged to have intercourse with her? For this reason, none of the four imams or comparable leading Muslim scholars held intercourse to be obligatory. Rather, the Prophet deferred the divorce until the second period of purity. Had he not initially divorced her, he would have been permitted to divorce her during the first period of purity; for, had he been permitted to divorce her during

---

## Page 15983

the first period of purity, retaining her would serve none of the purposes of marriage if he retained her only to divorce her. If he intended to divorce her during that first period of purity, this would merely cause them both further harm, and the Lawgiver does not command such a thing. Thus, if he is prevented from divorcing her during the first period of purity so that he has the opportunity for intercourse that is not followed by divorce, then, if he has not had intercourse with her, or has had intercourse with her, or she has menstruated thereafter, he may divorce her. Furthermore, if he abstains from intercourse with her during that period of purity and then divorces her during the second, this indicates that he needs to divorce her because he has no desire for her. Had he desired her, he would have had intercourse with her during the first period of purity.

They further argued that he did not instruct Ibn ‘Umar to have witnesses present when taking her back, as Allah and His Messenger commanded. Had the divorce taken effect and he been taking her back, he would have been instructed to have witnesses present. Moreover, although Allah Most High mentions divorce in several verses, He does not command anyone to take his wife back immediately after divorcing her. Rather, He says:

“When they have nearly reached the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably.”

Thus, when the waiting period approaches its end, He gives the husband the choice between retaining her honourably—which means taking her back—and releasing her, leaving her free to go when the waiting period ends. He must not keep her confined after the waiting period has ended as she was confined on his account during it. Allah Most High says:

“Do not turn them out of their homes, nor should they leave, unless they commit a clear indecency.”

Furthermore, if the prohibited divorce had taken legal effect, the harm that Allah and His Messenger abhor would already have occurred. That harm is not removed by taking her back when he is permitted to divorce her

---

## Page 15984

after taking her back. To command a reconciliation that serves no purpose is something Allah and His Messenger are far above. If he desires the woman, he may take her back; but if he has no desire for her, he has no right to take her back. Thus, commanding him to take her back while holding the divorce binding serves no purpose recognised by the Sharia; rather, it causes further harm. The Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—must be held above commanding anything that necessarily causes further harm. Allah and His Messenger prohibited divorce contrary to the Sunnah precisely to prevent harm. How, then, could he command something that necessarily increases it?

The second group’s position accords more closely with the foundational principles and scriptural texts, for the position just discussed is self-contradictory. The principle upheld by the early generations and the jurists is that prohibited acts of worship and contracts, when performed in the prohibited manner, are not valid and binding. Although a group of speculative theologians disputed this, the correct position is that of the early generations and the leading jurists. For the Companions and those who followed them faithfully would infer the invalidity of acts of worship and punishments from the Lawgiver’s prohibition of them. This is abundantly attested in reports from them.

Furthermore, if prohibition did not indicate their invalidity, there would be nothing from the Lawgiver to distinguish the valid from the invalid. Those who said, “Prohibition does not entail invalidity,” said: “We determine the validity or invalidity of acts of worship and contracts by the Lawgiver’s designation of something as a condition, an impediment, or the like, and by His saying

---

## Page 15985

‘This is valid’ or ‘This is not valid,’ as declarations of legal status and statements of fact.” Yet it is well known that such expressions do not occur in the words of Allah and His Messenger: expressions such as “Purification is a condition of prayer,” “Disbelief is an impediment to the validity of prayer,” and “This contract and this act of worship are invalid,” and the like.

Rather, their words contain commands and prohibitions, declarations of permissibility and prohibition, and statements denying acceptance or propriety, such as:

“Allah does not accept prayer without purification, nor charity from misappropriated property.”

And:

“This is not proper.”

They also contain statements such as “Allah dislikes such-and-such,” promises, and similar expressions. Thus, on their account, we can ascertain validity and invalidity only through the expressions they have mentioned. Yet their account does not require that the Lawgiver should have explained this—an implication whose falsity is known with certainty.

Furthermore, the Lawgiver prohibits something because it involves unmitigated or predominant harm. His purpose in prohibiting it is to prevent that harm and ensure that it does not occur. If, despite its prohibition, it produced the same legal effects as something lawful, so that He made it binding and effective just like something lawful, He would thereby be imposing the very harm He intended to prevent. This would mean that He intended that harm not to occur while nevertheless binding people to it. This is a contradiction far beneath the Lawgiver—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Some of these scholars have said: “He prohibited triple divorce only so that the divorcing husband would not regret it. This indicates that regret necessarily follows if he does so, which entails that the divorce is valid.”

---

## Page 15986

He may be answered as follows:

This implies that everything Allah has forbidden would be valid—for example, being married to a woman and her paternal aunt at the same time, which is forbidden lest it lead to severing ties of kinship. It could then be said: “If this man’s argument is correct, this provides evidence that the marriage contract is valid, since, if it were invalid, no severing of kinship would occur.” This is ignorance.

The Lawgiver has explained the wisdom behind prohibiting what He has forbidden: if He permitted it, harm would necessarily follow. Thus, Allah’s words:

“You do not know; perhaps Allah will bring about a new situation after that,”

and the Prophet’s words, peace be upon him:

“A woman must not be married along with her paternal or maternal aunt, for if you do so, you will sever your ties of kinship,”

and similar statements explain that, were the act permitted, harm would result from it. It was therefore forbidden to prevent that harm. Harm arises from permitting the act and from performing it in the belief that it is permissible or valid. But when one believes it to be forbidden and invalid, and abides by the command of Allah and His Messenger, the harm does not arise. Rather, harm arises from disobeying the command of Allah and His Messenger.

Such harms involve tribulation and punishment. Allah Most High says:

“So let those who oppose his command beware lest a tribulation befall them or a painful punishment afflict them.”

As for the claim, “If the divorce were not binding, the harm would not occur,” the answer is:

That is precisely the purpose of the Lawgiver, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He therefore prohibited it and ruled it invalid so that the harm would be removed. Otherwise, people would do it, believing it valid, and harm would necessarily follow.

This resembles the argument of someone who says: “Prohibiting something indicates that it is intended, that it has legal recognition, and that it may be called a sale, a marriage, or a fast.” They make such arguments concerning his prohibition of shighār (exchange marriage), his curse upon the man who marries a divorced woman to make her lawful again for her former husband and upon the former husband for whom this is done, and his prohibition of selling fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent

---

## Page 15987

and his prohibition of fasting on either of the two Eid days, and the like.

The answer is:

There is no doubt that the act is physically conceivable. This is like his prohibition of marrying mothers and daughters, and of selling wine, carrion, pork, and idols. As reported in the two Sahīh collections from Jābir, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has forbidden the sale of wine, carrion, pigs, and idols.” He was asked: “O Messenger of Allah, what about the fat of carrion? It is used to coat ships and treat hides, and people burn it in their lamps.” He replied: “No, it is forbidden.” Then he said: “May Allah destroy the Jews! Fat was forbidden to them, so they melted it down, sold it, and consumed its proceeds.”

His calling such an act a marriage or a sale did not prevent it from being defective and invalid; it merely indicated that it was physically possible.

As for someone saying, “It is recognized by the Sharia”: if he means that it bears the name the Lawgiver gave it, that is correct. If he means that Allah has permitted it, that contradicts the revealed text and scholarly consensus. If he means that Allah has attached its legal effects to it, made it achieve its intended purpose, and made those effects binding upon people, as with a permissible act, then this is false by consensus in most of the cases involved in the dispute. Nor can he establish that claim in a case on which there is consensus. What these people chiefly invoke is the Prophet’s prohibition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, of divorce during menstruation and similar matters that are themselves disputed. They therefore have no instance in which their contention has been established, either by a revealed text or by consensus.

Likewise, the accursed “man who makes her lawful again” was cursed because, through his marriage contract, he intended to make her lawful for her former husband—not because he actually made her lawful. For if he had married her out of a genuine desire for marriage, he would have made her lawful for her former husband by consensus; yet such a man is not accursed, also by consensus.

---

## Page 15988

It follows, then, that the curse applies to whoever intends to make her lawful for her former husband. It is also clear that the accursed man has not actually made her lawful, and that the curse indicates that his act is forbidden, whereas the opposing scholar maintains that it is permissible. Thus, they have no supporting proof; rather, the early generations and the leading jurists are correct. Any renowned scholar who departs from this principle in particular cases, if he has no sound answer, has contradicted himself, just as he has elsewhere.

The principles free of contradiction are those established by a revealed text or scholarly consensus. Contradictions occur in anything beyond that, and it constitutes no binding proof against anyone. Sound analogical reasoning, which involves no contradiction, accords with revealed texts and scholarly consensus. Indeed, the revealed text must itself have indicated the ruling, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

This is the meaning of protection from error: the statements of one protected from error do not contradict one another. Muslims do not dispute that the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is protected from error in what he conveyed from Allah Most High. Thus, by Muslim consensus, he is protected from error in what he prescribed for the Muslim community. Likewise, the community is protected from collectively agreeing upon misguidance. This protection does not extend beyond these bounds.

For this reason, the leading scholars of the religion held that anyone’s statements may be accepted or rejected except those of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah has made it obligatory upon all His creatures to believe in him, obey him, regard as lawful what he declared lawful, and regard as forbidden what he declared forbidden. Through him, Allah distinguished between the believer and the unbeliever, the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire, guidance and misguidance, and error and right conduct.

The believers—the people of Paradise, guidance, and right conduct—are his followers. The unbelievers—the people of the Fire, error, and misguidance—are those who did not follow him.

---

## Page 15989

Whoever believes in him inwardly and outwardly and strives to follow him is among the blessed believers, even if he has erred or been mistaken regarding some of what he brought because it did not reach him or he did not understand it. Allah Most High says of the believers:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.”

It is established in the Sahīh, on the authority of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Allah said: “I have done so.”

In the Sunan collections, it is reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The scholars are the heirs of the prophets. The prophets left neither dinars nor dirhams as an inheritance; rather, they left knowledge. Whoever acquires it has acquired an abundant share.”

Allah Most High also says:

“And David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which some people’s sheep had strayed by night, and We were witness to their judgment. We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave judgment and knowledge.”

Thus, He singled out one of the two noble prophets for understanding, while praising both for having been granted knowledge and judgment. Likewise, when Allah grants one of two scholars knowledge and understanding of a matter, this does not warrant censuring the scholar who has not attained it. Rather, everyone who fears Allah to the best of his ability is among Allah’s pious allies, even if some aspect of the religion that another understands remains hidden from him.

Wāthilah ibn al-Asqaʿ said—and some narrators trace this back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“Whoever seeks knowledge and attains it has two rewards, and whoever seeks knowledge but does not attain it has one reward.”

This accords with what is reported in the Sahīh from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀs and Abū Hurayrah, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When a judge strives to reach a judgment and is correct, he has two rewards; when he strives to reach a judgment and errs, he has one reward.”

These principles are discussed at length elsewhere. The purpose here is merely to draw attention to this, because an unlawful divorce is among the matters that many people maintain are legally binding. The early generations, the leading jurists, and the majority acknowledge that prohibition entails

---

## Page 15990

invalidity. Yet they offer no sound distinction to justify this exception. This has given those who dispute their principle that prohibition entails invalidity grounds to challenge them, citing the cases they themselves concede. This, however, is a dialectical argument: it does not establish the truth of the challenger’s position. It establishes only that his opponents have erred, either in the supposed counterexamples or in the issue under dispute. Their error in one of these does not entail that their error concerns the disputed issue.

Rather, this is a fundamental principle upon which many rulings of Islamic law depend. It cannot therefore be overturned by the opinions of certain scholars who have neither a scriptural text nor consensus to support them. Indeed, the established principles and scriptural texts do not support their position; they contradict it. Whoever reflects upon the Quran and Sunnah will see clearly that Allah has never prescribed unlawful divorce at all. As for irrevocable divorce, He has prescribed it before consummation and after the waiting period has expired.

Some scholars say to those who count three pronouncements of divorce made together as only one: “You have contradicted ʿUmar. The practice of treating them as binding became established during his time.” Some even regard this as a consensus. They may be answered: “You yourselves have contradicted ʿUmar concerning a position famously reported from him, on which the Companions agreed—indeed, a position supported by the Quran and Sunnah—for some of you permit a marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.”

It is authentically reported that ʿUmar said: “If a man who contracts such a marriage and the former husband for whose benefit it is contracted are brought before me, I will stone them both.”

The Companions agreed that this practice was forbidden, including ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn ʿUmar and others. No Companion is known to have restored a woman to her former husband through such an arranged marriage. ʿUmar and the rest of the Companions are supported by the Quran

---

## Page 15991

and Sunnah—as shown, for example, by the Prophet’s (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) cursing both the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband and the former husband for whose benefit this is done. Those who disagreed with the Companions on this matter did so through independent scholarly judgment. May Allah be pleased with all Muslim scholars.

Furthermore, it is authentically reported that ʿUmar held that expressions such as “You are free” and “You are released” constitute a single revocable divorce. Yet most of them disagree with ʿUmar on this.

It is also authentically reported that when a missing man returned and found that his wife had remarried, ʿUmar gave him the choice between his wife and the dowry. This ruling is also known from other Companions, including ʿUthmān and ʿAlī. Ahmad reported it from eight Companions and said: “What basis does anyone who opposes these men have?” Nevertheless, most of them disagree with ʿUmar and the other Companions on this matter; some even overturn a judge’s ruling when he rules accordingly.

ʿUmar and the Companions designated lands conquered by force—such as those of Syria, Egypt, Iraq, Khurāsān and the Maghrib—as fay’ for the Muslims. Neither ʿUmar nor ʿUthmān distributed any land conquered by force. Nor did ʿUmar obtain the consent of all those entitled to the spoils concerning these lands, although some scholars supposed that their consent had been obtained regarding the Sawād. Rather, Bilāl, al-Zubayr and others asked him to divide the lands conquered by force, but he did not grant their request. Nevertheless, some of these scholars disagree with ʿUmar and the Companions on a matter of such importance, concerning which practice had been settled since their time. Indeed, they even overturn the ruling of a judge who rules as the Companions did.

Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī never set aside a fifth of fay’ property, nor did the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). Nor did they divide the fifth of the spoils of war into five equal shares. Yet many of these scholars disagree with this. There are numerous comparable examples.

---

## Page 15992

The fundamental principle upon which Muslim scholars agree is that whatever they dispute must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger, as Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and best in outcome.”

No one may suppose that, after the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), the Companions unanimously agreed upon something contrary to his revealed law. Such a claim is, rather, characteristic of heretics. Nor may anyone claim that what the Messenger prescribed was abrogated by anyone’s consensus after him, as some mistakenly suppose. Rather, everything upon which the Muslims unanimously agree must accord with what the Messenger brought, not contradict it. Whenever the Muslim community unanimously agrees that a text has been abrogated, it possesses the text that abrogates it. The community preserves the abrogating text just as it preserves the abrogated text; indeed, preserving the abrogating text is more important to it and a greater obligation upon it than preserving the abrogated text. It is impossible that ʿUmar and the Companions with him unanimously agreed upon something contrary to an explicit statement of the Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). An individual may, however, exercise independent scholarly judgment and be challenged by others. This occurs in many matters, and this is one of them, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Thus, when ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) held that an irrevocably divorced woman was entitled to accommodation and maintenance, believing that the Quran indicated this, most of the Companions disagreed with him. Some said that she was entitled only to accommodation, while others said that she was entitled to neither maintenance nor accommodation. Among the latter were Ibn ʿAbbās, Jābir and Fātimah bint Qays, who reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) had said to her:

“You are entitled to neither maintenance nor accommodation.”

When they argued against her using ʿUmar’s evidence, namely, the words of Allah Most High, “Do not turn them out

---

## Page 15993

of their homes, nor should they leave unless they commit a clear indecency,” she and other Companions—including Ibn ʿAbbās, Jābir and others—replied that this concerns a revocably divorced woman, because the Most High says:

“You do not know; perhaps Allah will bring about something new thereafter.”

What new development could there be after three divorces? The jurists versed in hadith, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal—according to the apparent position of his school—and others, agree with Fātimah bint Qays.

Likewise, concerning divorce, when the Most High said, “Perhaps Allah will bring about something new thereafter,” a number of Companions, Successors and scholars said that this indicates that the divorce Allah mentioned is revocable divorce. For had He prescribed pronouncing all three divorces, the husband would regret doing so, with no way to take his wife back, and would thus suffer harm. Allah commands His servants to do what benefits them and forbids them from doing what harms them. Hence the Most High also said thereafter:

“When they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably.”

This applies only to revocable divorce, not to three divorces or to irrevocable divorce.

The Most High said:

“And call two just men from among you to witness, and give testimony for Allah’s sake.”

He thus commanded that taking a wife back be witnessed. The Muslim community unanimously agrees that witnessing this is commanded, though some say the command makes it obligatory, while others say it makes it recommended.

Some people have supposed that the command to have witnesses concerns divorce, and that a divorce which is not witnessed does not take effect. This contradicts scholarly consensus, the Quran and the Sunnah, and none of the well-known scholars held this view. For He initially permitted divorce, but did not command

---

## Page 15994

that it be witnessed. He commanded witnesses only when He said:

“When they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably.”

Parting here means letting her go once her waiting period has ended. This is neither divorce, nor taking her back, nor marriage. There is consensus among the Muslims concerning witnessing in this case. It is therefore clear that the witnessing applies only to taking the wife back.

One aspect of the wisdom behind this is that a man may divorce his wife and then take her back, whereupon Satan makes concealing this seem attractive to him. He may subsequently pronounce a prohibited divorce without anyone knowing, so that she remains with him unlawfully. Allah therefore commanded him to have witnesses when taking her back, to make it known that one divorce has occurred. Similarly, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed anyone who finds lost property to have witnesses to it, lest Satan make concealing it seem attractive. Divorce is different: if he divorces her and does not take her back, but lets her go, he makes it known to people that she is no longer his wife but is divorced. By contrast, if she remains with him as his wife, people do not know whether he has divorced her or not.

As for marriage, it must be distinguished from fornication and taking secret lovers, as Allah Most High commanded. Hence the Sunnah prescribes making it public; it must not be concealed like fornication. But is the requirement simply to have witnesses, or simply to make it public even without witnesses, or does either suffice? Scholars disagree on this, as discussed elsewhere.

Allah Most High said:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from where he does not expect. Whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him. Indeed, Allah will accomplish His purpose. Allah has set a measure for everything.”

This verse applies generally to everyone who fears Allah. The context of the verse indicates that fearing Allah is intended by this general statement. Thus, whoever fears Allah in matters of divorce and divorces as

---

## Page 15995

Allah Most High has commanded, then Allah will grant him a way out of difficulties that constrain others. Whoever transgresses Allah’s limits by doing what Allah has forbidden him has wronged himself. If someone was unaware that innovated forms of divorce are prohibited, not knowing that divorce during menstruation or pronouncing three divorces at once is forbidden, then, once he learns of the prohibition and repents, he becomes one of those who fear Allah and thus deserves to be granted a way out by Allah.

If someone knew that this was forbidden but committed it, believing that his wife thereby became unlawful to him, and had access only to those who ruled that she became unlawful to him, then he is punished in proportion to his wrongdoing—just as the Sabbath-breakers were punished by having the fish prevented from coming to them. Since he did not fear Allah, he was punished with hardship.

But if Allah guides him, makes the truth known to him and inspires him to repent, and he repents, then one who repents of a sin is like one who has no sin. He has then joined those who fear Allah and thus deserves to be granted relief and a way out by Allah. Our Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the Prophet of mercy and the Prophet of battle. Everyone who repents therefore has a means of relief under his law, unlike the law prescribed for those before us, under which those who repented were subjected to punishments such as killing themselves and other such penalties.

For this reason, when Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about someone who had divorced his wife three times, he would say to him: “Had you feared Allah, He would have granted you a way out.” At times, he agreed with ‘Umar in treating the three divorces as binding upon those who repeatedly committed this forbidden innovation despite knowing that it was prohibited. It is also reported that, at other times, he treated only one divorce as binding.

Ibn Mas‘ūd would become angry with those who practised this innovation and say: “People! Whoever approaches the matter in the proper way will find it clear. Otherwise, by Allah, we cannot cope with everything you introduce.”

---

## Page 15996

During the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī, there was no openly practised “marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband” known to the witnesses, the woman and her guardians. Nor has anyone reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or his Rightly Guided Caliphs restored a woman to her former husband through such a marriage. For divorces at that time generally followed the Sunnah, and people did not swear oaths invoking divorce. Thus, no specific report has been transmitted from the Companions concerning such oaths; what has been transmitted from them concerns actually pronouncing divorce, not swearing by it.

The distinction between divorce and swearing by divorce is clear, just as there is a recognized distinction between making a vow and using a vow as an oath. If a man seeks something from Allah and says, “If Allah cures my illness, settles my debt or delivers me from this hardship, I pledge to Allah that I will give a thousand dirhams in charity, fast for a month or free a slave,” this is a conditional vow which he must fulfil, according to the Quran, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus.

But if he makes a vow conditional in the manner of an oath, saying, “If I travel with you”; “If I arrange so-and-so’s marriage”; “That I should strike so-and-so”; or “If I do not leave you”—followed by “then I must perform Hajj,” “then my wealth is to be given in charity,” or “then I must free a slave”—then, according to the Companions and the majority of scholars, he is swearing an oath by a vow, not making a vow. If he does not fulfil the obligation he has undertaken, expiation for an oath suffices him.

Likewise, the Companions ruled that if someone said, “If I do such-and-such, every slave I own is free,” this constituted an oath, for which expiation for an oath sufficed. Many of the Successors likewise held this view concerning all these matters when al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf introduced the practice of making people swear oaths of allegiance—oaths invoking divorce and the freeing of slaves, oaths sworn in Allah’s name, and pledges to give one’s wealth in charity. It was also said that these included pledges to perform Hajj—

---

## Page 15997

the Successors and those who came after them then discussed these oaths, addressing some of them along these lines. Some said that whoever broke such an oath was bound by whatever he had undertaken. Others said that only divorce and the freeing of slaves became binding. Others said, rather, that these were oaths of the kind sworn by polytheists, and that nothing became binding through them. Still others said that these were Muslim oaths, subject to the same obligations as all other Muslim oaths. This last group followed what had been transmitted from the Companions concerning this kind of oath and what the Quran and Sunnah indicated, as explained at length elsewhere.

The point here is that, during the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Rightly Guided Caliphs, no woman was returned to her former husband through a marriage arranged to make her lawful for him again. Such arrangements were made only in secret. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has cursed the recipient of usury, its payer, its two witnesses and its scribe; and He has cursed the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband, and the former husband for whom this is done.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.”

In the case of usury, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the recipient, the payer, the two witnesses and the scribe, because it involves a debt that is recorded and witnessed. In the case of a marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband, he cursed the man who married her for this purpose and the former husband for whom it was done. He did not curse the two witnesses or the scribe, because in his time dowers were not recorded in writing. The general practice was to provide the dower before consummation, leaving not a single dinar outstanding as a debt owed by the husband, so there was no need for a written document and witnesses. The man who married her for this purpose and the former husband for whom it was done kept their arrangement secret. The woman, her guardians and the witnesses knew nothing of it.

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband, and the former husband for whom this is done,”

because they—not the others—were the ones who committed the forbidden act. Generally, people had no need for such arrangements, since a man would reach three divorces only by divorcing again after taking his wife back or entering into a new marriage contract. Thus, only rarely would anyone regret it after three divorces; and this would

---

## Page 15998

occur after he had disobeyed Allah and transgressed His limits, thereby deserving punishment. Whoever seeks to make the woman lawful for him again is therefore cursed; these men too are cursed, because the two have cooperated in sin and transgression.

Then the practice of swearing oaths by divorce arose. Many jurists believed that whoever broke such an oath was bound by what he had undertaken, and that expiation for an oath would not suffice. Many also believed that a prohibited divorce was binding; that pronouncing three divorces together was not prohibited; that a divorce pronounced while intoxicated took effect; and that a divorce pronounced under coercion took effect. Some of these opinions were disputed among the Companions, while others emerged after their time. Consequently, the belief that divorce had taken effect became widespread, despite the immense harm and damage to religious and worldly life resulting from a man’s separation from his wife. Those who held divorce to be binding in these disputed situations thus divided into two groups.

One group followed what had been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Companions concerning the prohibition of marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband. They therefore prohibited this, while also prohibiting those aforementioned cases which the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had not prohibited. Their position thus imposed fetters, burdens and immense hardship, leading to grave harm in religious and worldly life. Among its consequences were some people’s apostasy from Islam after being given a ruling that what they had undertaken was binding upon them; the shedding of inviolable blood; loss of reason; enmity between people; disparagement of Islamic law; numerous sins; and other grave consequences.

The other group sought to remove this immense hardship through various legal stratagems by which the woman could return to her husband.

---

## Page 15999

Among the earliest innovations was “marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.” Some scholars held that whoever did this would be rewarded, because they believed that returning the woman to her husband removed those harmful consequences. This served as a stratagem for undoing the effects of divorce in all these situations. Other stratagems were then introduced concerning “oaths.” First came manipulation of the wording of an oath; then the use of khulʿ (marital dissolution for compensation) to evade an oath; then circularly conditional divorce; and finally attempts to have the marriage declared invalid.

The majority of the early generations, the scholars and their leading authorities condemned these and similar stratagems. They held that such practices nullified the wisdom of the Sharia and the true substance of oaths enshrined in Allah’s verses, and regarded them as a form of deceit and mockery of Allah’s verses. Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī even said of such people:

“They try to deceive Allah as though they were deceiving children. Had they committed the act openly, I would have found it less objectionable.”

The disbelievers and hypocrites then exploited these practices to disparage the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They made them among their strongest arguments against those who believed in him, supported him and honoured him; among their strongest means of turning people away from Allah’s path and obstructing those who wished to believe in him; and among their strongest reasons for refusing to believe themselves. Those among them who subsequently embraced the faith have related this about themselves. They explained that the merits of Islam had become clear to them, except for practices of the kind represented by “marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband,” for which they could find no explanation that satisfied their misgivings.

Allah Most High says:

“And My mercy encompasses all things, so I shall decree it for those who fear Me, pay zakat and believe in Our signs.”

“Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find described in the Torah and the Gospel they possess. He commands them to do what is right and forbids them from doing what is wrong, makes good things lawful for them and forbids them impure things

---

## Page 16000

and relieves them of their burdens and the shackles that bound them. So those who believe in him, honour him, support him and follow the light sent down with him—it is they who will prosper.”

Thus, He describes His Messenger as commanding everything right, forbidding everything wrong, making all good things lawful, prohibiting all impure things, and removing the burdens and shackles imposed upon those before him. Every less well-founded opinion that contradicts the Book and wisdom he brought is an innovated opinion. At best, it belongs to the abrogated law that Allah superseded with the law of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—if its proponent is among the finest and most distinguished members of this community, has exercised independent scholarly judgment in reaching that opinion, and has feared Allah to the best of his ability. Such a person is rewarded for his scholarly endeavour and piety, and forgiven his error. The Messenger cannot therefore be held accountable for an opinion someone else reached through independent scholarly judgment.

It is authentically reported from him in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“When a judge exercises independent judgment and reaches the correct decision, he receives two rewards. When he exercises independent judgment and errs, he receives one reward.”

It is also authentically reported in the Sahīh that he would say to someone whom he appointed to command a military detachment or army:

“When you besiege the people of a fortress and they ask you to let them surrender subject to Allah’s judgment, do not let them surrender on that basis, for you do not know what Allah’s judgment concerning them is. Rather, let them surrender subject to your judgment and that of your companions.”

This accords with the authentic report in the Sahīh:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed Sa'd ibn Mu'ādh to judge the case of Banū Qurayzah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had besieged them, and they had surrendered subject to his judgment. He then made their surrender subject to the judgment of Sa'd ibn Mu'ādh, after allies of theirs among the Ansār asked him to treat them kindly. Contrary to what some of his people expected of him, Sa'd ibn Mu'ādh gave precedence to the pleasure of Allah and His Messenger over the approval of

---

## Page 16001

his people; that is why, when he died, the Throne of the Most Merciful shook with joy at the arrival of his soul. He ruled that their fighting men should be killed, their women and children taken captive, and their property divided. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘You have indeed judged them according to the judgment of the King.’” Another version reads:

“You have indeed judged them according to Allah’s judgment from above seven heavens.”

The scholars are the heirs of the prophets. Allah Most High says:

“And remember David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which some people’s sheep had strayed by night. We witnessed their judgment. We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave sound judgment and knowledge.”

These were two noble prophets judging a single case. Allah singled out one of them for understanding it, while praising both for having received sound judgment and knowledge. The same applies to scholars who exercise independent judgment—may Allah be pleased with them. Whoever reaches the correct conclusion receives two rewards, while the other receives one. Each obeys Allah to the best of his ability, and Allah does not require of him what he is unable to know. Nevertheless, the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cannot be held responsible for another person’s opinion, nor can the law he brought be held responsible for newly introduced opinions, especially abhorrent ones. Thus, when the Companions spoke on the basis of their independent judgment, they dissociated the Messenger’s law from their own errors and those of others.

For example, concerning a woman whose dowry had not been stipulated, ‘Abdullāh ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“I give my own opinion concerning her. If it is correct, it is from Allah; if it is mistaken, it is from me and Satan. Allah and His Messenger are free of it.”
