The same was reported from al-Siddīq concerning kalālah inheritance, and from ‘Umar concerning certain matters. Yet they would reach correct conclusions when speaking in this manner, and a revealed text would subsequently be found to agree with their independent judgment, just as the revealed text agreed with the independent judgment of Ibn Mas‘ūd and others.

---

## Page 16002

Rather, they knew Allah and His Messenger too well, and understood too well the reverence owed to the Messenger’s law, to attribute to him anything except what they knew to have come from him. Concerning any errors they made—even while exercising independent judgment—they said:

“Allah and His Messenger are free of it.”

Allah Most High says:

“The Messenger’s duty is only to convey the message clearly.”

And He says:

“He is responsible only for what he has been charged with, and you are responsible for what you have been charged with.”

And He says:

“We will surely question those to whom messengers were sent, and We will surely question the messengers.”

Thus, you find that the Muslim community has differed over certain questions, holding several opinions, although only one of them represents what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent with. As for the other opinions, if those who hold them are qualified to exercise independent judgment and are people of knowledge and religious integrity, then they are obedient to Allah and His Messenger, rewarded rather than sinful. This is like people travelling who cannot determine the direction of the qiblah: each group exercises its judgment and prays towards one of the four directions. The Kaaba lies in only one of those directions, yet the others are rewarded for their prayers because they have feared Allah as much as they could.

One of the signs of the message brought by the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is that when it is clearly presented alongside other views, its light and guidance become apparent, and the other opinion is recognized as inferior to it. For the best speech is Allah’s speech, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, says:

“Say: If mankind and the jinn were to come together to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce its like, even if they supported one another.”

And this challenge

---

## Page 16003

and the demonstration that it cannot be matched extend to its wording, composition, and meaning, as explained elsewhere.

Examples include the questions of divorce over which Muslims have differed. You find “three” positions on these: one involves burdens and shackles; another involves deception and stratagems; and a third involves knowledge and moderation. One entails a kind of injustice and inconsistency; another entails a kind of injustice, indecency, and disgrace; and a third follows the path of the Emigrants and the Helpers.

Likewise, on questions of oaths involving vows, divorce, and manumission, you find them holding three positions. One nullifies Muslims’ oaths and treats them like the oaths of polytheists. Another holds that binding oaths admit neither expiation nor release, as under the religious law of those outside the Muslim community. A third upholds the sanctity of the oaths of those who affirm Allah’s oneness and believe in Him, distinguishing them from the oaths of polytheists and idolaters. It provides for the expiation and release prescribed by the revealed texts—a provision granted specifically to the people of the Quran, not to the people of the Torah and the Gospel.

This is the law brought by the Seal of the Messengers, the leader of the God-fearing, and the best of all creation. May Allah bless him, his family, his good and pure Companions, and those who follow them in righteousness until the Day of Judgment.

---

## Page 16004

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

A Concise, Comprehensive Account of Questions Concerning “Oaths and Divorce” and Their Points of Agreement and Difference

A question may concern oaths but not divorce, divorce but not oaths, or both. Statements relating to divorce fall into three categories, and oaths likewise fall into three categories.

Statements relating to divorce take one of three forms: an immediate pronouncement, a conditional pronouncement, or an oath.

The “immediate pronouncement” effects divorce outright, without attaching any condition or oath to it. Examples include saying: “You are divorced,” or “You have been divorced”; “So-and-so is divorced”; “You are divorce itself”; or “I have divorced you.” Similar expressions may take the form of a verb, a verbal noun, an active participle, or a passive participle. This is called an immediate divorce, an unrestricted divorce, or an unconditional divorce—that is, one not made dependent on a condition. This effects a divorce; it is not

---

## Page 16005

an oath that one may choose to break or keep; nor is any expiation due for this, by the agreement of the Muslims. In their established terminology, jurists do not call this an oath or an act of swearing. Some people, however, say, “I swore an oath invoking divorce,” when they mean that they pronounced a divorce.

As for the “oath formula,” this is when a person says: “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do such-and-such,” or, “I will not do such-and-such.” He thus invokes divorce in an oath to urge himself or someone else to act, to prevent himself or someone else from acting, or to affirm or deny the truth of a report.

This falls under questions concerning both divorce and oaths. It is an oath by the agreement of scholars of the Arabic language, since it takes the form of an oath. It is also an oath in the terminology of the jurists: they have not disputed its designation as an oath, but they have disputed its ruling. Some jurists gave precedence to its character as a divorce pronouncement and therefore held that divorce takes effect if he breaks the oath. Others gave precedence to its character as an oath and therefore did not hold that divorce takes effect. Rather, they said that he must offer expiation for breaking an oath, or that nothing is required of him at all.

They likewise differed concerning someone who swears an oath by making a vow, saying: “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj, fast for a month, or give my wealth in charity.” However, statements concerning this kind of oath are widely reported from the early generations, including the Companions and others. They said that these are oaths for which the expiation prescribed for breaking an oath suffices, since such oaths occurred frequently in the time of the Companions. By contrast, discussion of oaths invoking divorce is known only from the Successors and those after them, who differed over it, holding the two positions mentioned above.

---

## Page 16006

The third is a “conditional formula,” such as saying: “If you enter the house, you are divorced.” This is called divorce contingent upon a condition. The speaker may intend to swear an oath, while disliking the prospect of divorce taking effect if the condition is fulfilled; alternatively, he may intend divorce to take effect when the condition is fulfilled.

The first case has the same ruling as an oath invoking divorce, by agreement of the jurists. If someone says, “If I swear an oath, I must free a slave,” and then swears an oath invoking divorce, he has broken his oath, with no disagreement known to us among the recognized scholars. The same applies to all other conditional statements intended as oaths, such as: “If I do such-and-such, I must free a slave,” “then my slaves are free,” “then I must perform Hajj,” “then I must fast for a month,” or “then my wealth is to be given in charity or as a sacrificial offering,” and similar statements. These are equivalent to saying: “Freeing a slave shall be binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such,” or “Hajj shall be binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such,” and similar statements. However, what comes last in the conditional formula comes first in the oath formula, and what is negated in one formula is affirmed in the other.

The second case is where the speaker intends divorce to take effect upon fulfillment of the condition. According to the generality of early and later scholars, divorce takes effect when that condition is fulfilled, just as an immediate pronouncement of divorce takes effect. The same applies if he specifies a time for divorce, saying, for example: “You are divorced at the beginning of the month.” More than one scholar has reported consensus that such a conditional divorce takes effect, and no early disagreement concerning it was known. Ibn Hazm, however, claimed that divorce does not take effect through it, which is also the position of the Imāmī Shia—even though Ibn Hazm himself reported in his Book of Consensus that the scholars agreed that divorce does take effect through it.

---

## Page 16007

and he stated that the disagreement concerns only cases in which the pronouncement is intended as an oath: does divorce take effect, does it not take effect with nothing incumbent upon him, or is it an oath whose breach requires expiation? There are three views, just as there are these same three views concerning comparable oaths.

As for this form—divorce made contingent upon a condition, with the intention that divorce take effect when it occurs, and without any intent to urge or deter, as when someone says, “If the sun rises, you are divorced”—does it constitute an oath? There are two views:

The first is that it is an oath. This is the view of Abū Hanīfa and one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school.

The second is that it is not an oath. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and the other position within Ahmad’s school. This view is sounder in terms of Islamic law and language. Customary usage, however, varies.

Section

As for the three types of oaths, the first is to swear an oath by Allah; the second is to make an oath to Allah; and the third is to swear an oath by other than Allah or make one to other than Allah.

The first consists of swearing by Allah. This constitutes a binding oath whose breach requires expiation, as established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus.

As for the third, it consists of swearing an oath by something created or making one to something created—for example, swearing by false deities, one’s father, the Kaaba, or any other created thing. Such an oath is not

---

## Page 16008

recognized by Islamic law; it is not binding, and breaking it requires no expiation, by scholarly consensus. However, the very act of swearing such an oath is prohibited. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath, saying in his oath, ‘By al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā,’ should say, ‘There is no god but Allah.’”

Oaths sworn by angels, prophets, or others are all alike in this respect, by scholarly consensus. However, concerning swearing by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there are two views within Ahmad’s school. The majority hold that such an oath is not binding and requires no expiation.

As for making an oath to other than Allah, examples include making vows to idols or churches, or swearing an oath involving such a commitment, saying: “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to give such-and-such to the church, or such-and-such to so-and-so’s grave,” and the like.

If this is a vow, it is shirk (associating partners with Allah). If it is an oath, it is shirk when he says it out of reverence, just as a Muslim says: “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to make a sacrificial offering.” But if he says it out of aversion to that, just as a Muslim says, “If I do such-and-such, then I am a Jew or a Christian,” he is not thereby a polytheist. Scholars have two well-known views on whether expiation is incumbent upon him.

Anyone who makes a vow or oath involving shirk must repent to Allah for having made it. Such a commitment is neither to be fulfilled nor expiated; those obligations apply only to commitments made to Allah or oaths sworn by Allah.

As for commitments made to Allah, these take two forms. The first is when a person intends to draw near to Allah, not merely to urge or deter. This is a vow, for it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

---

## Page 16009

“The expiation for a vow is the same as the expiation for an oath.”

It is also authentically reported that he said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah must obey Him, and whoever vows to disobey Allah must not disobey Him.”

Thus, if a person intends to vow an act of obedience to Allah, he must fulfil it. If he vows something that is not an act of obedience, he is not obliged to fulfil it. If it is forbidden, he may not fulfil it. However, if he does not fulfil a vow made to Allah, he must offer the expiation for an oath according to most of the early scholars. This is also the view of Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah. According to one view, this applies without qualification; according to another, it applies only when the vow serves as an oath.

The second form is when his intention is to urge or deter, or to affirm or deny something. This constitutes swearing an oath involving a vow, divorce, manumission, zihār (declaring one’s wife as forbidden to oneself as one’s mother), or a declaration of prohibition. Examples include saying: “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to perform Hajj and fast for a year; my property is to be given in charity, my slaves are free, and my wives are divorced.” This category falls under the rulings on oaths, as well as those on divorce, manumission, vows, and zihār. Scholars hold three views concerning it.

The first is that, if he breaks his oath, he is bound by whatever he stipulated, because he committed himself to the consequence upon fulfilment of the condition. Since the condition has been fulfilled, that consequence becomes binding, just as with a conditional vow intended as an act of devotion.

The second view is that this is not a legally binding oath, so breaking it entails nothing: neither expiation nor the stipulated consequence taking effect. This is because it constitutes swearing by other than Allah, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath must swear by Allah or remain silent.”

In another authentic narration:

“Do not swear except by Allah.”

---

## Page 16010

The third view is that these are oaths for which expiation is due if they are broken, just as with other oaths. Some scholars distinguish between an obligation one undertakes for Allah’s sake—an oath involving a vow—and a prohibition one undertakes for Allah’s sake—an oath involving divorce or manumission. Regarding the first, they say that he must offer the expiation for an oath if he breaks it. Regarding the second, they say that, if he breaks his oath, the consequence he made conditional—and invoked in his oath—takes effect. This is because, in the first case, what he has undertaken is an obligatory act, and he is not discharged from it until he performs it; he can therefore offer expiation before doing so. In the second case, what he has undertaken is that a prohibition should take effect. This occurs upon fulfilment of the condition and cannot be lifted by expiation.

The “third view” is the one supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning. The statements of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also support it in general, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

For Allah says in His Book:

“But He will hold you accountable for the oaths you have solemnly sworn. The expiation for this is to feed ten needy people,”

through to His words:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn them.”

And the Most High says:

“Allah has prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

It is also authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should do what is better and offer expiation for his oath.”

Both the wording and the meaning of these texts encompass all the oaths of Muslims. As for the wording, Allah says:

“Allah has prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths,”

and:

“That is the expiation for your oaths.”

These words address the believers, so every oath of theirs falls within their scope. Swearing by created beings, however, is an act of associating partners with Allah and is not among their oaths, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears by other than Allah has associated partners with Him.”

This was narrated by the compilers of the Sunan: Abū Dāwūd

---

## Page 16011

and others. Such oaths therefore do not fall among the oaths of Muslims. As for an oath sworn by Allah or based on an undertaking made to Allah, it is among the oaths of Muslims and thus falls within the scope of these texts.

Hence, if someone says, “The oaths of Muslims—or the oaths of allegiance—are binding upon me,” intending to include divorce and manumission, these are included, as the jurists have stated. I know of no disagreement concerning this. Swearing by the Kaaba or any other created being, however, is not included. If these are among the oaths of Muslims, they are encompassed by the address.

As for the meaning of these texts, Allah prescribed expiation for the oaths of Muslims so that an oath would not make something obligatory or forbidden for them with no way out. Such was their situation in the early days of Islam, before expiation was prescribed: the person who swore an oath had no way out except to fulfil it. If there were oaths for which no expiation was available, this harm would remain.

Furthermore, Allah the Most High says:

“And do not make Allah, through your oaths, an obstacle to doing good, being mindful of Him, and making peace between people.”

Allah forbade them to let swearing by Him prevent them from doing what He had commanded, lest the oaths they had sworn keep them from obeying Him. If there were binding oaths for which no expiation was available, swearing such an oath would prevent them from obeying Allah.

The Most High also says:

“For those who swear to abstain from their wives there is a waiting period of four months. If they return, then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“And if they resolve to divorce, then Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

Īlā’ means swearing and taking an oath. Here it means that a man swears not to have intercourse with his wife. If he swears an oath by Allah, he has made an oath of abstention; and if he swears an oath based on an undertaking made to Allah

---

## Page 16012

—such as an oath involving a vow, zihār (declaring one’s wife unlawful by likening her to an unmarriageable female relative), divorce, or manumission—he has made an oath of abstention according to the overwhelming majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī in his later position, and Ahmad.

Some scholars, such as Ibn al-Mundhir and others, mention no disagreement on this issue. Ibn ‘Abbās is reported to have said: “Any oath that prevents intercourse is an oath of abstention.” Allah, Glorified and Exalted, has given the man who swears such an oath two choices: either to return to conjugal relations or to divorce. Returning here means intercourse. He is given the choice between retaining his wife honourably and releasing her graciously. If he returns and has intercourse with her, her purpose is fulfilled, and he has retained her honourably. The Most High says:

“If they return, then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

His forgiveness and mercy towards the man who has sworn abstinence entail absolving him of sin and preserving his marriage. The obligation of expiation, however, is not waived, as in His words:

“O Prophet, why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has already prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

He thus explains that He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful through His prescription of a release from oaths: He has shown mercy to His servants through the expiation He has prescribed for them, and through it He has forgiven them for breaking the oaths they bound themselves to observe. For a binding undertaking would require fulfilment were it not for the release He has prescribed, by which the bond of the oath is dissolved. But if the man who has sworn abstinence does not return to conjugal relations and has instead resolved to divorce, then Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

The ruling concerning the man who has sworn abstinence in the Book of Allah is therefore that he must either return to conjugal relations or resolve to divorce. If he returns, then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful, and no divorce takes effect thereby. This is agreed upon in the case of an oath sworn by Allah the Most High.

As for “an oath invoking divorce,” whoever holds that divorce takes effect through it and that no expiation is made maintains that, if the man whose oath of abstention invokes divorce returns to conjugal relations, divorce takes effect; and if he resolves to divorce and pronounces it, he incurs

---

## Page 16013

divorce. Thus, according to this view, divorce is inevitable, whether he retains her honourably or releases her graciously.

The Quran, however, indicates that the man who has sworn abstinence has a choice: either to return to conjugal relations or to divorce. If he returns, divorce is not binding upon him; rather, he must offer expiation for breaking his oath, according to the view that an oath invoking divorce entails expiation.

When a man who has sworn abstinence by Allah returns to conjugal relations, he must offer expiation for breaking his oath according to the majority of scholars. There is an anomalous view that he owes nothing whatsoever, but the majority’s view is more correct. Allah has explained expiation for oaths in His Book, in Sūrat al-Mā’idah, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that something else is better should do what is better and offer expiation for his oath.”

If it is said, “When a man who has sworn abstinence by invoking divorce returns to conjugal relations, Allah forgives him for his earlier delay in having intercourse with his wife and shows him mercy thereby, even if divorce takes effect,” the answer is: This is not valid. For one of the two views among the scholars who uphold this principle is that a man who swears, on pain of a threefold divorce, not to have intercourse with his wife may not have intercourse with her under any circumstances. Once he penetrates her, he breaks his oath, and his withdrawal would then be from a woman who is no longer his wife. This is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad and one of the two views within Mālik’s school.

The second view is that he may have intercourse with her once, withdrawing immediately thereafter, and that this act makes his wife unlawful to him. Yet it is well known that the ruling on an oath of abstention was instituted to uphold the woman’s right to intercourse. A woman would not choose an act of intercourse immediately followed by a threefold divorce unless she disliked her husband. Her purpose is therefore not fulfilled by such a return to conjugal relations.

Furthermore, on this assumption, there is no benefit in

---

## Page 16014

granting this respite; rather, she would prefer an immediate divorce so that she could complete her waiting period and become free to marry someone else. If divorce is inevitable under either alternative, the respite would be purely harmful to her. This runs contrary to the purpose of the ruling on oaths of abstention, which was instituted for the woman’s benefit, not to harm her.

The Companions and other scholars cited the scriptural texts I have mentioned as evidence concerning this class of oaths. Thus, when someone swore, saying, “If I do such-and-such, my wealth shall be given as sacrificial offerings and my slaves shall be free,” or something similar, they ruled that he should offer expiation for his oath. They therefore treated this as an oath for which expiation could be offered. Likewise, a number of earlier and later scholars regarded this ruling as encompassing oaths invoking divorce, emancipation, and other such commitments. They held that every oath a person swears is subject to the prescribed expiation for an oath, however solemn it may be.

Some scholars have supposed that this kind of undertaking bears some resemblance to vows, divorce, and emancipation, and some resemblance to oaths. That is not so. These are simply oaths, not vows, divorces, or emancipations. Some jurists call them “vows of obstinacy and anger,” but this is a qualified designation; it does not mean that they fall under the unqualified term “vow.”

The leading jurists who followed the Companions explained that these are simply oaths, as al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others established concerning oaths invoking vows. They are, however, oaths in which a breach is made conditional upon two things: first, performing the act to which the oath relates; and second, failing to carry out the undertaking invoked in the oath.

---

## Page 16015

Thus, saying, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj this year,” is equivalent to saying, “By Allah, if I do such-and-such, I shall certainly perform Hajj this year.” Were he to say this, expiation would only become due if he did that act and failed to perform Hajj that year. Likewise, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj this year,” expiation only becomes due if he does that act and fails to perform Hajj that year.

The same applies if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I must free my slave or divorce my wife.” Expiation only becomes due if he does that act without divorcing his wife or freeing his slave. The same applies even if he says, “By Allah, if I do such-and-such, then, by Allah, I shall certainly divorce my wife and free my slave.”

Likewise, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, my wife is divorced and my slave is free,” this is equivalent to saying, “By Allah, if I do such-and-such, divorce and emancipation shall certainly take effect in my case, and I shall certainly bring about that divorce and emancipation.” If he does that act, expiation only becomes due if the divorce and emancipation do not take effect. If he does not bring them about, they do not occur, because the condition for breaching the oath has not been fulfilled: the breach depends upon two conditions.

What is made conditional may be either an obligation or an occurrence. Thus, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I must fast for a month,” what is made conditional is the obligation to fast. If he says, “Then my slave is free and my wife is divorced,” what is made conditional is the occurrence of emancipation and divorce.

As explained earlier, if the person making the conditional pronouncement intends its consequence to occur when the condition is fulfilled, it does occur. For example, he may intend to divorce his wife if she releases him from his obligation to pay her dowry, saying, “If you release me from paying your dowry, you are divorced.” Here, the divorce takes effect when the stipulated condition is fulfilled.

But if his intention is to swear an oath, while he is averse to the consequence occurring when the condition is fulfilled, then he is taking an oath, just as if he said, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I shall certainly do such-and-such.”

---

## Page 16016

As for the claim, “He has committed himself to divorce upon fulfilment of the condition, so it is binding upon him,” this is invalid for several reasons.

First, someone who swears an oath invoking unbelief or Islam—for example, by saying, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Jew or a Christian,” or, in the case of a non-Muslim under Muslim protection, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Muslim”—has committed himself to unbelief or Islam upon fulfilment of the condition. Yet, by consensus, neither consequence follows, because he did not intend it to occur when the condition was fulfilled; rather, he intended to swear an oath by it. This same consideration applies to all other kinds of oaths expressed in conditional form.

Second, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I must divorce my wife,” then, by consensus, he is not obliged to divorce her if he does that act.

Third, when someone commits himself to something upon fulfilment of a condition, it becomes binding upon him only if two requirements are met: first, what he undertakes must be an act of devotion; and second, his intention must be to draw closer to Allah through it, not to swear an oath by it. Thus, if he undertakes something that is not an act of devotion, such as divorcing, selling, leasing, eating or drinking, it does not become binding upon him.

Likewise, if he undertakes an act of devotion, such as prayer, fasting or Hajj, by way of swearing an oath, it does not become binding upon him. Rather, expiation for an oath suffices, according to the Companions and the majority of the early Muslims. This is the position of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, the later of the two opinions reported from Abū Hanīfa, and the view of the discerning scholars among Mālik’s followers.

---

## Page 16017

Here, the person who swears an oath invoking divorce has committed himself to its taking effect by way of an oath, while disliking its occurrence if the condition is fulfilled—just as he dislikes falling into unbelief when he invokes it in an oath, and dislikes those acts of worship becoming obligatory upon him when he invokes them in an oath.

As for the objection, “He is swearing by other than Allah, so no expiation is binding upon him,” the reply is: The scriptural text concerns those who swear by created beings. This is why it describes such swearing as shirk (associating partners with Allah): the person has sworn by other than Allah. But someone who makes a sworn commitment to Allah binds himself more emphatically than someone who swears by Allah. This is why a vow is more emphatic than an oath. Thus, expiation is all the more obligatory for a commitment made to Allah than for an oath sworn by Allah. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about the difference between divorce and swearing an oath, and to clarify the ruling on this.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah, and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him, his family and his Companions, and grant them peace.

The expressions people use concerning divorce, manumission, vows, zihār, and declaring something forbidden to oneself fall into three categories.

---

## Page 16018

The first category

An expression that takes immediate effect, such as saying, “My wife is divorced,” “You are divorced,” “So-and-so is divorced,” “She is divorced,” or something similar. Such words bring about divorce, and expiation is of no avail in this case, by the consensus of the Muslims. Anyone who says that expiation suffices in this case is called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is put to death.

Likewise, if someone says, “My slave is free,” “I am bound to fast for a month,” “I am bound to free a slave,” “What is lawful is forbidden to me,” or “You are to me like my mother’s back,” all these declarations bring the respective legal acts into effect through immediate, unconditional expressions.

The second category

This is to invoke such matters in an oath, saying, “Divorce shall be binding upon me—I will certainly do such-and-such,” or “I will not do such-and-such.” Or he swears that someone else—such as his slave or a friend whom he expects to honour his oath—will certainly do such-and-such, or will not do it. Or he says, “What is lawful is forbidden to me—I will certainly do such-and-such,” or “I will not do it.” Or he says, “I am bound to perform Hajj—I will certainly do such-and-such,” or “I will not do it,” and similar expressions.

These are forms of oath: he is invoking these matters in an oath, not bringing them into effect. Scholars hold three views concerning such oaths:

The first is that, if he breaks his oath, whatever he invoked in it becomes binding upon him.

The second is that nothing becomes binding upon him.

The third is that he must offer expiation for an oath. Some scholars draw distinctions between oath-taking, divorce, manumission, and other such matters. The third view is the strongest, because Allah Most High says:

“Allah has prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

And He says:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

It is authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, in hadiths narrated by Abū Hurayrah, ‘Adī ibn Hātim, and Abū

---

## Page 16019

Mūsā, that he said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should do what is better and offer expiation for his oath.”

The same meaning is reported in the two Sahīh collections in hadiths narrated by Abū Hurayrah, Abū Mūsā, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Samurah. This encompasses all the oaths of Muslims: whoever swears any such oath and breaks it, expiation for an oath suffices him.

As for someone who swears a polytheistic oath—for example, by his father’s grave, the Kaaba, the sultan’s favour, the shaykh’s life, or any other created thing—such an oath is not binding, and no expiation is due if he breaks it, by agreement of the scholars.

The third category

This consists of expressions that make divorce, manumission, or a vow conditional upon something. For example, someone says, “If such-and-such happens, divorce shall be binding upon me,” or “Hajj shall be binding upon me,” or “My slaves shall be free,” and similar expressions.

Here, his intention must be considered. If he intends to swear an oath by this, rather than intending these matters to take effect—as when he does not intend divorce to take effect if the condition is fulfilled—then his case is treated as that of someone taking an oath, and falls under the rules governing oaths.

If, however, he intends these matters to take effect—as when he intends divorce to take effect upon fulfilment of the condition—an example is his saying to his wife, “If you absolve me in respect of your divorce, you are divorced,” and she absolves him. Another example is when he intends to divorce her if she commits an indecent act, and says, “If you do such-and-such, you are divorced.”

This differs from someone whose purpose is to swear an oath concerning her conduct in order to restrain her, but who would have no wish to divorce her even if she did it. Sometimes divorcing her is more abhorrent to him than the condition being fulfilled; in that case, he is taking an oath. At other times, the objectionable condition is more abhorrent to him than divorcing her; in that case, he brings divorce into effect upon the occurrence of

---

## Page 16020

that condition. In this case, divorce takes effect. Likewise, if he says, “If Allah heals my sick relative, I must fast for a month,” and that person recovers, he is obliged to fast.

The governing principle here is to consider the speaker’s intention and purpose. If his purpose is for these matters to take effect, they do so, whether declared outright or made conditional with the intention that they take effect when the condition is fulfilled. But if his purpose is to swear an oath by them, while disliking their taking effect should he break his oath, even if the condition is fulfilled, then he is swearing an oath by them, not bringing them into effect. His statement therefore falls under “oaths,” not “pronouncing divorce and making vows.”

Someone swearing an oath undertakes a commitment whose fulfilment he dislikes should he act contrary to his oath. For example, he might say, “If I do such-and-such, then I am a Jew or a Christian, my wives are divorced, my slaves are free, and I must walk to the House of Allah.” This and similar statements constitute an oath. This differs from someone who intends the consequence to take effect, whether by making a vow, pronouncing divorce, or making a conditional declaration: such a person intends and chooses to be bound by what he has undertaken. Both undertake a commitment, but the person swearing an oath dislikes the stipulated consequence taking effect, even if the condition on which it depends is fulfilled.

For example, if someone says, “If I do such-and-such, then I am a Jew or a Christian,” he dislikes disbelief even if the condition is fulfilled. Thus, he is swearing an oath. By contrast, someone bringing a consequence into effect intends that stipulated consequence to take effect when the condition on which it depends is fulfilled, whether he desires that condition, dislikes it, or does not desire it. Such a person is bringing the consequence into effect, not swearing an oath. Both undertake a conditional commitment, but the person swearing an oath dislikes the consequence taking effect.

This distinction is established from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the leading Successors. It is supported by the Quran and the Sunnah and is the position of the majority of scholars.

---

## Page 16021

such as al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, concerning conditional vows. They said: “If his intention is to make a vow and he says, ‘If Allah heals my sick relative, I must perform Hajj,’ then he is making a vow: if Allah heals his sick relative, Hajj becomes obligatory for him. This person is swearing an oath; expiation for an oath suffices him, and he is not obliged to perform Hajj.”

Likewise, the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—including Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, ʿĀʾishah, Umm Salamah, Zaynab, the Prophet’s stepdaughter, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and several other Companions—said concerning someone who declares, “If I do such-and-such, every slave I own is free”: “He must expiate his oath, but he is not obliged to free them.”

This is despite emancipation being an act of obedience and a means of drawing near to Allah. All the more reason, then, that divorce should not take effect. As Ibn ʿAbbās, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Divorce is for a purpose, and emancipation is that by which Allah’s pleasure is sought.” Al-Bukhārī cited this in his Sahīh. Ibn ʿAbbās explained that divorce takes effect only for someone whose purpose is to bring it about, not someone who dislikes its taking effect, such as someone swearing an oath by divorce or someone coerced into it.

It is also reported that ʿĀʾishah said: “Every oath, however grave, is expiated in the same way as an oath sworn by Allah.”

This encompasses all oaths, including those involving divorce, emancipation, vows, and other matters. The view that divorce does not take effect for someone who swears an oath by it is held by many scholars, both earlier and later. Some, however, do not require him to offer expiation, such as Dāwūd and his followers, while others require expiation for an oath, such as Tāwūs and other earlier and later scholars.

The oaths people swear are of three kinds.

The first is a sacred, binding oath, such as swearing by the name of Allah Most High. Expiation for such an oath is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus.

---

## Page 16022

The second is swearing by created things, such as swearing by the Kaaba. No expiation is due for such an oath, by agreement of the Muslims.

The third is to bind oneself by an oath to Allah, saying, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj,” or “my wealth shall be given in charity,” or “my wives are divorced,” or “my slaves are free,” and similar declarations. The three opinions mentioned earlier apply here: either the pledged consequence becomes binding, expiation is due, or neither applies.

Under the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, there are only two kinds of oath: an oath recognized as an oath of the Muslims, for which expiation is due; or an oath not recognized as an oath of the Muslims, for which nothing is due if it is broken. Thus, if these oaths are among the oaths of the Muslims, expiation is due; if they are not, nothing becomes binding through them. To posit an oath that obliges the swearer to fulfil what he has pledged, without allowing expiation to suffice, has no place in the religion of the Muslims. Rather, it contradicts the Quran and the Sunnah.

Allah Most High mentions the ruling on Muslims’ oaths in Surah al-Tahrīm, and the ruling on Muslims’ divorce in the surah preceding it. He says in Surah al-Tahrīm:

“O Prophet, why do you prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has already ordained for you the release from your oaths. Allah is your Protector, and He is the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

And He says in Surah al-Talāq:

“O Prophet, when you divorce women, divorce them at the proper time for their waiting period, and count the waiting period accurately. And fear Allah, your Lord. Do not turn them out of their homes, nor should they leave, unless they commit a clear indecency. These are the limits set by Allah. And whoever transgresses the limits of

---

## Page 16023

Allah has wronged himself. You do not know; perhaps Allah will bring about a new situation thereafter.”

“When they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably. Call two just men from among you as witnesses, and bear witness for Allah. This is an admonition for whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day. And whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him.”

“And He will provide for him from where he does not expect. Whoever puts his trust in Allah, He will suffice him. Indeed, Allah will accomplish His purpose. Allah has set a measure for everything.”

Thus, glory be to Him, He explained the ruling on divorce in this surah, and the ruling on Muslims’ oaths in the other. Muslims must understand the limits of what Allah has revealed to His Messenger, so that they know what falls under divorce and what falls under Muslims’ oaths, and judge these matters according to the ruling of Allah and His Messenger. They must not transgress Allah’s limits by applying the ruling on their oaths both to Muslims’ oaths and to their divorces, for this contradicts the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger.

Although some of this has been unclear to many Muslim scholars, other Muslim scholars have understood it. Those among the Companions and the Successors who distinguished between the two are held in higher esteem by Muslims than those who confused them. Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and leads to the best outcome.”

Whatever Muslims dispute must therefore be referred to the Quran and the Sunnah. Reasoning by comparison—which is the soundest and clearest form of analogy—supports only the view of those who distinguish between the two. This also benefits Muslims in their religious and worldly affairs if

---

## Page 16024

they distinguish between what Allah and His Messenger have distinguished. For those who fail to distinguish between these two matters have been led by this confusion either into burdens and fetters or into deception and stratagems: manipulating the wording of oaths, seeking to invalidate a marriage, employing dawr al-talāq (circular divorce), resorting to khulʿ (marital dissolution) to evade an oath, and arranging an intervening marriage to make remarriage to a former husband lawful.

Allah has spared Muslims any need for such things through their Prophet, of whom He says:

“He enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong, makes lawful for them what is good and forbids them what is impure, and relieves them of their burdens and the fetters that were upon them.”

That is, he frees them from burdens and fetters, and from becoming involved in the wrongdoing of those who resort to stratagems. And Allah Most High knows best.

Section: Distinguishing between conditional divorce intended to take effect and conditional divorce intended as an oath

The first is when a man intends the consequence to follow upon fulfilment of the condition, even though he dislikes that condition. If the condition is fulfilled, however, he wants the divorce, because the condition is more objectionable to him than divorce. Although he dislikes both divorcing her and the condition itself, he chooses to divorce her if the condition is fulfilled.

For example, he may be averse to being married to a woman who is a prostitute, immoral, or treacherous; or he may not wish to divorce her, yet would choose to do so if she committed such acts. He therefore says, “If you commit adultery, steal, or betray my trust, you are divorced.” His intention is to divorce her if she does that, either to punish her or because he is unwilling to remain with her

---

## Page 16025

in that condition. Such a man is making divorce take effect upon fulfilment of the condition, not swearing an oath. That divorce takes effect in such cases is what has been transmitted from the Companions, such as Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿUmar, and from the Successors and the other scholars. I know of no one among the early generations who said that divorce does not take effect in such a case. Some of the Shīʿah and some of the Zāhirīs, however, have disputed this.

Such a man is not swearing an oath, nor does his statement fall within the category of oaths that may be discharged by expiation, as mentioned in the Quran and Sunnah. Nevertheless, some people call him an oath-taker, just as some call anyone who makes a pronouncement conditional an oath-taker, while others call anyone who pronounces an immediately effective divorce an oath-taker. These three terminological usages have no basis in the language, in the words of the Lawgiver, or in the words of the Companions. This is called an oath only because, in the view of the person using that designation, it shares something with an oath: his belief that divorce takes effect upon fulfilment of the condition.

As for a conditional pronouncement intended as an oath, its meaning can be expressed in the form of an oath, unlike the first type, whose meaning cannot be expressed in that form. When such an oath is expressed in conditional form, it is only an oath if the speaker dislikes the consequence more than he dislikes the condition. Thus, he dislikes the condition, but dislikes the consequence still more, and commits himself to what he dislikes more in order to deter himself from what he dislikes less.

He might say, “If I do such-and-such, my wife is divorced,” or “My slaves are free,” or “Hajj becomes incumbent upon me,” and similar statements. Or he might say to his wife, “If you commit adultery, steal, or betray my trust, you are divorced,” intending to restrain or frighten her through the oath, not to make divorce take effect if she does so; for he wants to keep her even if

---

## Page 16026

she does so, because divorcing her is more abhorrent to him than her remaining in that condition. He has therefore made divorce conditional upon that act to prohibit and prevent it, not to make divorce take effect. Such a man is swearing an oath, not pronouncing an effective divorce. This is the oath-taker referred to in the Book and the Sunnah, for whom expiation suffices. People swear using the form of an oath, and they may also swear using a conditional expression that conveys the same meaning. If this is understood, the two are equivalent by scholarly consensus. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said, after discussing the three foundations upon which rulings concerning the fundamentals of religion rest—the Book, the Sunnah, and consensus—as mentioned earlier:

Section

“Divorce is of two types”

One type Allah has permitted, and another He has forbidden. The permitted type is for a man to divorce his wife, if she menstruates, after her menstruation has ended and before he has intercourse with her. This is called “divorce in accordance with the Sunnah.” If she does not menstruate, he may divorce her whenever he wishes. He may also divorce her while she is pregnant, once her pregnancy has become evident.

If he divorces her during menstruation, or during a period of purity after having intercourse with her, this is a forbidden divorce according to Muslim consensus. Scholars hold “two views” as to whether it takes effect. The stronger view is that it does not take effect.

---

## Page 16027

Permissible divorce in accordance with the Sunnah consists of pronouncing a single divorce, then either leaving her until her waiting period expires and she is no longer his wife, or taking her back during the waiting period.

If he pronounces three divorces, or pronounces a second or third divorce during that same period of purity, this is forbidden, and whoever does so is an innovator according to most scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad, according to the well-known position reported from him. The same applies if he pronounces a second and third divorce before taking her back or entering into a new marriage contract, according to Mālik, Ahmad—on the apparent position of his school—and others. However, does this count as one divorce or three? There are two views.

One view is that all three divorces take effect. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the well-known position of the other three imams.

The other view is that only one divorce takes effect. This is the view of many earlier and later scholars, as well as a group of scholars within the schools of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah. This view is stronger. It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, during Abū Bakr’s time, and during the early part of ʿUmar’s caliphate, three divorces counted as one.”

In Imam Ahmad’s Musnad, Ibn ʿAbbās reports through a good chain of transmission:

“Rukānah ibn ʿAbd Yazīd divorced his wife three times in a single sitting. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘It counts as one.’”

No one has transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through an established chain of transmission, that he enforced three divorces against someone who pronounced them all at once. As for the report about Rukānah which relates that he divorced her definitively, and that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “asked him,” saying, “Did you intend no more than one divorce?”—it is weak according to the leading hadith scholars. Ahmad, al-Bukhārī, Abū ʿUbayd, and Ibn Hazm declared it weak because its narrators were not established as possessing integrity and accuracy. Ahmad explained that the authentic account of Rukānah’s hadith is that he divorced her three times and the Prophet counted it as one. We have discussed this at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16028

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

When a man swears an oath, oaths fall into three categories:

The first consists of oaths that are not among the oaths of Muslims: swearing by created beings or things, such as the Kaaba, angels, shaykhs, kings, fathers, the soil of their graves, and the like. Such an oath is not binding and requires no expiation, by scholarly agreement. Indeed, scholars agree that such oaths are forbidden; according to the more correct of their two views, this prohibition means that they are unlawful.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever wishes to swear an oath should swear by Allah or remain silent.”

He also said:

“Allah forbids you to swear by your fathers.”

In the Sunan collections, he is reported to have said:

“Whoever swears by anything other than Allah has associated partners with Him.”

The second is an oath sworn by Allah Most High, such as saying, “By Allah, I will certainly do it.” This is a binding oath that requires expiation if it is broken, by the agreement of the Muslims.

There are also oaths taken by Muslims that have the same import as swearing by Allah: the person taking them intends to venerate the Creator, not to swear by created things. These include oaths involving vows, declaring something forbidden to oneself, divorce, or emancipation, such as saying: “If I do such-and-such, I must fast for a month or perform pilgrimage to the House of Allah”; or, “What is lawful is forbidden to me: I will not do such-and-such”; or, “If I do such-and-such, everything I own is forbidden to me”; or, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do such-and-such,” or, “I will not do it”; or, “If

---

## Page 16029

I do it, my wives shall be divorced, my slaves shall be free, and everything I own shall be given in charity,” and similar expressions. Scholars hold three views concerning these oaths. One view is that, if he breaks his oath, whatever he made conditional and swore to becomes binding upon him. Another is that nothing is binding upon him. A third is that he must offer the expiation for an oath.

Some scholars hold that expiation suffices for an oath involving a vow, whereas an oath involving divorce or emancipation makes what he swore to binding upon him.

The strongest view—which accords with the positions authentically reported from the Companions and is supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and reasoning—is that the expiation for an oath suffices for all oaths taken by Muslims. As Allah Most High says:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

And He says:

“Allah has already ordained for you the means of releasing yourselves from your oaths.”

It is authentically recorded that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should do what is better and offer expiation for his oath.”

Thus, if someone says, “What is lawful is forbidden to me: I will not do such-and-such”; or, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such”; or, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform pilgrimage”; or, “My wealth shall be given in charity”—the expiation for an oath suffices in these cases. If he offers the expiation prescribed for zihār, that is better.

For the expiation of an oath, he may choose between freeing a slave, feeding ten needy people, or clothing them. If he feeds them, he should give each person a customary ration in his locality—for example, eight or nine Syrian ounces—together with an accompaniment to the bread, as is customary among the people of Syria when distributing rations of bread and an accompaniment. If he offers expiation for his oath, no divorce takes effect thereby.

---

## Page 16030

If, however, he intends to effect a divorce in the manner prescribed by Islamic law—for example, by pronouncing an immediate, single divorce during a period of purity in which he has not had intercourse with her—the divorce takes effect by scholarly consensus.

Likewise, he may make divorce conditional upon a circumstance, intending it to take effect when that circumstance occurs. For example, he wishes to divorce her if she does a particular thing, so he tells her, “If you do it, you are divorced,” intending to divorce her if she does it. This constitutes a pronouncement of divorce, which takes effect according to the early generations and the overwhelming majority of later scholars.

This differs from someone whose intention in taking the oath is to forbid and deter her: even if she did what he dislikes, it would not be permissible for him to divorce her. Rather, he wishes to keep her even if she does it, but uses the oath to prevent her from doing it, without intending divorce to take effect even if she does. This is an oath, and according to the stronger of the two scholarly views held among the early and later generations, it does not cause divorce to take effect. Rather, the expiation for an oath suffices, as explained above.

Section

The divorce that unquestionably takes effect is the divorce Allah has authorized and permitted: a single divorce pronounced during a period of purity before he has intercourse with her, or after her pregnancy becomes apparent.

---

## Page 16031

As for “prohibited divorce,” such as divorcing her during menstruation or after having intercourse with her but before her pregnancy becomes apparent, this is prohibited by scholarly consensus. Likewise, pronouncing three divorces in one statement or several statements during a single period of purity is prohibited according to the majority of scholars. They disagree, however, about its effect. Some hold that all three divorces take effect; others hold that only one takes effect. The latter is the stronger view, supported by the Quran and Sunnah, as explained at length elsewhere.

Similarly, scholars hold two views on whether a prohibited divorce pronounced during menstruation or after intercourse takes effect. The stronger view is that it does not, just as a prohibited marriage or sale does not take effect. It is established in the Sahīh that Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), the time of Abū Bakr, and the early part of ‘Umar’s caliphate, a triple divorce counted as one.”

It is also established in the Musnad of Ahmad:

“Rukānah ibn ‘Abd Yazīd divorced his wife three times in a single sitting. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, ‘It counts as one.’”

Nothing contrary to this Sunnah has been established from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). Rather, reports that appear to contradict it are either weak—indeed, outweighed by stronger evidence—or authentic but do not indicate anything contrary to it, as explained at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16032

Section

Divorce includes divorce in accordance with the Sunnah, which Allah Most High has permitted, and divorce contrary to the Sunnah, which Allah has forbidden. Divorce in accordance with the Sunnah means pronouncing a single divorce after a woman’s menstruation has ended, before having intercourse with her, or divorcing her while she is visibly pregnant. If he divorces her during menstruation, or has intercourse with her and then divorces her before her pregnancy becomes apparent, this is “prohibited divorce” according to the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the Muslims. Scholars hold two views on whether it takes effect. The stronger view is that it does not.

If he pronounces three divorces in one statement or several statements during a single period of purity, before taking her back—for example, saying, “You are divorced three times,” “You are divorced a thousand times,” or “You are divorced; you are divorced; you are divorced,” or similar words—this is prohibited according to the majority of earlier and later scholars. It is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad, and is the apparent position of his school.

Likewise, if he pronounces three divorces before her waiting period has ended, this too is prohibited according to most scholars. It is the position of Mālik and the apparent position of Ahmad’s school.

The Sunnah, however, is that after pronouncing a single divorce, he does not divorce her a second time until he has either taken her back during the waiting period or remarried her under a new marriage contract after the waiting period. He may then divorce her a second time

---

## Page 16033

and likewise a third time. If he divorces her a third time as Allah and His Messenger have commanded, she becomes unlawful to him until she marries another husband.

But if he pronounces “three divorces” in a prohibited manner—for example, by saying to her, “You are divorced three times,” all at once—the scholars hold two views. “The first” is that all three divorces take effect. “The second” is that only one divorce takes effect, and he may take her back during the waiting period or remarry her under a new marriage contract after it ends. This is the view of many earlier and later scholars, including a group of scholars belonging to the schools of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal. It is the stronger of the two views, supported by much evidence.

Among this evidence is the report established in the authentic hadith collection from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“During the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, and Abū Bakr, and during the early part of ‘Umar’s caliphate, three divorces were counted as one.”

Another is the report transmitted by Imam Ahmad and others with a good chain of transmission from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“Rukānah ibn ‘Abd Yazīd divorced his wife three times in one sitting, then came to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who said, ‘It is only one divorce,’ and restored her to him.”

Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others authenticated this hadith. Ahmad, Abū ‘Ubayd, Ibn Hazm, and others, however, judged weak the report:

“That he divorced her ‘definitively,’ and that [the Prophet] required him to swear, asking, ‘Did you intend only one divorce?’”

The narrators of this latter report are unknown: neither their accuracy in transmission nor their uprightness is established, whereas the narrators of the former report are known for both.

No one has transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, through an acceptable chain of transmission, that someone divorced his wife three times in a single statement and that he ruled all three divorces binding upon him. Reports to that effect have indeed been narrated, but the scholars of hadith agree that they are all fabricated.

Authentic hadiths do, however, state:

“So-and-so divorced his wife three times.”

This means three separate divorces. It is also reported:

“The husband who engaged in mutual imprecation pronounced three divorces.”

But that was a woman whom he had no possibility of taking back; rather, she is

---

## Page 16034

forbidden to him whether he divorces her or not. This is like a Muslim pronouncing three divorces upon his wife after she apostatizes; or a Jewish man’s wife embracing Islam and his pronouncing three divorces upon her; or a polytheist woman’s husband embracing Islam and pronouncing three divorces upon her. Divorce as prescribed by Islamic law concerns a wife whom the husband may take back or remarry under a new marriage contract. And Allah knows best.

Section

(*)

If a married man swears an oath by declaring the lawful forbidden to himself, saying, “I bind myself to prohibition: I will not do such-and-such”; or, “What is lawful is forbidden to me: I will not do such-and-such”; or, “What Allah has made lawful is forbidden to me if I do such-and-such”; or, “What is lawful for Muslims becomes forbidden to me if I do such-and-such”; or something similar—there is a well-known disagreement on this issue among earlier and later scholars. The stronger view, however, is that this is an oath like other oaths, and no divorce becomes binding upon him through it, even if he intended thereby to swear by divorce.

This is the well-known position of Imam Ahmad. Indeed, even if a man says, “You are forbidden to me,” intending divorce by those words, no divorce takes effect according to him.

Likewise, if he says, “You are to me like my mother’s back,” intending divorce, no divorce takes effect according to the general body of scholars. Allah revealed Quranic verses concerning this, for they used to regard zihār (declaring one’s wife forbidden by likening her to one’s mother) as divorce, and an oath to abstain from marital relations as divorce. Allah abolished all of that and prescribed the major expiation for zihār. He made the oath to abstain from marital relations an oath for which the husband is allowed four months; thereafter, he must either retain his wife honourably or release her graciously.

Similarly, many earlier and later scholars said that if a married man declares his wife forbidden to himself, or declares everything lawful forbidden to himself without qualification, he has made a declaration of zihār. This is Ahmad’s position. If he swears, by invoking zihār or declaring the lawful forbidden, that he will not do something, and then breaks

> **Footnotes:**

---

## Page 16035

his oath, an expiation suffices for him according to his school. However, it has been said that the required expiation is that prescribed for zihār, whether he uses the declaration as an oath or makes it effective. This is the position transmitted from Ahmad.

Another view is that if he uses it as an oath, the expiation for an oath suffices; but if he makes it effective, the expiation for zihār is binding upon him. This view is stronger and more consistent with the principles of Ahmad and others.

Thus, the expiation for an oath suffices for someone who swears by declaring the lawful forbidden, just as it suffices for someone who swears by invoking a vow, saying, “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to perform Hajj,” or, “My wealth shall be given in charity.” Likewise, if he swears by invoking the freeing of a slave, an expiation suffices according to most of the early scholars among the Companions and their successors. Similarly, the expiation for an oath also suffices for an oath invoking divorce, as [a group] of earlier and later scholars ruled. What is authentically reported from the Companions does not contradict this; rather, its meaning accords with it. Thus, every form of oath that Muslims use in their swearing admits of the expiation for an oath, as indicated by the Quran and the Sunnah.

If, however, a man intends to divorce, free a slave, or pronounce zihār, then the declaration he makes is binding upon him, whether immediate or conditional, and the expiation for an oath does not suffice. And Allah, Glorified is He, knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

If someone divorces his wife during menstruation or postnatal bleeding, does the divorce take effect or not?

He replied:

As for his saying to her, “You are divorced three times,” while she is menstruating, this issue rests on two principles. The first is that divorce during menstruation is forbidden by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus, for

---

## Page 16036

there is no known disagreement concerning its prohibition. It is an innovated form of divorce. Divorce in accordance with the Sunnah, by contrast, is to divorce her during a period of purity in which he has not had intercourse with her, or while she is visibly pregnant. If he divorces her during menstruation, or after having intercourse with her and before her pregnancy becomes apparent to him, this is an innovated form of divorce. As Allah Most High says:

“O Prophet, when you divorce women, divorce them at the proper time for their waiting period, and keep count of the waiting period.”

The Sahīh, Sunan, and Musnad collections report that Ibn ʿUmar divorced his wife while she was menstruating. ʿUmar mentioned this to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Tell him to take her back until she menstruates, then becomes pure, then menstruates, then becomes pure. Then, if he wishes, he may keep her, and if he wishes, he may divorce her before having intercourse with her. That is the waiting period in which Allah has commanded that women be divorced.”

As for pronouncing all three divorces together, there are two views. The first is that this too is forbidden, according to most scholars among the Companions, their successors, and those who came after them. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, which most of his followers adopted. Ahmad said: “I reflected on the Quran and found that every divorce mentioned in it is revocable”—meaning the divorce of a wife with whom the marriage has been consummated—“except in His words:

‘If he divorces her, she is not lawful for him thereafter until she marries another husband.’”

According to this view, may he pronounce the second and third divorces before taking her back, by spreading the divorces over three periods of purity and pronouncing one divorce in each? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad. The first is that he may do so. This is the view of a group of the early scholars and the position of Abū Hanīfah. The second is that he may not do so. This is the view of most of the early scholars

---

## Page 16037

and the position of Mālik, as well as the sounder of the two positions reported from Ahmad, adopted by most of his followers, including Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, Judge Abū Yaʿlā, and his followers.

The second view is that pronouncing all three divorces together is not forbidden, but merely falls short of what is preferable. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the other position reported from Ahmad, which al-Khiraqī adopted. They cited as evidence that “Fātimah bint Qays was divorced three times by her husband, Abū Hafs ibn al-Mughīrah; Rifāʿah’s wife was divorced three times by her husband; and the husband involved in mutual imprecation divorced his wife three times, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not object to this.”

The majority replied that, in the reports concerning Fātimah and Rifāʿah’s wife, the three divorces were pronounced on separate occasions. This is established in the Sahīh: the third was the last of three separate divorces. Neither husband pronounced all three together.

A Companion’s statement, “He divorced her three times,” includes divorcing her on three separate occasions: he divorces her, then takes her back, then divorces her, then takes her back, then divorces her. This is divorce in accordance with the Sunnah, and all the imams agree that it takes effect. This was the commonly understood meaning of divorcing three times during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Pronouncing all three divorces in a single utterance, however, was considered objectionable among them and occurred only rarely. An unqualified expression may therefore not be interpreted as referring to the rare, objectionable practice rather than the common, legitimate one. Nor may it be claimed that either of these husbands pronounced all three divorces together. Such a claim has no evidence; indeed, it contradicts the evidence.

As for the husband involved in mutual imprecation, his pronouncement of divorce came after irrevocable separation, or after separation had become obligatory—a separation that makes the wife unlawful to him more absolutely than a third divorce does. His pronouncement thus confirmed the consequence of mutual imprecation.

---

## Page 16038

and the dispute concerns only divorce pronounced by a man who can retain his wife. Moreover, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, separated the couple. If that separation preceded the three pronouncements, no divorce took effect, whether three or otherwise. If it followed them, this indicates that the marriage had remained in force. What is known is that he separated them after the husband had pronounced three divorces. This indicates that the three divorces did not take effect, for otherwise she would have become unlawful to him until she married another husband. It would then have been impossible for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to separate them, since they were no longer married to one another.

The most that could be argued, however, is: “He made her permanently unlawful to him.” The reply is: “In that case, he should have declared her unlawful to him, rather than separated them.” His separating them therefore indicates that the marriage had remained in force and that all three divorces had not taken effect. This differs from saying that a single revocable divorce took effect, for in that case he could still separate them.

Sahl ibn Saʿd’s statement, “He divorced her three times, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave effect to it against him,” indicates that this required the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to give it effect, and that this was specific to the husband involved in mutual imprecation. Had his legislation provided that three pronouncements made her unlawful, there would have been nothing specific to that husband, nor any need to give the pronouncement effect. This therefore indicates that, when the husband intended by pronouncing three divorces to make her unlawful to him, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave effect to his intention—indeed, went beyond it. For the prohibition resulting from mutual imprecation is more stringent than that resulting from divorce: the former is not lifted even if she marries another husband. According to one of the two scholarly views, it is permanent and is not lifted by repentance.

The majority also argued that the Glorious Quran indicates that Allah has permitted only revocable divorce, and only divorce timed to the waiting period, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“O Prophet, when you divorce women

---

## Page 16039

, then divorce them at the commencement of their waiting period and keep count of the waiting period,” down to His words:

“You do not know; perhaps Allah will bring about a new situation thereafter.”

“Then, when they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably.”

This applies only to revocable divorce.

His words:

“Then divorce them at the commencement of their waiting period”

indicate that it is impermissible to follow one divorce with another until the waiting period has ended or the husband has taken his wife back. For He permitted divorce only in relation to the waiting period—that is, at its commencement. Whenever a husband pronounces a second and third divorce before taking his wife back, she continues the existing waiting period rather than beginning it anew, as the overwhelming majority of Muslims agree. If there is an anomalous dissenting view on this matter from Khilās and Ibn Hazm, we have explained its invalidity elsewhere.

This is a weak position. In the early days of Islam, when a man wished to harm his wife, he would divorce her, then take her back just as her waiting period was about to end, and then divorce her again to prolong her confinement. Had she been required to begin the waiting period anew without his taking her back, there would have been no need for him to take her back. Allah, Exalted is He, limited them to three divorces to prevent this harm, as the transmitted reports relate. This indicates that it was an established ruling with Allah that the waiting period does not begin anew without the husband taking his wife back—whether because a divorce pronounced before taking her back does not take effect, or because it takes effect without requiring a new waiting period.

Ibn Hazm required a new waiting period only on the grounds that divorce must mark the commencement of a waiting period, so that every divorce after consummation must be followed by a waiting period, as the Quran indicates. On that basis, he was committed to this unsound position.

Those who adhere to what the Quran entails and what the transmitted reports indicate, however, say: The divorce Allah prescribed is one followed by a waiting period during which the husband has the choice of retaining his wife honourably or releasing her graciously. These conditions are absent when three divorces are pronounced during the waiting period before taking her back; therefore, this cannot

---

## Page 16040

be permissible, since that would not be a divorce at the commencement of a waiting period. Moreover, He said:

“Then, when they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or part with them honourably.”

He thus gave the husband the choice between taking her back and allowing her to complete the waiting period, thereby releasing her graciously. If he divorces her a second time before the waiting period ends, he has neither retained her honourably nor released her graciously.

Allah, Exalted is He, also said:

“Divorced women shall wait for three cycles. It is not lawful for them to conceal what Allah has created in their wombs, if they believe in Allah and the Last Day. Their husbands have a greater right to take them back during that period.”

This implies that such is the situation of every divorced woman; thus, He prescribed only this form of divorce. He then said:

“Divorce is twice.”

That is, the divorce just described may occur twice. If someone is told, “Glorify Allah twice,” or “three times,” it is not sufficient for him merely to say, “Glory be to Allah twice.” Rather, he must utter the glorification once after another. Likewise, one can only say, “He divorced her twice,” if he divorced her on one occasion and then another. If he says, “You are divorced three times,” or “twice,” one cannot say that he divorced her on three occasions or two occasions, although one may say that he pronounced three divorces or two divorces.

Then He said:

“If he divorces her, she is not lawful for him thereafter until she marries another husband.”

Allah prescribed this third divorce only after two occasions of revocable divorce.

Allah, Exalted is He, also said:

“When you divorce women and they have completed their waiting period, do not prevent them from remarrying their husbands.”

And so the verse continues. This applies only where fewer than three divorces have occurred, yet it encompasses every divorce. It is therefore clear that combining three divorces is not prescribed. And the proofs that pronouncing three divorces together is forbidden

---

## Page 16041

are many and compelling, drawn from the Quran and Sunnah, transmitted reports, and reasoned consideration, as explained in detail elsewhere. The reason is that “the basic rule concerning divorce is prohibition”; it is permitted only to the extent needed. As authentically reported from Jābir, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Iblīs sets his throne upon the sea and sends out his detachments. Those held closest to him are those who cause the greatest turmoil. A devil comes to him and says, ‘I kept after him until he did such-and-such.’ Eventually, a devil comes to him and says, ‘I kept after him until I separated him from his wife.’ He then draws him close, saying, ‘You are the one! You are the one!’ and embraces him.”

Allah, Exalted is He, said in condemnation of sorcery:

“They learned from them that by which they caused separation between a man and his wife.”

The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Women who seek divorce in return for compensation and those who seek to break away from their husbands are hypocrites.”

The Sunan collections also report that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:
