“Any woman who asks her husband for a divorce without any hardship necessitating it will be forbidden the fragrance of Paradise.”

For this reason, divorce is permitted only three times, and after the third divorce the woman becomes unlawful for him until she marries another husband. Since divorce is permitted only to meet a need, and that need is met by a single divorce, anything beyond that remains subject to the original prohibition.

The second principle concerns whether a prohibited divorce, known as an “innovated divorce,” takes effect when someone pronounces it. The earlier and later generations differed over this. The majority hold that it takes effect, while affirming that it is prohibited. Others hold that it does not take effect, including Tāwūs, Ikrima, Khilās, Umar, Muhammad ibn Ishāq, Hajjāj ibn Artāh, and the literalists:

---

## Page 16042

such as Dāwūd and his followers, and a group among the followers of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad. This is also reported from Abū Jaʿfar al-Bāqir, Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad al-Sādiq, and others of the Prophet’s household. It is the position of the literalists—Dāwūd and his followers—although some of them do not regard pronouncing three divorces together as prohibited.

Among the followers of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad are some who are known to hold that, when three divorces are pronounced together, they do not all take effect; rather, only one takes effect. Their position on divorcing a menstruating woman, however, is not known. Nevertheless, the view that all the divorces take effect is held by groups among the speculative theologians and the Shīʿa.

Among both these groups are those who say that, if a man pronounces three divorces together, none takes effect at all. This, however, is an innovated position whose advocates have no known predecessor among the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness. And groups among the speculative theologians and the Shīʿa. Ibn Hazm, however, who belongs to the literalists, does not regard pronouncing three divorces together as prohibited; he therefore holds that they take effect. Most literalists regard this as prohibited and hold that only one divorce takes effect.

Some of these scholars’ positions on triple divorce are known, while their positions on divorce during menstruation are not, as with those followers of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik from whom this view is transmitted. It has been reported from Ibn Umar through two routes that such a divorce does not take effect. Through other, better-known and more reliable routes, however, it has been reported from him that it does take effect. This has also been reported from Zayd.

As for “pronouncing three divorces together,” the Companions’ positions on this are numerous and well known. The view that they take effect has been reported from Umar, Uthmān, Ali, Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn Abbās, Ibn Umar, Abū Hurayra, Imrān ibn Husayn, and others. The view that they do not take effect has been reported from Abū Bakr, from Umar during the early part of his caliphate, and also from Ali ibn Abī Tālib, Ibn Masʿūd, and Ibn Abbās, as well as from al-Zubayr and Abd al-Rahmān ibn Awf. May Allah be pleased with them all.

---

## Page 16043

Abū Jaʿfar Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Mughīth said in his book entitled The Sufficient Guide to the Principles of Legal Documents and the Elucidation of Their Subtleties:

“An innovated divorce is for a man to pronounce three divorces upon his wife in a single utterance. If he does so, the divorce is binding upon him. The scholars, while agreeing that he has pronounced a divorce, then differed: how many divorces take effect?

“Ali ibn Abī Tālib and Ibn Masʿūd, may Allah be pleased with them both, said that only one divorce takes effect. Ibn Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him and his father, held the same view. This is because saying ‘three times’ has no significance, since he has not divorced her on three occasions. If he were reporting what had happened previously and said, ‘I divorced her three times,’ reporting three divorces that he had pronounced in three separate acts, that would be correct. But if he had divorced her only once and then said, ‘I divorced her three times,’ he would be lying.

“Similarly, if he swore by Allah three times, repeating the oath, these would be three oaths. But if he swore by Allah, saying, ‘I swear by Allah three times,’ he would have sworn only one oath. The same applies to divorce.”

He said: “Al-Zubayr ibn al-Awwām and Abd al-Rahmān ibn Awf held the same view. We have transmitted all of this from Ibn Waddāh”—meaning Imam Muhammad ibn Waddāh, who studied under scholars of the generation of Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ibn Abī Shayba, Yahyā ibn Maʿīn, Sahnūn ibn Saʿīd, and their contemporaries.

He said: “Among the scholars of Cordoba who held this view were Ibn Zinbāʿ, a rightly guided shaykh; Muhammad ibn Abd al-Salām al-Husaynī, the leading jurist of his age; Ibn Baqī ibn Makhlad; Asbagh ibn al-Habbāb; and a number of other jurists of Cordoba.” He also attributed this view to more than ten devout jurists of Toledo who followed the school of Mālik ibn Anas.

I say: Al-Tilimsānī mentioned it as a view reported from Mālik. It is also the position of Muhammad ibn Muqātil al-Rāzī, one of the leading Hanafī scholars. He related it from al-Māzinī and others. This has also been mentioned as a view reported from

---

## Page 16044

Mālik. Shaykh Abū al-Barakāt ibn Taymiyya would sometimes issue fatwas to this effect. He and others cite as evidence the hadith reported by Muslim in his Sahīh, Abū Dāwūd, and others, through Tāwūs, from Ibn Abbās, who said:

“During the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, during Abū Bakr’s time, and for two years of Umar’s caliphate, a triple divorce was counted as one. Then Umar ibn al-Khattāb said, ‘People have rushed into a matter in which they had room for deliberation. What if we made it binding upon them?’ So he made it binding upon them.”

Another version relates that Abū al-Sahbāʾ said to Ibn Abbās:

“Tell us something of what you know. Was not a triple divorce counted as one during the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, and Abū Bakr?”

He replied: “That was indeed so. But during Umar’s time, people began resorting to divorce in quick succession, so he made it binding upon them and upheld its effect.”

Those who rejected this hadith offered strained interpretations of it. Likewise, every report stating that “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, made three divorces binding on account of an oath pronouncing them all at once, or that someone in his time pronounced them all at once and he held him bound by them”—such as a report narrated from Ali, another from Ubāda ibn al-Sāmit, another through al-Hasan from Ibn Umar, and others—is weak by the agreement of hadith scholars. Indeed, these reports are fabricated, and scholars versed in hadith criticism recognize them as fabrications, as explained at length elsewhere.

Their strongest objection to this hadith is their statement: “It is established through several routes that Ibn Abbās issued fatwas declaring three divorces binding.”

---

## Page 16045

Those who cite this hadith as evidence reply that it is also reported from Ibn Abbās, through Ikrima, that he counted it as one divorce. Reports through Ikrima from Ibn Abbās agreeing with Tāwūs’s hadith are established, both as reports traced back to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and as statements of Ibn Abbās himself. Nothing contrary to this has been established from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. The report traced back to him states:

“Rukāna divorced his wife three times, but the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, restored her to him.”

Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal said in his Musnad: Sa‘īd ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us: My father narrated to us, from Ibn Ishāq, who said: Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn narrated to me, from Ikrima, the freedman of Ibn Abbās, who said:

“Rukāna ibn Abd Yazīd, a member of Banū al-Muttalib, divorced his wife three times in one sitting and then grieved deeply over her. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, asked him, ‘How did you divorce her?’

“He replied, ‘I divorced her three times.’

“He asked, ‘In one sitting?’

“He replied, ‘Yes.’

“He said, ‘That counts as only one divorce, so take her back if you wish.’ He then took her back.”

Ibn Abbās used to say: “Divorce is to be pronounced only at each period of purity.”

I say: In this hadith, Ibn Ishāq expressly said, “Dāwūd narrated to me.” Dāwūd was one of Mālik’s teachers and one of the narrators cited by al-Bukhārī. When Ibn Ishāq says, “He narrated to me,” he is considered trustworthy by hadith scholars. This is a good chain of transmission, and it has a corroborating report through another route, related by Abū Dāwūd in his Sunan. Abū Dāwūd did not mention this good route, and therefore considered the report of a single irrevocable divorce more authentic. The matter is not as he stated; rather, Imam Ahmad preferred this version to that one, and Ahmad’s assessment is correct. We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 16046

This account of Rukāna is reported from Ibn Abbās through two routes: it is Ikrima’s report from Ibn Abbās, transmitted through two routes from Ikrima. It is more firmly established than the report of Abd Allāh ibn Alī ibn Yazīd ibn Rukāna and Nāfi ibn Ajīr: “He divorced her with the wording al-batta (definitively), and the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, required him to swear an oath, asking, ‘You intended only one divorce?’” These narrators are unknown, their standing is not known, and they are not jurists. Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Abū Ubayd, Ibn Hazm and others classified their hadith as weak.

Ahmad ibn Hanbal said: “Rukāna’s hadith concerning the wording ‘definitively’ has no standing.”

He also said: “The report about Rukāna does not establish that he divorced his wife with the wording ‘definitively,’ because Ibn Ishāq narrates from Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn, from Ikrima, from Ibn Abbās, that Rukāna divorced his wife three times. The people of Medina refer to three divorces as ‘definitively.’”

Thus, Ahmad cited this other hadith, which states that he divorced her three times, as evidence that the hadith containing the wording “definitively” is unsound. He explained that the people of Medina describe someone who has pronounced three divorces as having divorced “definitively.” This shows that he regarded the hadith as established. Other leading hadith scholars have also clarified this. This chain of transmission—Ibn Ishāq’s statement, “Dāwūd ibn al-Husayn narrated to me, from Ikrima, from Ibn Abbās”—is considered established by Ahmad and other scholars.

Through this same chain it is reported that “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, restored his daughter Zaynab to her husband on the basis of their original marriage contract.” Ahmad and other scholars authenticated this report.

When Ibn Ishāq says, “He narrated to me,” hadith scholars consider his report authentic. The only concern is possible concealment of an intermediary when he narrates using “from.” Abū Dāwūd also related this report from Ibn Abbās through another route in his Sunan. Both versions agree with Tāwūs’s hadith from Ibn Abbās. Ahmad used to counter Tāwūs’s hadith with the hadith of Fātima bint Qays, which states that her husband divorced her three times, and with similar reports.

---

## Page 16047

Ahmad used to regard pronouncing three divorces together as permissible. He later retracted this view and said: “I reflected on the Quran and found that the divorce mentioned in it is revocable divorce”—or words to that effect. This became his settled position, followed by the majority of his companions. It also became clear from Fātima’s hadith that she had been divorced three times on separate occasions, not all together. Moreover, he accepted as established two hadiths from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, stating that when someone pronounces three divorces together, only one takes effect. Nothing reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, contradicts this; rather, the Quran agrees with it. Furthermore, according to Ahmad, prohibition entails invalidity.

These texts and his established principles therefore imply that, according to his school, only one divorce takes effect. His initial decision not to follow the hadith of Rukāna and other reports arose because they conflicted with his view at that time that pronouncing three divorces together was permissible; this indicated that those reports had been abrogated. He subsequently abandoned that objection, recognizing that the counterargument was unsound and that pronouncing three divorces together was impermissible. His own principles therefore required him to act upon the texts that remained free of counterevidence.

Nor is Tāwūs’s hadith impugned by Ibn Abbās’s having issued a contrary fatwa. This was his understanding according to one of the two reports from him. However, the apparent position of his school, followed by his companions, is that this does not undermine acting upon the hadith, especially since Ibn Abbās explained Umar ibn al-Khattāb’s justification—may Allah be pleased with him—for making all three divorces binding. Ibn Abbās’s own justification was the same one he related concerning Umar, may Allah be pleased with him: when people repeatedly engaged in what Allah had forbidden them, they deserved punishment for it, and were therefore punished by having those divorces made binding upon them. Their circumstances had previously been different, for they had not been committing the prohibited act so frequently.

---

## Page 16048

This is comparable to how, when people began drinking wine frequently and treating its prescribed punishment lightly, Umar would impose eighty lashes, banishment, and shaving of the head—measures not practiced during the lifetime of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Likewise, Ali fought some of those who faced the same direction in prayer, something that had not occurred during the Prophet’s lifetime, peace and blessings be upon him.

Separating spouses was also among the punishments they sometimes imposed, either while leaving the marriage bond intact or by dissolving it. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, separated the three men whose case had been deferred from their wives until Allah accepted their repentance, without any divorce taking place. A man who divorces his wife three times is forbidden to remarry her until she marries another husband, as a punishment intended to deter him from divorce. Umar ibn al-Khattāb and those who agreed with him, such as Mālik and Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him, held that a woman married during her waiting period became permanently forbidden to the man who married her. Because he had hastened to obtain what Allah had made lawful, he was punished with the opposite of what he intended.

According to most of the early scholars, the two arbiters may separate the spouses without compensation if they judge the husband to be unjust and abusive, since this restrains his wrongdoing and protects the wife from harm. This is supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and reports from the early generations. It is Mālik’s position and one of the two views within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

When Umar made all three divorces binding after people began pronouncing them frequently, he may have regarded this as a punishment to be applied when needed. Alternatively, he may have regarded it as a binding legal ruling, believing that the concession had applied when Muslims pronounced such divorces only rarely.

Similarly, people have differed in explaining his prohibition of performing umrah followed by Hajj on the same journey: was he merely discouraging it as a matter of preference, because making a separate journey for Hajj and another for umrah was better than combining them in one journey? Or had he prohibited converting Hajj into umrah because he believed that this had been exclusively permitted to the Companions? In either case, the Companions had

---

## Page 16049

disputed this with him, and many of their leading figures, including members of the consultative council and others, disagreed with him regarding both performing umrah followed by Hajj on the same journey and making three pronouncements of divorce binding. When they disagree over something, the matter in dispute must be referred to Allah and the Messenger. Similarly, Umar held that an irrevocably divorced woman was entitled to neither maintenance nor accommodation. Many Companions disagreed with him, and most scholars follow their position. He and Ibn Mas‘ūd also held that someone in a state of major ritual impurity could not perform dry ablution. Ammār, Abū Mūsā, Ibn Abbās, and other Companions disagreed with them, and the scholars unanimously adopted the latter view, since it was supported by the Quran and Sunnah. There is much to discuss on this subject, and it has been treated extensively elsewhere. The purpose here is to draw attention to the positions people have adopted.

Those who do not consider prohibited divorce binding say: This accords with the foundational principle followed by the leading jurists, such as Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others: concluding prohibited contracts does not make them binding. Examples include prohibited sales, prohibited marriages, and prohibited contracts by which slaves purchase their freedom. Accordingly, they invalidated shighār marriages (exchange marriages) and muhallil marriages (marriages intended to make a divorced woman lawful again for her former husband). Mālik and Ahmad also invalidated sales concluded at the call to Friday prayer.

This differs from the prohibited declaration of zihār (declaring one’s wife as forbidden as one’s mother), for that utterance is itself prohibited, just as slanderous accusations of unlawful sexual intercourse, false testimony, knowingly false oaths, and all other utterances that are themselves prohibited. Such speech cannot be divided into valid and invalid forms; rather, whoever utters it deserves punishment in every case. Thus, the person who pronounced zihār was punished by being required to offer expiation, while the divorce he intended by it did not take effect. They used it to intend divorce, which its wording entailed, but the Lawgiver nullified that effect because it was a prohibited utterance and required expiation for it.

Divorce, however, is lawful in principle, like marriage and sale: it is permissible in some circumstances and prohibited in others

---

## Page 16050

and thus falls into valid and invalid forms, just as sale and marriage do. Prohibition in matters of this kind entails the invalidity of what is prohibited. Since the people of pre-Islamic ignorance used to divorce their wives through zihār, and the Lawgiver nullified this because it was a prohibited utterance, the implication is that divorce cannot take effect through any prohibited utterance. They did, after all, intend divorce by the formula of zihār, just as by declaring one’s wife forbidden. This follows by analogy from the foundational principles of the leading jurists Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. Those who departed from the implications of their own principles regarding divorce did so because of the transmitted reports that had reached them.

When they considered it established that Ibn Umar had counted the divorce he pronounced upon his wife while she was menstruating, they said, “They were better informed about his case,” and followed him in this.

Those who dispute their position say: Ibn Umar and others have always narrated hadiths without scholars necessarily accepting their understanding of them. What matters is what they narrated, not their own views and interpretations. Most scholars rejected Ibn Umar’s interpretation of the Prophet’s words:

“Then reckon it.”

Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and others rejected his interpretation of the hadith:

“The buyer and seller have the right to choose,”

even though his view accords with the hadith’s apparent meaning. Most scholars also rejected his interpretation of the words:

“So approach your place of cultivation however you wish,”

and his statement that this verse was revealed concerning such-and-such.

The same applies when a narrator’s opinion contradicts what he narrated. Thus, the four imams and others rejected Ibn Abbās’s view that selling a slave woman constitutes her divorce, even though he narrated the hadith about “Barīrah and how the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, gave her the choice after she had been sold and freed.” What matters is what they narrated, not their own views and interpretations.

---

## Page 16051

When they considered it established that the leading Companions had enforced three divorces pronounced together, they said: “They would only enforce this if Islamic law required it.” Some held that this divorce was binding and that there was consensus on the matter, because they knew of no established disagreement—especially since the contrary view had become associated with the Shia, who have never differed from Ahl al-Sunnah on the basis of truth.

Those advancing the argument said: “The view held by some Shia and a group of speculative theologians—that pronouncing three divorces together has no effect whatsoever—is not known to have been held by anyone among the early generations. Rather, consensus had already been established regarding part of it. The question is only whether one divorce is binding or three take effect. Disagreement among the early generations on this point is established and cannot be dismissed.

“Those who make this a binding rule of Islamic law for the Muslim community have no evidence from the Quran, the Sunnah, or consensus that must be followed. Some have indeed argued for it from the Quran, others from the Sunnah, and others from consensus; some have invoked two or more of these grounds. Their opponent, however, demonstrates that all these arguments are weak, that the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning indicate that it is not binding, and that there is no consensus on the matter. Indeed, the authentic reports from the Companions who enforced three divorces pronounced together indicate that they did not regard this as a binding rule prescribed by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, for his community, as he prescribed that a woman becomes forbidden to her husband after the third divorce. Rather, they exercised their independent judgment in imposing this as a punishment when the practice became widespread and people would not desist from it.”

---

## Page 16052

The statements reported from the Companions, which have already been cited, show that they enforced three divorces against anyone who disobeyed Allah by pronouncing them together. As for one who fears Allah, Allah says:

“And whoever fears Allah, He will make for him a way out.”

“And will provide for him from where he does not expect.”

Thus, someone who did not know this was forbidden until after pronouncing them, then repented upon learning of the prohibition and committed himself not to return to it, does not deserve to be punished. Nothing in the sources of Islamic law—the Quran, the Sunnah, consensus, or analogy—requires that all three divorces be binding upon him. His marriage is established with certainty, and his wife is forbidden to anyone else with certainty. Enforcing all three divorces against him makes her lawful for another man while making her forbidden to him, and opens the way to a tahlīl marriage—a marriage intended to make her lawful again for her former husband—which Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.

“Tahlīl marriage” was not openly practised during the time of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his caliphs. It has never been reported that, during their time, a woman was returned to her husband after the third divorce through a tahlīl marriage. Rather, “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, cursed the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband, and the former husband for whose benefit this is done.” He also “cursed the recipient of usury, its payer, its two witnesses, and its scribe.”

In the case of tahlīl, he did not mention the witnesses, the wife, or her guardian, because the tahlīl then practised involved an intention concealed by the man marrying her, or an arrangement between him and the divorcing husband for whose benefit it was done. The woman and her guardian did not know his intention. Had they known, they would not have agreed to marry her to him, for people regarded this as among the most abhorrent and reprehensible practices. Moreover, it was not their custom to record the dower in a document or have it witnessed. Rather, they would marry and announce the marriage publicly, without undertaking to have two witnesses present at the time of the marriage contract, as is

---

## Page 16053

the position of Mālik and, according to one of the two reports from him, Ahmad. There is no authentic hadith from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, requiring witnesses to a marriage. This is what Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others stated.

Since no tahlīl marriage was openly practised during the time of ʿUmar, may Allah be pleased with him, and he considered enforcing the three divorces a deterrent against the forbidden practice, he did so on the basis of his own legal judgment. However, if the person concerned does not deserve punishment, and enforcing the three divorces leads to tahlīl marriages—which are forbidden by explicit scriptural texts and the consensus of the Companions—as well as harmful beliefs and other evils, it is impermissible to remove a genuine harm by means of greater harms. Rather, counting the three divorces as one in such circumstances, as was done during the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, and Abū Bakr, is more appropriate. For this reason, some scholars, such as Abū al-Barakāt, issued rulings that the three divorces were binding in certain circumstances but not in others, as has been reported from the Companions.

This was either because they regarded it as a matter of “discretionary punishment,” which may be imposed as the need arises—such as exceeding forty lashes for drinking wine, imposing banishment for it, or shaving the head—or because their legal judgments differed: at times they considered the three divorces binding, and at other times they did not.

In short, whatever the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prescribed for his community as “binding legislation” cannot be changed, because no abrogation is possible after the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. It is impermissible to suppose that any Muslim scholar would intend such a thing, especially the Companions, and above all the Rightly Guided Caliphs.

Only people of ignorance and misguidance entertain such suspicions about the Companions, such as the Rāfidah and the Khawārij, who declare some of the caliphs unbelievers or evildoers. Even if it were supposed that someone had done so,

---

## Page 16054

the Muslims would not have acquiesced in his doing so; for that would mean acquiescing in one of the gravest wrongs, and the Muslim community is protected from unanimously agreeing upon such a thing.

It has been reported from a group—including Īsā ibn Abān and other proponents of speculative theology and juristic reasoning among the Mu‘tazilah and the followers of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik—that consensus may abrogate the texts of the Quran and Sunnah. We used to interpret their statements as meaning that consensus indicates the existence of an abrogating scriptural text. But we then found someone reporting that they regarded consensus itself as the abrogating authority. If that is what they meant, this position permits Muslims to alter their religion after their Prophet, just as Christians claim that the Messiah authorized their religious scholars to prohibit whatever they considered beneficial to prohibit, and to permit whatever they considered beneficial to permit. This is not the religion of the Muslims, nor did the Companions consider themselves entitled to do such a thing. Anyone who believes that the Companions regarded this as lawful must be called upon to repent, as must others who hold similar views. A judge or mufti may, however, exercise independent legal judgment: if he is correct, he receives two rewards; if he errs, he receives one reward.

Whatever the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prescribed as “legislation contingent upon a particular cause” is prescribed only when that cause exists. An example is giving to those whose hearts are to be reconciled, which is established by the Quran and Sunnah. Some people thought this had been abrogated because of the report that ‘Umar stated that Allah had made it unnecessary to win people over: whoever wished could believe, and whoever wished could disbelieve. This assumption is mistaken. Rather, in his time ‘Umar had no need to give to those whose hearts were to be reconciled, so he ceased doing so because there was no need for it, not because it had been abrogated. This is comparable to supposing that, at certain times, there were no wayfarers, debtors, or others in similar categories.

---

## Page 16055

And as for “combining ‘umrah with Hajj,” it is reported that ‘Umar prohibited it. His son, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, and others, however, used to say that he had not forbidden it; rather, he intended to direct people to what was better: setting out from their own homes to perform ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj. The imams agree that this ‘umrah is better than the ‘umrah performed in tamattu‘ (performing ‘umrah and then Hajj under separate consecrations) or qirān (combining them under a single consecration). Indeed, Abū Hanīfah’s position—and Ahmad’s explicitly stated view—is that performing ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj and then performing Hajj alone during its months is better than merely performing tamattu‘ or qirān, although both hold these to be better than ifrād (performing Hajj alone) without a separate ‘umrah.

Others held that ‘Umar’s prohibition concerned converting Hajj into ‘umrah. They maintained that this was forbidden and impermissible, and that the Prophet’s instruction to his Companions to make this conversion—peace and blessings be upon him—applied exclusively to them. This is the view of many jurists, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and al-Shāfi‘ī.

Others among the earlier and later generations took the opposite position, saying that this conversion is obligatory, and that no one may perform Hajj except as tamattu‘, whether intending it from the outset or converting to it, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed his Companions during the Farewell Pilgrimage. This is the view of Ibn ‘Abbās, his companions, and those among the Zāhirīs and Shī‘ah who followed him.

The “third position” is that this conversion is permissible and preferable, but that not making the conversion is also permissible. This is the view of many among the earlier and later generations, including Ahmad ibn Hanbal and other hadith-oriented jurists. The only way a person can perform Hajj in a manner whose permissibility is unanimously accepted is to undertake tamattu‘ from the outset, without converting to it. As for Hajj performed as ifrād or qirān, there is well-known disagreement about it among the earlier and later generations, just as they disagreed over the permissibility of fasting while travelling and of performing the prayers in full while travelling, though they did not dispute the permissibility of fasting and shortening the prayers in general.

---

## Page 16056

When ‘Umar prohibited combining ‘umrah with Hajj, other Companions disagreed with him, including ‘Imrān ibn Husayn, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās, and others. This was unlike his prohibition of temporary marriage, in which ‘Alī and the rest of the Companions agreed with him. ‘Alī reproached Ibn ‘Abbās for permitting temporary marriage, saying:

“You have gone astray! The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited temporary marriage and the meat of domestic donkeys in the year of Khaybar.”

Thus, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib reproached Ibn ‘Abbās for permitting domestic donkey meat and temporary marriage, since Ibn ‘Abbās permitted both. ‘Alī objected to this and told him “that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited temporary marriage and domestic donkeys.” It was domestic donkeys that were prohibited on the day of Khaybar. Temporary marriage, however, was prohibited in the year of the conquest of Mecca, as established in the authentic hadith collection. Some people thought that it was prohibited, then permitted, then prohibited again; some thought that this happened three times. That is not the case.

‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“People have rushed into a matter in which they had room for deliberation. Why not make it binding upon them?”

He then made it binding upon them. This shows that people had introduced conduct for which, in his view, they deserved to have the three divorce pronouncements enforced against them. This, then, would either be like prohibiting tamattu‘ through converting Hajj into ‘umrah on the grounds that this had been exclusive to the Companions. That explanation, however, is invalid, for this was practiced during Abū Bakr’s time, and because he mentioned nothing establishing that it was exclusive to the Companions. This also invalidates the claim of those who thought that it had been abrogated, just as temporary marriage had been abrogated. Even if it is supposed that ‘Umar considered this binding, it was his own independent judgment, which he exercised in prohibiting the conversion of Hajj into ‘umrah because he thought that it had been a special dispensation.

---

## Page 16057

and this is a less well-supported opinion, rejected by several Companions; the second argument supports those who rejected it. The same applies to enforcing the three divorce pronouncements. Anyone who regards ‘Umar’s position on this as binding religious law should be told:

This was his own independent judgment, which other Companions disputed. When they disagree over something, the matter must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger. The evidence supports those who rejected this less well-supported opinion.

Alternatively, ‘Umar may have imposed this as a punishment to be applied when needed. This is the more likely of the two explanations of his action. Such a punishment is itself subject to independent judgment in “two respects.”

First, is this form of punishment prescribed or not? A ruler may consider it appropriate to impose a punishment that another does not consider appropriate. Such was ‘Alī’s burning of the heretics, which Ibn ‘Abbās condemned. The majority of jurists agree with Ibn ‘Abbās.

Second, punishment applies only to those who deserve it. Someone who fears Allah deserves that Allah grant him relief and a way out; he does not deserve punishment. If someone did not know that combining three divorce pronouncements was forbidden, then repented upon learning that it was forbidden, resolving from that day onward to divorce only in accordance with the Sunnah, he is among those who fear Allah in matters of divorce. There is no justification for enforcing all three pronouncements against such a person; rather, only one is to be counted against him.

These are weighty questions. We have discussed them at length elsewhere in two volumes; here we have merely drawn brief attention to them.

The statements of the Companions should be understood in one of two ways:

Either they regarded this as a discretionary punishment that may be imposed according to need, such as imposing more than forty lashes for drinking wine.

---

## Page 16058

or their independent judgments differed, so they regarded it as binding at times and not binding at others.

As for the view that counting all three divorces is a binding religious rule like other religious prescriptions, no evidence from the revealed law supports it. According to the preferred view, the man who pronounced this divorce may count it as a single divorce and take his wife back. Yet nothing is binding, since she was menstruating, provided he fears Allah and has repented of this innovation.

Section

As for “divorce during menstruation,” the disagreement over whether it takes effect stems from the following report:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, when he informed him that ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar had divorced his wife while she was menstruating: ‘Tell him to take her back until she menstruates, then becomes pure, then menstruates again, then becomes pure.’”

Some scholars understood his words, “Let him take her back,” to mean taking back a divorced wife. On this basis, they held that a man who divorces his wife during menstruation is instructed to take her back, although the divorce takes effect. Is this instruction a recommendation or an obligation? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad. The position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī is that it is recommended; Mālik’s position is that it is obligatory.

May he divorce her during the first period of purity following the menstruation in which he divorced her, or must he wait until the period of purity following a second menstruation? Here too there are two views, both reported from Ahmad and both held within Abū Hanīfa’s school.

Must he have intercourse with her before the second divorce? Most of these scholars do not consider this obligatory. Some, however, do; this is one view within Ahmad’s school. It is strong by analogy with the position of those who consider the divorce effective, but weak in terms of the evidence.

---

## Page 16059

They also disagreed over the reason for prohibiting divorce during menstruation. Is it because it prolongs the waiting period, as the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī and most of Ahmad’s followers maintain? Or is it because this is a time when the husband has little desire for intercourse with her, so that he may divorce her only at a time when he desires intercourse, since divorce is prohibited and permitted only when there is a need—as the followers of Abū Hanīfa and Abū al-Khattāb among Ahmad’s followers maintain? Or is it a devotional requirement whose rationale cannot be discerned, as some Mālikī scholars maintain? These are the three views.

Some scholars have said that his words, “Tell him to take her back,” do not necessarily mean that the divorce took effect. Rather, when he divorced her in a forbidden manner, he turned away from her and kept apart from her because he thought the divorce had taken effect. He was therefore instructed to restore her to her former situation. Similarly, in the authentic hadith, he said to someone who had sold one sāʿ-measure for two: “This is usury, so return it.”

An authentic report from ʿImrān ibn Husayn relates: “A man emancipated six slaves. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, divided them into three groups, freed two, and returned four to slavery.”

The Sunan collections also relate from Ibn ʿAbbās: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, returned Zaynab to her husband Abū al-ʿĀs under their original marriage contract.” This, too, was a case of returning her.

“He instructed ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib to return the slave boy whom he had sold separately from his brother.”

“He instructed Bashīr to take back the slave boy whom he had given to his son.”

There are many similar examples. The term “murājaʿa” (returning) indicates a return to the former state. This may take place through a new marriage contract, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“If he divorces her, there is no blame on either of them if they return to each other.”

It may also take place through each spouse physically returning to the other, even though no divorce has occurred, as when

---

## Page 16060

he sends his wife or slave woman out of his house and is told, “Return to her,” meaning, “Bring her back.” Similarly, in the hadith of ʿAlī, it says, “when he resumed commanding what was right.” And in ʿUmar’s letter to Abū Mūsā: “And return to the truth, for the truth is ancient.”

The term murājaʿa implies reciprocal action. Revoking a divorce, however, is something the husband can do independently, merely by his words, so the term murājaʿa is hardly ever used for it. This differs from his bringing the woman physically back to him and her returning voluntarily: the two have then returned to one another, just as they return to one another through a marriage contract by mutual choice after she has married another husband.

The terms used for revoking divorce are radd (“returning”) and imsāk (“keeping”). They are also used for maintaining an existing marriage, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“And when you said to the one whom Allah had favored and you had favored, ‘Keep your wife.’”

No divorce had occurred in that case. Allah Most High also says:

“Divorce is twice; then either retain her on fair terms or release her with kindness.”

Here, retaining her means taking her back after divorce. The husband can revoke the divorce independently, and he is instructed to have this witnessed. Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not instruct Ibn ʿUmar to have witnesses. He said, “Tell him to take her back,” using falyurājiʿhā, and did not say liyartajiʿhā.

Furthermore, had the divorce taken effect, taking her back in order to divorce her during the first or second period of purity would have imposed an additional burden and harm on her and increased the number of disliked divorces. There would have been no benefit in this for either of them. Rather, if the divorce had taken effect, his taking her back in order to divorce her a second time would have caused additional harm. He did not forbid him to divorce her; rather, he permitted him to do so at the beginning of

---

## Page 16061

the period of purity, even though he wished to divorce her. It is therefore clear that he merely instructed him to keep her with him and postpone the divorce until a time when it was permissible, just as someone who does something prematurely is instructed to undo it and, if he wishes, do it at its proper time. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever performs an act that is not in accordance with our command, it is rejected.”

A prohibited divorce is not in accordance with the command of Allah and His Messenger, so it is rejected. He instructed him to postpone the divorce until the second period of purity so that he could have intercourse during the first. Had he divorced her during that first period, he could only have done so before intercourse. Thus, ordering him to keep her until then, only for him to divorce her during the first period of purity, would merely have caused her additional harm.

Moreover, this was a punishment requiring him to act in a manner Allah had permitted; he was thus punished by being subjected to the opposite of what he intended. A detailed discussion of this issue and a full account of the arguments of both sides belong elsewhere. The purpose here is simply to draw attention to these positions and their grounds.

There is no doubt that the presumption is that the marriage remains intact. No evidence from Islamic law establishes its dissolution through a prohibited divorce; rather, the scriptural texts and legal principles indicate the contrary. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16062

Chapter: Divorce Pronounced by an Intoxicated Person and Others in a Similar State

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning “an intoxicated man who has lost his reason”: if he swears an oath of divorce, is he held liable for breaking it or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The scholars hold “two views” on this issue. The sounder view is that his divorce does not take effect. Thus, an intoxicated person’s oath is not binding, nor does his pronouncement of divorce take effect. This position is established from the Commander of the Faithful, Uthmān ibn Affān. To my knowledge, no contrary position has been established from any of the Companions.

It is the view of many earlier and later scholars, including Umar ibn Abd al-Azīz and others. It is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, adopted by a group of his followers. It is al-Shāfiʿī’s earlier position, which a group of his followers also adopted, and the view of a group of Abū Hanīfah’s followers, such as al-Tahāwī. Other scholars likewise held this position.

This view is correct, for it is established in an authentic report that:

“When Māʿiz ibn Mālik came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and confessed to having committed unlawful sexual intercourse, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered them to smell his breath.”

This was to determine whether he was intoxicated. Had he been intoxicated, his confession would not have been valid. If his confession was not valid, this establishes that his statements have no legal effect, like those of an insane person; and because

---

## Page 16063

an intoxicated person, though sinful for drinking, does not know what he is saying. If he does not know what he is saying, he cannot have a valid intention, and “Actions are judged only by intentions.” His case is like that of someone who consumes something forbidden that renders him insane: although his insanity resulted from a sin, neither his divorce nor his other statements have legal effect.

Anyone who reflects on the principles and objectives of the Sharia will see that this view is correct, and that the view that an intoxicated person’s divorce takes effect has no sound evidence to support it. Hence many discerning scholars of the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, such as Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī and Abū al-Maʿālī al-Juwaynī, apply these legal rulings to someone affected by drink. As for someone known not to understand what he is saying, his divorce unquestionably does not take effect.

The correct view is that divorce takes effect only when pronounced by someone who knows what he is saying. Just as his prayer is invalid in this state, his divorce does not take effect either. Allah has said:

“Do not approach prayer while you are intoxicated until you know what you are saying.”

And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked about “the conduct of an intoxicated person.”

Scholars, both earlier and later, have disagreed on this matter. There is disagreement within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere, and in many of his answers concerning it, Ahmad refrained from giving a definite ruling.

The views found within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere are: that his conduct is legally valid without qualification, encompassing both his statements and his actions; that it is all invalid; that a distinction is to be drawn between his statements and his actions; that a distinction is to be drawn between prescribed punishments and other matters; and that a distinction is to be drawn

---

## Page 16064

between what is in his favour and what counts against him; and between acts he can perform unilaterally and those he cannot. This disagreement exists within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

They also disagreed about someone who loses his reason through something other than intoxication, such as banj (a stupefying drug): should he be treated like an intoxicated person or an insane person? There are two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. Each group among Ahmad’s followers relies on some statement of his concerning this. These are not one reported view from him and one view inferred by his followers; rather, they are two reported views, each resting on an interpretation. They also disagreed over whether someone compelled to drink wine incurs sin by doing so, with two opinions within the school.

Some of Ahmad’s followers, such as al-Khallāl, uphold the view that an intoxicated person’s divorce does not take effect. Others, such as al-Qādī, uphold the view that it does. Those who hold that his divorce takes effect rely on three grounds.

The first is that this serves as a punishment for him. Someone adopting this argument may distinguish between prescribed punishments and other matters. This is weak, however, for the Sharia has never punished anyone in this manner, by making a divorce take effect or preventing it from taking effect. Moreover, this causes impermissible harm to his innocent wife and others, for no one may be punished for another person’s sin. Furthermore, the punishment for an intoxicated person is what the Sharia has prescribed, such as flogging; punishing him otherwise alters the punishments prescribed by the Sharia. The Companions punished him only for what intoxication was likely to lead to, namely incoherent speech and slander

---

## Page 16065

in speech, on the grounds that “When he becomes intoxicated, he speaks incoherently; when he speaks incoherently, he slanders; and the prescribed punishment for a slanderer is eighty lashes.” This makes clear that his deliberately becoming intoxicated—a condition likely to lead to slander—places him on the same footing as someone who deliberately commits slander. Here, the circumstance in which the rationale for the ruling is likely to be present is treated as equivalent to its actual presence, because that rationale is hidden: it may not be known whether he has committed slander, when he does so, or whom he slanders. Similarly, someone lying down may enter a state of ritual impurity without knowing whether he has done so; sleep therefore stands in place of actual ritual impurity.

This is a well-known principle of jurisprudence. If his legal acts belonged to this category, his wife would have to be divorced whether he pronounced divorce or not, just as he receives the prescribed punishment for slander whether he commits slander or not. Yet no one holds this view.

“The second ground”

His loss of reason can be known only through his own statement, yet his drinking makes him an evildoer, so his statement concerning his loss of reason and intoxication is not accepted. What this position actually entails is that the divorce does not take effect in reality, but that, in outward adjudication, his claim that grounds exist to invalidate it is not accepted. Someone adopting this position may distinguish acts he can perform unilaterally from others.

“The third ground”

This is the ground explicitly stated by the imams al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad: he remains subject to religious obligations, unlike an insane person, who is exempt from accountability, or someone asleep. This is because the insane person is exempt from accountability, whereas the intoxicated person is subject to punishment, as the Companions stated. There is no stronger ground than this.

Ahmad likewise said: “Nothing better has been said on this matter.”

This too, however, is weak. If what is meant is that he is subject to commands and prohibitions while intoxicated, that is false; for anyone who

---

## Page 16066

lacks reason and cannot understand what is said to him is not known, on any basis in revelation or otherwise, to be subject to commands and prohibitions. Indeed, the evidence of revelation and reason rules out addressing such a person in this way.

If, however, what is meant is that he may be held accountable for what he does while intoxicated, this is generally correct. But this is because, while sober, he was commanded not to drink wine, which leads to those offenses. If he does what is prohibited, he is not excused for the unlawful acts he commits. As I have said concerning intoxication arising from inward states: if the cause of intoxication is forbidden, the intoxicated person is not excused. What I have said may imply that, regarding prescribed punishments, he is treated like a sober person; this is a plausible view.

My discussion, however, concerns only the validity or invalidity of his legal acts. As for Allah’s words,

“Do not approach prayer while you are intoxicated,”

this prohibits them from becoming so intoxicated that they miss the prayer, or prohibits them from drinking shortly before prayer, or addresses those beginning to feel the effects of intoxication. While actually intoxicated, however, a person is in no way subject to commands and prohibitions.

There are several grounds establishing that his legal acts are invalid:

“The first”

The hadith of Jābir ibn Samura in Sahīh Muslim, in which “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, ordered that the breath of Māʿiz ibn Mālik be smelled.”

“The second”

His acts of worship, such as prayer, are invalid according to the revealed text and scholarly consensus. Allah has forbidden approaching prayer while intoxicated until one knows what one is saying, and there is agreement on this. This differs from someone who has drunk but is not intoxicated: his worship is valid if its conditions are fulfilled. It is well known that the intoxicated person’s prayer is invalid only because he does not know what he is saying, as the Quran indicates.

We therefore say: if invalidity applies to a person’s

---

## Page 16067

acts of worship because he lacks reason, then his contracts are all the more clearly invalid, as with someone asleep, someone insane, and others like them. Indeed, a person’s acts of worship may be valid even though his legal acts are invalid because of his deficient reason, as with a child or someone placed under legal restriction for prodigality.

“The third”

All utterances and contracts depend upon the presence of discernment and reason. The words of someone who has neither discernment nor reason carry no weight whatsoever in Islamic law. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Indeed, in the body there is a morsel of flesh: when it is sound, the rest of the body is sound, and when it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt. Truly, it is the heart.”

If the heart has lost the reason by which a person speaks and acts, how can legal effect be given to his commands and prohibitions, or to his establishment or termination of ownership? This is known through reason and affirmed by the Lawgiver.

“And the fourth”

Contracts and other legal acts depend upon intentions. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Actions are only according to intentions.”

I have established this principle in Bayān al-Dalīl ʿalā Butlān al-Tahlīl (Exposition of the Evidence for the Invalidity of Tahlīl). I have also established that any utterance made without the speaker intending it—because of inadvertence, a slip of the tongue, or lack of reason—has no legal consequence. As for someone who intends the words but not their meaning, such as someone speaking in jest, this requires further distinctions.

What is meant here by “intention” is rational intention, which is specific to the faculty of reason. As for the animal intention possessed by every animal, this is indispensable to the occurrence of voluntary words and deeds. Yet this alone is insufficient for contracts and utterances to be valid, for the insane, children, and others possess

---

## Page 16068

this intention, just as animals do. Nevertheless, without discernment, their sounds and utterances have no legal effect. A discerning child, however, and an insane person who sometimes possesses discernment have their words given legal weight when they possess discernment.

“The fifth”

This falls within declaratory and informative rulings, not prescriptive rulings. Whether an intoxicated person is liable to punishment has no bearing on the validity or invalidity of his contracts. Contracts are neither acts of worship for which one is rewarded nor offences for which one is punished. Rather, they are legal acts undertaken by the righteous and the wicked, believers and disbelievers alike, and are an indispensable part of human life. Human welfare cannot be secured without agreements and their fulfilment, because people need one another to obtain benefits and avert harm. Such agreements can proceed only from reason. Someone who possesses neither reason nor discernment has therefore neither made an agreement, sworn an oath, sold anything, contracted a marriage, pronounced a divorce, nor freed a slave.

This is clarified by the well-established agreement that, before wine was prohibited, an intoxicated person’s words had no legal effect. Thus, when Hamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muttalib, may Allah be pleased with him, said while intoxicated before the prohibition:

“Are you anything but slaves of my father?”

he was not held accountable for those words. Likewise, when one of the earliest Emigrants muddled his recitation of the surah “Say: O disbelievers” before the prohibition, he was not reproached. Similarly, if disbelievers were to drink wine, make agreements, and stipulate conditions, these acts of theirs would, by consensus, be given no legal weight. The same applies to someone whose intoxication is not punishable—for example, someone who drinks something without knowing that it will intoxicate him, or the like. As for someone who becomes intoxicated by drinking something forbidden, there is no doubt that he incurs sin

---

## Page 16069

by doing so and deserves whatever punishment Allah Most High has prescribed in this world and the Hereafter.

This distinction between him and someone whose intoxication is excusable is established. As for whether an agreement he makes with other people is legally binding, produces its legal effects, and achieves its intended purpose, there is no distinction here between excusable and inexcusable intoxication. What makes such an agreement valid is that the person entered into it while possessing reason and discernment, not whether he is righteous or wicked. Islamic law has never treated an intoxicated person as equivalent to a sober person.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had such a fierce quarrel with his wife that his reason was affected, and he said to her, “You are divorced three times.” Does this make the divorce binding or not?

He answered:

If matters reached the point where he did not understand what he was saying—as with an insane person—no divorce took effect. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man became angry and said, “Divorced,” without mentioning his wife or her name.

He answered:

If he did not intend thereby to divorce her, no divorce took effect through this wording.

---

## Page 16070

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who had been compelled to divorce his wife.

He answered:

If he was wrongfully compelled to divorce her, the divorce does not take effect according to the great majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. This is also the position transmitted from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—peace and blessings be upon him—such as ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb and others.

If, when he pronounced the divorce, he was surrounded by people known to be hostile towards him or to beat him, and he was unable at that time to defend himself against them, his claim that they compelled him to divorce is accepted.

If the witnesses to the divorce testify to those circumstances and he claims coercion, his statement is accepted. There is disagreement over whether he must be made to swear an oath.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who had been seized, beaten, imprisoned, and forced to divorce his wife. He pronounced a single divorce, and she left while pregnant by him.

---

## Page 16071

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. This divorce does not take effect. As for her marrying while pregnant by her first husband, that marriage is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims, even if the divorce had taken effect—how much more so when it had not. Whoever compelled him to divorce her, and whoever conducted this forbidden and invalid marriage, must receive discretionary punishment. The couple must be separated until she completes her waiting period from the first husband by giving birth. There is disagreement concerning a waiting period from the second man. If he knew that the marriage was forbidden, the correct view is that such a waiting period is necessary. If, however, he believed the marriage to be valid, she must observe a waiting period following intercourse with the second man.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man said, “I do not want you. Get up and go to your family. I am going to divorce you,” intending divorce by these words. Is it lawful for him to take her back and marry her with a new dowry? Please give us a ruling.

He answered:

A promise to divorce does not effect a divorce, however often it is expressed. Fulfilling such a promise is neither obligatory nor recommended.

But if he actually effected a divorce before saying, “Go to your mother’s house,” and meant to state that he was divorcing her, rather than that he would divorce her in the future, then this counts as a single divorce, provided he did not intend more. He may take her back during the waiting period without her consent, a marriage guardian, or a dowry. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16072

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A married man has children, but his mother dislikes his wife and urges him to divorce her. Is it permissible for him to divorce her?

He answered:

It is not lawful for him to divorce her merely because his mother tells him to. Rather, he must treat his mother dutifully, but divorcing his wife is not part of that duty. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman and her husband are on good terms, but her mother wants them to separate. The daughter has refused to comply. Is she sinful if her mother prays against her?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Once a woman is married, she is not obliged to obey her father or mother in leaving her husband or visiting them, nor may she obey them in such matters. Rather, provided her husband does not command her to disobey Allah, her duty to obey him takes precedence over her duty to obey her parents.

“Any woman who dies while her husband is pleased with her will enter Paradise.”

If the mother seeks to separate her from her husband, then she is

---

## Page 16073

of the same kind as Hārūt and Mārūt. She must not be obeyed in this, even if she prays against her daughter—unless, of course, the couple are acting together in disobedience, or the husband commands the daughter to disobey Allah while her mother commands her to obey Allah and His Messenger, as every Muslim is obliged to do.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man intended to divorce his wife when she menstruated, but did not pronounce a divorce. When she menstruated, he believed that she had become divorced merely through his intention, so he told the witnesses, “I divorced my wife.”

They asked, “When did you divorce her?”

He replied, “The day before yesterday,” on the basis of that belief.

After two menstrual periods had passed, apart from the period during which he thought she had been divorced, the witnesses married her to another man. She stayed with him, and then he divorced her. After she completed her waiting period, her first husband wanted to take her back. Is she lawful for him under the original marriage contract, or is a new contract required?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he merely intended to divorce her when she menstruated, no divorce takes effect through that, by agreement of the scholars. Rather, he must actually pronounce the divorce afterward; if he does not, no divorce takes effect.

If he believed that this intention constituted a divorce and therefore acknowledged that he had divorced her through that intention, no divorce takes effect in reality through this acknowledgment, although he is held to it in judicial proceedings. If no divorce has taken effect thereby, she remains his wife in reality. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16074

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had a wife whom his mother did not want him to remain with, so he divorced her. He then said, “Any woman I marry from this city within the walls”—neither his wife nor any other woman. If he takes his wife back or marries another woman from the city, will the marriage contract be valid?

He answered:

Yes, he may marry a woman from that city if he wishes, or from elsewhere if he wishes, and the marriage contract will be valid.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man quarrelled with his wife and intended to say, “She is divorced once,” but his tongue slipped and he said “three times,” though that was not his intention. What is the ruling?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If his tongue slipped and he said “three” unintentionally, when he intended only one, then only one divorce takes effect. Indeed, even if he intended to say “pure” but his tongue slipped and he said “divorced,” no divorce takes effect as a matter between him and Allah. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16075

He was asked:

A woman lent her husband money, then said to him, “I am afraid you will not repay me.” He told her, “If I do not repay you by the end of this Ramadan, you are divorced three times.” The husband is away in Qūs and has appointed no one to act on his behalf. If the woman releases her husband from the debt and the month passes, does the divorce take effect or not? If someone voluntarily pays the debt on his behalf, is the debt discharged without the divorce taking effect when the month passes, or does it take effect?

He answered:

If she releases him from the debt, he does not break his oath according to many jurists, including Abū Hanīfa and Muhammad, and according to one opinion within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. This is for two reasons:

First: releasing him from the debt precludes repayment, making its payment impossible.

Second: an act that one swears to perform is comparable to an act that one is commanded to perform. It is known that a person is commanded to repay a debt only so long as it remains outstanding. The same applies to oaths and customary usage; the two cases are analogous. Ordinarily, someone who swears such an oath intends to free himself of liability, put an end to the creditor’s demands against him, and repay the creditor if the debt remains outstanding.

Likewise, if someone pays the debt on his behalf, his liability for it is discharged without any action on his part, just as it is discharged when the debt is remitted. Repayment on his part becomes impossible, and the creditor’s purpose has been fulfilled. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—treated payment on a debtor’s behalf as equivalent to the debtor’s own payment when he said:

“Tell me, if your father had…”

And in another hadith:

“Your mother had a debt, and you repaid it on her behalf, would that discharge the obligation?” She said, “Yes.” He said, “Allah has a greater right to be paid what is due.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16076

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man pronounced a threefold divorce against his wife before consummating the marriage, while she was still a virgin. Is there any way for him to take her back?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. According to the four imams, a threefold divorce establishes the same prohibition whether pronounced before or after consummation.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man contracted a marriage on the understanding that the woman had reached puberty. He neither consummated the marriage nor had intercourse with her, then pronounced a threefold divorce against her. Another man then contracted a marriage with her, but he too neither consummated the marriage nor had intercourse with her, then pronounced a threefold divorce against her. Is it permissible for the first man who divorced her to marry her again?

The answer

He answered:

According to the four imams, divorcing her before consummation is the same as divorcing her after consummation: she is not lawful for him until she marries another husband and that husband consummates the marriage with her. If the latter divorces her before consummation, she does not become lawful for the first husband.

---

## Page 16077

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man said, “Everything I own is forbidden to me.” Do his wife and his female slave thereby become forbidden to him?

He answered:

Regarding anything other than his wife, he must offer expiation for breaking an oath. Regarding his wife, the scholars disagree: does this constitute divorce, or must he offer the expiation for zihār? Mālik’s position is that it constitutes divorce. According to Abū Hanīfa, and according to the stronger of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two opinions, he must offer expiation for breaking an oath. Ahmad’s position is that he must offer the expiation for zihār, unless he intends something else, in which case there is disagreement. The correct view is that no divorce takes effect through this statement.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man quarreled with his wife and struck her. She said to him, “Divorce me.” He replied, “You are forbidden to me.” Does she thereby become forbidden to him?

He answered:

Regarding his statement, “You are forbidden to me,” the scholars hold two views. One is that he must offer the expiation for zihār if she allows him intimacy with her. The other is that nothing is required of him. The scholars do not disagree that she is obliged to allow him intimacy with her. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16078

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had a wife with whom he had sons and daughters, and he married another woman. He then drew up a written authorization for his new wife, saying, “Whenever I take back the mother of my children, the power to divorce her will be in your hands,” and authorized her to divorce the first wife for a period of ten years. He has since divorced the wife who held this authorization. Is the authorization valid? If it is valid, does divorcing the appointed agent revoke it?

He—may Allah have mercy on him—answered:

Praise be to Allah. Some may suppose that the authorization in this case remains in force, reasoning that if a husband appoints his wife as his agent in a sale or a similar transaction and then divorces her three times, the divorce does not revoke her authorization, as the jurists have stated. But these are not the same case. The correct view in the case presented is that the divorce revokes the authorization. Here, he was not seeking to divorce the first wife and appointing someone else to act on his behalf, as someone wishing to sell his possessions appoints an agent. Rather, his purpose was to give this wife herself the power to divorce the other, placing the other wife’s marital status in her hands: if she wished, the other would be divorced; if she wished otherwise, she would not divorce her. He stipulated for her that the other wife’s marital status would be in her hands, so that the other could remain his wife only with her consent.

The intended meaning is: “I will not marry her without your consent.” This means: “I will not keep both you and her as wives,” because a woman dislikes having a co-wife, which may interfere with the rights conferred by the marriage contract, such as the allocation of time and the like. Once he has divorced her three times, however, she no longer has any right to a share of his time

---

## Page 16079

or similar rights. The other woman therefore neither competes with her for these rights nor remains her co-wife, and her consent is not required for him to marry that woman. Ordinarily, a man seeks to please a woman by giving up another wife for her only while she remains his wife. Once their marriage has been irrevocably dissolved, he no longer seeks to please her. How much more so when he has divorced her three times—the utmost way of displeasing her! How could someone who has displeased her in this way intend to please her in a lesser matter? Such considerations, together with people’s customary practice, show that he placed the other wife’s marital status in her hands only for as long as the woman entrusted with this power remained his wife. Once she is no longer his wife, she has no authority over the other woman’s marital status.

All this assumes that the condition is regarded as binding. If it is not binding, it is as though he had simply said to her, without any prior stipulation, “The power to divorce yourself is in your hands,” or, “The power to divorce so-and-so is in your hands.” He may revoke this authorization. In the case presented, however, this was stipulated in the marriage contract. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The conditions you are most bound to fulfill are those by which marital intimacy becomes lawful for you.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both recorded this in their Sahīh collections. Accordingly, groups of earlier and later scholars—including ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, Hammād ibn Zayd, Tāwūs, al-Awzā‘ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others—held that if a husband undertakes, as a condition of marriage, not to marry another woman alongside his wife, the condition is valid. If he then marries another woman, his wife has the option of ending her marriage. This is more far-reaching than stipulating that, should he marry another woman, the power to divorce that wife will be in her hands; yet her aim is the same. In both cases, she has this choice only for as long as she remains his wife.

---

## Page 16080

As for the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, they regard this condition as invalid and nonbinding. Accordingly, it is as though he had granted this authority without any prior stipulation. Agency is a revocable contract by scholarly consensus, so he may revoke it.

When the scholars differed over the case of a husband saying to his wife, “The power to divorce yourself is in your hands,” al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others said that this amounts to appointing her as his agent, and that he may revoke the authorization before she makes her choice. Abū Hanīfa and Mālik said that it amounts to conferring the power upon her, so he cannot take it away from her.

However, the case at hand falls under the position of Mālik, Ahmad, and others who hold that a husband may include in the marriage contract a stipulation by which his wife gains control of al-tarq if he marries another woman. Undoubtedly, she possesses this authority only while her marriage remains in force. Once he has irrevocably divorced her, she has no entitlement under that condition. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had an exchange with his wife while preparing to travel. He told his agent, “If she is satisfied with this customary maintenance, give her the maintenance. If she is not satisfied with the maintenance, hand her her document.”

After the husband departed, the agent handed her her document, pronounced one revocable divorce on the husband’s behalf, and sent word to him that he had pronounced one revocable divorce. When the husband learned of this, he could not bear it, so he called witnesses to attest that he had taken her back and sent for her. When the agent heard that he had taken his wife back, he stated that he had divorced her three times on the husband’s behalf. Is the man permitted to take his wife back after this statement by the agent?

---

## Page 16081

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah.

His instruction, “Hand her her document,” is an indirect expression for divorce. If the principal states that he intended divorce by it, or this is evident from the circumstances, the agent is authorized to pronounce one divorce, but not three, unless the principal authorizes him to do so.

If the principal tells the agent that he did not intend to authorize three divorces, his statement is accepted, and the agent is not permitted to pronounce three divorces. If the agent pronounces one divorce and the husband then takes her back, his taking her back is valid.

---

## Page 16082

Chapter: Oaths Involving Divorce and Other Matters

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a knowingly false oath involving divorce, and about a man who said to his wife, “Your family shall not enter my house.” This proved difficult for him, so he swore by triple divorce that he had not said it, knowing that he had.

He replied:

The oaths people swear are of two kinds: the first is the oaths of the Muslims; the second is the oaths of the polytheists.

The second category consists of swearing by created things, such as the Kaaba, the angels, shaykhs, kings, forefathers, the sword, and other things by which many people swear. These oaths have no sanctity; indeed, they are not binding, and no expiation is due from anyone who breaks them, by the consensus of the Muslims. Rather, whoever swears such an oath should affirm Allah’s oneness, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath, saying, ‘By al-Lāt and al-Uzzā,’ should say, ‘There is no god but Allah.’”

It is also established in the Sahīh that he said:

“Whoever swears an oath should swear by Allah or remain silent.”

The Sunan collections also report that he said:

“Whoever swears by other than Allah has associated partners with Him.”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated this and graded it authentic. These oaths, by agreement of the imams

---

## Page 16083

and most of them hold that the Prophet prohibited them. Indeed, Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ibn ‘Abbās, and others are reported to have said:

“I would rather swear by Allah falsely than swear by anyone other than Him truthfully.”

He said: This is because swearing by other than Allah is polytheism, and polytheism is graver than lying.

Making vows to created beings is graver than swearing by them. Whoever makes a vow to a created being, his vow is not binding, nor is he obliged to fulfil it, by the agreement of the scholars. This includes someone who makes a vow to a deceased prophet, shaykh, or anyone else—for example, to Shaykh Jākīr, Abū al-Wafā’, al-Muntazar, Lady Nafīsa, Shaykh Raslān, or others. The same applies to someone who vows to give oil, candles, coverings, or money—gold, dirhams, or anything else—to others besides these. All such vows are forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. Fulfilling them is neither obligatory nor even permissible, by the consensus of the Muslims.

A vow is to be fulfilled only when it is made to Allah, Almighty and Majestic, and involves obedience to Him. A vow is permissible only when it involves an act of worship, and Allah may be worshipped only in ways He has prescribed. Whoever makes a vow to other than Allah is guilty of polytheism graver than that involved in swearing by other than Allah; it is like prostrating to other than Allah. If someone vows to do something that is not an act of worship—for example, if a woman vows to fast during her menstrual period—that vow is not binding. Fasting during menstruation is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. As related in the Sahīh, ‘Ā’isha, may Allah be pleased with her, reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”
