If someone vows to travel to the grave of a prophet or a shaykh, to his shrine or a site associated with him, or to a mosque other than the three mosques, he is under no obligation to fulfil his vow, by the agreement of the imams.

---

## Page 16084

Likewise, if someone vows to pray, fast, give charity, undertake spiritual retreat, offer an Eid sacrifice or a sacrificial offering, or travel to the Prophet’s Mosque—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or to Jerusalem, the scholars hold two views, both of which are reported from al-Shāfi‘ī.

The first is that he is not obliged to fulfil it. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and those whose principle is that a vow makes an act obligatory only if religious law already imposes an obligation of the same kind, as with prayer, fasting, and spiritual retreat. Spiritual retreat thus becomes obligatory through a vow because fasting is required according to him, as well as according to Ahmad in one of the two reports from him, and according to Mālik. For this reason, he holds it to be obligatory. Visiting the mosque, however, is not obligatory under religious law, so according to him it does not become obligatory through a vow.

The second view is that a vow to visit the two mosques must be fulfilled. This is the position of Mālik and Ahmad, because doing so is an act of obedience to Allah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him.”

This applies if his intention is to travel to the mosque to pray there, undertake spiritual retreat, or perform similar acts. If, however, his intention is specifically to visit the Prophet’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and not to worship in his mosque, he is not to fulfil this vow. Mālik and other scholars explicitly stated this, and there is no disagreement among the imams concerning it, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both related it in their Sahīh collections.

---

## Page 16085

Whoever vows to travel to a place in order to venerate it, other than these three mosques—such as Mount Sinai, where Allah spoke to Moses son of ‘Imrān; the cave of Hirā’, where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to worship; or the cave of Thawr, concerning which Allah Most High said:

“one of two, when they were in the cave”—

must not fulfil that vow, by agreement of the imams. How much more, then, does this apply to other caves and caverns! The same applies if someone vows to travel to the grave of Allah’s intimate friend, peace be upon him, the grave of Abū Barīd, the grave of Ahmad ibn Hanbal, or the graves of those buried in al-Baqī‘. Visiting graves is prescribed for someone who is nearby and whose purpose is to supplicate for the deceased. Travelling to them, however, is prohibited.

As for swearing by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the majority of scholars hold that this too is prohibited, that it does not constitute a binding oath, and that no expiation is due for it. (This is the view of Mālik, Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. According to another report from Ahmad, such an oath is binding.)

Section

“The Second Type”

The oaths of Muslims. If someone swears by Allah’s name, such oaths are binding according to explicit scriptural texts and scholarly consensus, and expiation is due if he breaks them.

If he swears by an obligation he undertakes before Allah—as in oaths involving vows, zihār (declaring one’s wife to be like one’s mother), declarations of unlawfulness, divorce, or emancipation—for example, saying:

“If I do such-and-such, I must perform ten Hajj pilgrimages”; or, “Then my property is to be given in charity”; or, “I must fast for a month”; or, “Then my wives are divorced”; or, “My slaves are free”; or saying:

“What is lawful is forbidden to me; I shall not do such-and-such”; or

---

## Page 16086

“Divorce shall be binding upon me if I do such-and-such,” or “… unless I do such-and-such”; and, “If I do such-and-such, my wives are divorced,” or “my slaves are free,” and similar statements—these are the oaths of Muslims according to the Companions and the majority of scholars, and they are binding oaths.

One group, however, said that these fall under the category of swearing by created beings and therefore are not binding. The first view is more correct, and it is the position of the Companions. ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, and others used to prohibit the first type, whereas they would instruct anyone who swore an oath of the second type to offer expiation for his oath, without forbidding him to swear in this manner. This belongs to the same category as swearing by Allah and making vows to Allah. In Sahīh Muslim, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“The expiation for a vow is the expiation for an oath.”

Thus, if someone says, “I owe it to Allah to do such-and-such,” intending an oath, it is an oath—just as if he says, “I owe such-and-such to Allah,” or, “If I kill so-and-so, I must offer expiation.” This is the position of Ahmad and Abū Hanīfa, and it is also the position mentioned by the Khurāsānī scholars of the Shāfi‘ī school.

Among those who regard this as a binding oath, some hold the person to whatever he has undertaken. Thus, if he breaks his oath, they enforce the vow, divorce, emancipation, zihār, or declaration of unlawfulness. This is Mālik’s view and one of the two positions reported from Abū Hanīfa. Others distinguish divorce and emancipation from the remaining cases; this is the well-known position of al-Shāfi‘ī. Others distinguish vows from the remaining cases; this is the well-known position of Ahmad. Still others distinguish divorce from the remaining cases; this is the position of Abū Thawr.

The correct view is that all these oaths require expiation if they are broken. Breaking them does not make a vow, divorce, emancipation, or declaration of unlawfulness binding upon the person. This is the meaning of the Companions’ statements: explicit reports to this effect have been reliably transmitted from them concerning oaths involving emancipation and vows. Their reasoning and the general wording of their statements

---

## Page 16087

encompass oaths involving divorce. It has also been reliably reported from several of the early generations that an oath involving divorce does not make divorce binding, as reported from Tāwūs, ‘Ikrima, Abū Ja‘far, and Ja‘far ibn Muhammad. Some of them required expiation, which is the correct view, while others did not.

Scholars thus hold more than four views concerning oaths involving divorce. One view is that divorce is binding in all cases, as held by the four imams. Another is that it is not binding in any case, as held by Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Shāfi‘ī, Ibn Hazm, and others. A further view is that if the person intended an oath, divorce is not binding. This is the most correct view, and it is what the Companions meant by “an oath.”

There are two views on whether expiation is required. The more correct is that it is required if the oath concerns the future. If, however, the oath concerns the past or present, and the person intends to state a fact—not to urge or prevent an action—as when he says, “By Allah, I have indeed done such-and-such,” or “… I have not done it”; or, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I have indeed done such-and-such,” or “… I have not done it”; or, “What is lawful is forbidden to me: I have indeed done such-and-such”—then he either believes that he is telling the truth or knows that he is lying.

If he believes that he is telling the truth, there are three views. The first is that nothing is incumbent upon him in any of these oaths. This is the stronger of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two positions and the second position reported from Ahmad. Thus, whoever swears an oath involving divorce, emancipation, or anything else

---

## Page 16088

concerning something he believes to be true—as when he swears to something that later proves otherwise—incurs no obligation according to this view. This is the most correct of these views.

The second view is that all such oaths are treated like oaths concerning the future. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s second position and the second position reported from Ahmad. Accordingly, expiation is required for those oaths that admit of expiation.

The third view is that if his oath admits of expiation, as with an oath sworn by Allah’s name, nothing is incumbent upon him; rather, this falls under unintentional oaths. If, however, it does not admit of expiation, as with an oath involving divorce or emancipation, its stipulated consequence becomes binding upon him. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfa, and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

If the oath is knowingly false—that is, he swears falsely while knowing that he is lying—then, by agreement of the Muslims, he sins by taking it and must seek Allah’s forgiveness for it. It is a major sin, especially if his intention is to wrong someone else, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears a wicked oath by which he wrongfully takes a Muslim’s property will meet Allah while He is angry with him.”

If such an oath is of a kind that admits of expiation, then expiation is required according to al-Shāfi‘ī and one position reported from Ahmad. Most scholars, however, said that its gravity is too great to be expiated. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfa, and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

They said: Major sins carry no prescribed expiation, just as there is no prescribed expiation for theft, unlawful sexual intercourse, or drinking wine. Likewise, according to the majority of scholars, there is no prescribed expiation for intentional killing.

---

## Page 16089

And if he takes a knowingly false oath by committing himself to a consequence—as in the case raised by the questioner—such as saying, “What is lawful is forbidden to me: I did not do such-and-such,” or, “Divorce is binding upon me: I did not do such-and-such,” or, “If I have done such-and-such, my wealth is pledged in charity,” or, “I am bound to perform Hajj,” or, “My wives are divorced,” or, “My slaves are free,” then there are two views.

According to one view, these consequences become binding upon him if we hold that no expiation is prescribed for a knowingly false oath, even if we hold that such oaths admit of expiation when they concern the future. Otherwise, these oaths would entail neither expiation nor fulfillment of the commitment he undertook. This is the view preferred by my grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt. Likewise, Muhammad ibn Muqātil al-Rāzī said: “Whoever takes a knowingly false oath staking his faith upon its truth must make expiation.”

The second view is that this is like a knowingly false oath sworn by Allah: it is a major sin, but the vow, divorce, or prohibition of what is lawful to which he committed himself does not become binding. This is the more correct of the two views. According to this view, no vow, divorce, emancipation, or prohibition of what is lawful becomes binding upon someone who did not intend that consequence, whether the oath is binding, knowingly false, or unintentional. Divorce, emancipation, and vows become binding only upon someone who intends them.

Making something conditional is of two kinds. In one kind, the intention is that the stipulated consequence should take effect when the condition is fulfilled. Such a conditional commitment is binding. Thus, if someone makes a vow, divorce, or emancipation conditional in this manner, it becomes binding upon him.

For example, if he says to his wife, “When you have purified yourself after menstruation, you are divorced,” or, “When your pregnancy becomes apparent, you are divorced,” the divorce takes effect when the specified condition is fulfilled. The same applies if he makes it conditional upon the appearance of the new moon. Likewise, if he forbids her to do something and says, “If you do it, you are divorced,” intending to divorce her if she does it, then the divorce takes effect. The same applies to similar cases.

---

## Page 16090

unlike, for example, when he forbids her to commit an indecent act, a betrayal, or an injustice, saying, “If you do it, you are divorced.” Although he dislikes divorcing her, if she commits that wrongful act, divorcing her is preferable to him to remaining with her under such circumstances. In this case, the divorce takes effect. It is established that the Companions gave effect to conditional divorce when the husband intended it to take effect upon fulfillment of the condition, just as they held him bound by a vow. This differs from the case of someone who merely intends an oath.

Someone who intends an oath wants neither the condition to be fulfilled nor the stipulated consequence to occur, even if the condition is fulfilled. For example, he might say, “If I travel with you, my wives are divorced, my slaves are free, my wealth is pledged in charity, I am bound to perform Hajj ten times, and I disavow the religion of Islam,” or similar words. It is known with certainty that he does not intend these consequences to become binding upon him, even if the condition is fulfilled. Such a person is taking an oath. Thus, in all conditional formulations, a distinction must be drawn between someone who intends the stipulated consequence to take effect and someone who intends an oath.

If a man divorces his wife with immediate effect, or makes the divorce conditional upon a circumstance in which he intends it to take effect, the divorce takes effect provided it is lawful: that is, he pronounces a single divorce during a period of purity in which he has not had intercourse with her, or while she is pregnant and her pregnancy has become evident.

As for unlawful divorce—such as divorcing her during menstruation, or during a period of purity after having intercourse with her but before her pregnancy becomes evident—there is disagreement concerning it. The more evident view is that it is not binding, just as an unlawful marriage and similar acts are not binding. Combining three divorces is unlawful according to the majority of scholars.

If he pronounces three divorces, do all three count, or only one? There are two views, the more evident being that only one counts. We have discussed these issues at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16091

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

If a man takes an oath invoking divorce, saying, “Divorce is binding upon me: I shall certainly do such-and-such,” or, “I shall not do it”; or, “Divorce is incumbent upon me: I shall certainly do it”; or, “If I do not do it, divorce will be binding upon me,” or, “incumbent upon me”—or uses similar expressions committing himself to divorce as part of his oath—and then breaks his oath, does divorce take effect?

There are two opinions among Muslim scholars of the four schools and other schools of Islamic scholarship.

The first is that divorce does not take effect. This is explicitly stated by Abū Hanīfa himself, and it is the opinion of a group of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers, such as al-Qaffāl and Abū Sa‘īd al-Mutawallī, the author of al-Tatimma (The Supplement). In these later times, a group of the followers of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī and others, both Sunnis and Shiites, issue fatwas and judicial rulings according to this opinion in the eastern lands, al-Jazīra, Iraq, Khurāsān, the Hijaz, Yemen and elsewhere.

It is also the opinion of Dāwūd and his followers—such as Ibn Hazm and others—who have continued to issue fatwas and judicial rulings in Persia, Iraq, Syria, Egypt and the Maghrib to this day. They are a vast multitude, including many judges and muftis. It is also the opinion of a group of the early Muslims, such as Tāwūs and others, and it is the basis on which fatwas are issued by many of

---

## Page 16092

the scholars of the Maghrib in these later times, both Mālikīs and others. Some shaykhs in Egypt also used to issue fatwas to this effect. This position is also supported by explicit statements transmitted from Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal and by the principles of his school in several places.

Even if a man “swears an oath invoking a threefold divorce,” saying, “Divorce is binding upon me three times: I shall certainly do such-and-such,” and then does not do it, a group of earlier and later scholars among the followers of Mālik, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Dāwūd and others have issued fatwas that three divorces do not take effect thereby. Some of them, however, hold that one divorce takes effect. This is reported from a group of the Companions, the Successors and others even concerning divorce pronounced with immediate effect, let alone divorce made conditional or invoked in an oath. This is also the position of those followers of Mālik, Ahmad and Dāwūd who followed them in this regard, whether the divorce is pronounced with immediate effect, made conditional or invoked in an oath. A group of prominent early scholars distinguished in this matter between women whose marriages had been consummated and those whose marriages had not.

Among those who do not regard divorce as taking effect when a man says, “Divorce is binding upon me: I shall certainly do such-and-such,” some hold that no divorce takes effect and do not require him to offer expiation, while others require expiation. Many scholars have issued fatwas according to each of these two opinions. The scholars’ positions on these questions, their exact statements, those who transmitted these positions from them, the books in which they are found, and the evidence supporting these opinions have been set out in detail elsewhere, in discussions amounting to several volumes.

---

## Page 16093

This differs from what I mentioned concerning the schools of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, namely, the case in which someone swears using a formula that makes divorce binding, such as, “Divorce is binding upon me,” or similar wording. The disagreement within these two schools applies whether the divorce is pronounced with immediate effect, made conditional, or invoked in an oath. There are three opinions within the two schools: is this explicit wording of divorce, an allusive expression, or neither explicit nor allusive, so that no divorce takes effect through it even if he intends divorce? Within Ahmad’s school there are two opinions: whether it is explicit or allusive.

As for swearing an oath invoking divorce, or making divorce conditional with the intention of swearing an oath, other scholars dispute this concerning formulas other than this one.

Thus, anyone who claims that a scholar issuing a fatwa that divorce does not take effect in such a case has opposed scholarly consensus and every position within the four schools has erred and pursued that of which he has no knowledge. Allah Most High says:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge.”

Rather, the four imams, their followers, and all other imams of comparable standing agree that if a judge rules that divorce does not take effect in such a case, his judgment may not be overturned. If someone qualified to issue fatwas gives this ruling, he is entitled to do so. By agreement of the four imams and other imams of the Muslims, neither he nor anyone who follows his ruling may be censured.

Even if someone were to judge or issue a fatwa according to a tenable opinion outside the positions of the four imams concerning oaths, divorce, or other matters over which disagreement among Muslim scholars is established, he would be entitled to do so, provided that the opinion contradicts neither the Quran nor the Sunnah nor their import, and that the judge or mufti supports it with evidence recognized by Islamic law, such as evidence from the Quran and Sunnah.

---

## Page 16094

And, by agreement of the four imams, his judgment, once issued, may not be overturned, nor may he be prevented from ruling or issuing fatwas on that basis, nor may anyone be prevented from following his opinion.

Anyone who says that such restrictions are permissible has opposed the consensus of the four imams—indeed, the consensus of the Muslims—as well as opposing Allah and His Messenger. For Allah Most High says in His Book:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is best and leads to the best outcome.”

Allah thus commands the believers to refer their disputes to Allah and the Messenger, which means referring them to the Quran and Sunnah.

Whoever says, “No one may refer disputed matters to the Quran and Sunnah; rather, Muslims must follow our opinion rather than the other,” without providing evidence recognized by Islamic law—such as evidence from the Quran and Sunnah—to establish the correctness of his opinion, has opposed the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the Muslims. Such a person must be called upon to repent and punished as others like him are punished.

If the issue is one disputed among Muslim scholars, and someone adheres to one of the two opinions, no argument based on evidence recognized by Islamic law—such as the Quran and Sunnah—has been brought against his opinion, and the proponent of the other opinion has no such evidence to refute it, then, by the consensus of the Muslims, the person who lacks proof establishing the correctness of his opinion has no right to restrain the one who argues on the basis of evidence recognized by Islamic law. Rather, he has allowed Muslims to be barred from holding the opinion that accords with the Quran and Sunnah, and required people to follow the opposing opinion without any evidence recognized by Islamic law that obliges them to follow this opinion and forbids them to follow the other. He has thus cast off religion; he must be called upon to repent and punished

---

## Page 16095

as others like him are punished. At most, he may be ignorant, in which case his ignorance is initially excused until the positions of the scholars and the evidence of the Quran and Sunnah have been made clear to him. If he then persists in opposing the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him, and follows a path other than that of the believers, he must be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death.

Every oath taken by Muslims other than an oath by Allah, Mighty and Majestic—such as oaths involving divorce, manumission, zihār (declaring one’s wife forbidden through comparison with a female relative permanently forbidden in marriage), declarations of unlawfulness, or undertakings to perform pilgrimage, walk, give charity, fast, and the like—is subject to a well-known disagreement among scholars. This applies whether someone uses an oath formula, saying, “A declaration of unlawfulness shall be binding upon me,” or “Manumission shall be binding upon me: I shall certainly do such-and-such”; or uses a manumission formula, saying, “If I do such-and-such, a declaration of unlawfulness shall be binding upon me and my wives shall be divorced,” or “My slaves shall be free,” or “My property shall be given in charity, and I shall be bound to walk to the House of Allah Most High.”

The four imams and all the other imams of the Muslims agree that, in all these cases, a judge may rule that someone who breaks his oath is not bound by what he stipulated: either nothing is required of him, or an expiation suffices. A mufti may likewise rule accordingly. From the time these forms of oath first arose until the present, there have always been Muslims who issue fatwas to this effect. Some prescribe expiation, while others rule that neither expiation nor fulfilment of what was stipulated is required. Others, in turn, rule that what was stipulated is binding. Each of these three positions has proponents within the Muslim community who issue fatwas accordingly concerning oaths involving divorce, manumission, declarations of unlawfulness, and vows.

As for swearing by created things, such as the Kaaba and the angels, no expiation is required for this, by the agreement of the Muslims.

---

## Page 16096

Thus, oaths fall into “three categories.” Oaths sworn by Allah require expiation, by agreement. Oaths sworn by created beings or things require no expiation, by agreement, except that there are “two views” in Ahmad’s school concerning swearing by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The majority hold that no expiation is required. Some of his followers have extended this ruling to all the prophets, but the overwhelming majority of scholars, both Ahmad’s followers and others, disagree with that.

As for binding oaths sworn by Allah Most High—namely, these oaths—Muslims hold “three positions” concerning them, even though some people have claimed consensus regarding certain forms. This resembles many disputed questions in which someone unaware of the disagreement claims consensus, meaning merely, “I know of no disagreement.” Someone who knows of the disagreement and establishes its existence makes an informed affirmation, which takes precedence over the denial of someone unaware of it, by the agreement of the Muslims.

If a question has been disputed among both earlier and later generations, and those who hold divorce or some other stipulated consequence to be binding on the oath-taker have no supporting text from the Quran or Sunnah, nor any consensus, then the position that it is not binding is permissible, by the agreement of the four imams and all the other imams of the Muslims. Indeed, they agree that no one may prevent a judge qualified to adjudicate from ruling accordingly, or a mufti qualified to issue fatwas from giving such a fatwa. They even permit fatwas and judgments based on weak positions because disagreement exists concerning them. How, then, could they prohibit a position such as this, supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound legal analogy, and demonstrably upheld by earlier and later generations?

Indeed, it is established that the Companions, who are the best of this community, issued fatwas concerning oaths involving manumission—which is more beloved to Allah Most High than divorce—to the effect that manumission is not binding on the oath-taker; rather, it suffices for him to offer

---

## Page 16097

expiation for an oath. What, then, would their position be regarding divorce, which is the most hateful of permissible things to Allah? Could it be supposed that the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, would say that someone who swears an oath involving acts of obedience beloved to Allah—such as prayer, fasting, charity, and pilgrimage—is not obliged to perform those acts, and that expiation for an oath suffices, yet say that something Allah does not love, but rather hates, becomes binding upon whoever swears an oath involving it?

The Muslims agree that swearing an oath involving disbelief or Islam does not thereby make someone a disbeliever or a Muslim. Thus, if someone says, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Jew,” and then does it, he does not become a Jew, by agreement. Whether he must offer expiation for an oath is subject to “two views.”

“The first” is that he must. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and the well-known position reported from Ahmad. “The second” is that he need not. This is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and one position reported from Ahmad. Some followers of Abū Hanīfa held that if someone believes that breaking his oath would make him a disbeliever and nevertheless swears such an oath, he becomes a disbeliever. They said, “Because he has chosen disbelief.”

The majority, however, said that he does not become a disbeliever, because his intention is to avoid falling into disbelief: he swore the oath precisely because of his hatred of it. Likewise, whoever swears an oath involving divorce or anything else intends only to avoid its becoming binding upon him, because of his intense aversion to it.

On this basis, the majority distinguished between a “vow of devotion” and a “vow made in obstinacy and anger.” They explained that, in the former, the person intends both the condition and its stipulated consequence to occur, unlike the latter. Thus, if he says, “If Allah heals

---

## Page 16098

my ailing loved one, I must free a slave,” or, “then my slave is free,” this becomes binding upon him by agreement. But if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I must free a slave,” or, “then my slave is free,” intending not to do it, this is where disagreement arises: must he free a slave in both cases, or is he not obliged to do so in either? Does expiation for an oath suffice? Or does expiation suffice when the obligation is made conditional, but not when emancipation itself is made conditional? These three positions also apply to divorce.

If a Jew says, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Muslim,” and then does it, he does not become a Muslim, by agreement, because the oath-taker has sworn by something whose occurrence would be binding upon him. The same applies when a Muslim says, “If I do such-and-such, my wives are divorced, my slaves are free, and I am a Jew,” while he hates to divorce his wives, free his slaves, or abandon his religion—even though the explicit ruling transmitted from the four imams is that emancipation takes effect.

It is well known that seven Companions—such as Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, Abū Hurayra, ʿĀʾisha, Umm Salama, Hafsa, and Zaynab, the Prophet’s stepdaughter, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—are more eminent than four Muslim scholars. If they and the leading scholars among the Successors say that the emancipation invoked in an oath is not binding upon him, and that expiation for an oath suffices, then this position—together with the evidence of the Quran and Sunnah—only supports this same conclusion.

How, then, can a person of knowledge and faith justifiably compel the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to follow the weaker position in light of the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound legal analogies, given also the benefits to their religious and worldly affairs? For

---

## Page 16099

the protection this affords their lives, families, wealth, and honor; the reconciliation of their differences; the maintenance of their ties of kinship; their unity in obedience to Allah and His Messenger; and their being spared any need to disobey Allah and His Messenger—all this suffices to warrant preferring this view even for someone unfamiliar with the import of the Quran and Sunnah. How much more so for someone familiar with their import!

Those who maintain that divorce takes effect have no proof strong enough to counter the position of those who deny that it takes effect. However hard anyone might strive to establish religious evidence that divorce takes effect for someone who swears such an oath—evidence free of any equally strong counterargument—he would be unable to do so, just as he has been unable to define its limits.

Is anyone, then, entitled to command something contrary to the consensus of the Muslims and depart from the path of the believers? No one may prohibit issuing fatwas or judgments according to a position adopted by some scholars when it contradicts neither a scriptural text, nor a consensus, nor anything of comparable authority, and precedence is given over it to the religious evidence of the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound analogy. This applies even if it has not been shown to be the stronger position. How much more so when its strength has become apparent through the Quran and Sunnah, and the favor Allah has bestowed through it has been made clear!

For Allah Most High says:

“Allah has already ordained for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

And He says in His Book:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

It is authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that something else is better should expiate his oath and do what is better.”

This is narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 16100

through many chains of transmission. It is recorded in Muslim through the narrations of Abū Hurayrah, ʿAdī ibn Hātim, and Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī. In both Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said to ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Samurah:

“When you swear an oath and then see that something else is better, do what is better and release yourself from your oath.”

Both Sahīh collections also report from Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“For one of you to persist obstinately in an oath concerning his family is more sinful for him than offering the expiation Allah has prescribed.”

Al-Bukhārī gives the wording: “Whoever persists obstinately concerning his family incurs greater sin.” The Prophet’s expression “persists obstinately” derives from the word for obstinacy. Hence these oaths are called “vows of obstinacy and anger.”

The expressions people use concerning divorce are of three kinds.

The first is an immediate, unconditional pronouncement, such as saying, “You are divorced,” or, “You have been divorced.” Divorce takes effect through this by the agreement of the Muslims.

The second is an oath formula, such as saying, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do such-and-such,” or, “I will not do such-and-such.” This is an oath by the agreement of language scholars, the various groups of jurists, ordinary people, and the people of the earth.

The third is a conditional formula, such as saying, “If I do such-and-such, my wife is divorced.” As for this formula, if his intention in using it is to swear an oath—and this describes someone who dislikes divorce taking effect under any circumstances, just as he dislikes

---

## Page 16101

leaving his religion, as when he says, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Jew,” or a Jew says, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Muslim”—then this is an oath, subject to the same ruling as the preceding form expressed explicitly as an oath, by agreement of the jurists.

An oath involves urging or restraining an action, affirming or denying something, by undertaking a consequence that the person swearing dislikes should he act contrary to his oath. A person is therefore only swearing an oath if he dislikes the consequence taking effect when the condition is fulfilled. If he intends the consequence to take effect when the condition is fulfilled, he is not swearing an oath: whether he desires only the condition and does not dislike its consequence, desires the consequence should the condition occur without desiring the condition itself, or desires both.

If, however, he dislikes the condition and dislikes the consequence altogether—disliking its occurrence and undertaking it only if the condition is fulfilled, in order to deter himself or someone else from the act stipulated as the condition, or to urge action by this means—then this is an oath.

If he intends divorce to take effect upon the occurrence of the stipulated outcome, as when he says, “If you give me a thousand, you are divorced,” “When you become free of menstruation, you are divorced,” or “If you commit adultery, you are divorced,” intending divorce to take effect if she commits that indecency, rather than merely swearing an oath concerning it, then this is not an oath. No expiation applies to this according to any jurist, as far as we know. Rather, according to the early generations and the majority of jurists, divorce takes effect through it when the condition is fulfilled.

---

## Page 16102

Thus, an oath intended to urge or restrain an action, or to affirm or deny something—by undertaking, should one act contrary to it, a consequence one dislikes—is an oath in the understanding of all people, Arabs and non-Arabs alike, whether expressed explicitly as an oath or as a conditional statement. For an utterance’s being an oath is like its being a command, a prohibition, or a statement. These meanings are recognized by everyone, Arab and non-Arab alike. Languages differ only in their expressions, not in these meanings. Whatever constitutes an oath, a command, or a prohibition among non-Arabs likewise constitutes an oath, a command, or a prohibition among Arabs. This, too, is an oath as understood by the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—and it is an oath both in common usage and according to all the jurists.

Since it is an “oath,” the Quran and Sunnah provide only two rulings for it. Either it is binding and must be honoured, in which case expiation is prescribed for breaking it; or it is not binding and need not be honoured—as with swearing by created beings or things, such as the Kaaba, the angels, and so forth—in which case no expiation is due, by agreement. As for a binding oath that must be honoured but cannot be discharged through expiation, this is a ruling found neither in the Book of Allah nor in the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Nor is it supported by any scriptural proof that withstands the counterevidence adduced.

If this oath belongs to the oaths of Muslims, then it falls within Allah’s words addressed to the Muslims:

“Allah has already ordained for you the dissolution of your oaths.”

If it does not belong to their oaths, but instead amounts to swearing by created beings or things, then breaking it entails neither expiation nor any other obligation, and it therefore has no legal effect.

---

## Page 16103

This and similar evidence from the Quran, the Sunnah, and reasoned consideration show that ruling that an oath-taker’s divorce takes effect is contrary to the Quran and Sunnah. The most that can be claimed for the other view is that it falls within the scope of admissible independent juristic reasoning.

As for saying that it is not obligatory for all Muslims to act on this view, yet they are forbidden to act on the other, no Muslim scholar would say this after becoming acquainted with the disagreement among Muslims and the evidence involved. Someone who adopts the weaker view because the stronger view is unknown to him can claim, at most, that his position is admissible, so that issuing judgments and fatwas on its basis is not prohibited.

But compelling Muslims to follow this view and preventing them from adopting the view supported by the Quran and Sunnah contravenes what Allah, His Messenger, and His believing servants—including the four imams and others—have enjoined. Whoever prohibits judgments and fatwas declaring that the divorce does not take effect, or prohibits following someone who denies its taking effect on this basis, has contradicted the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the consensus of the Muslims.

Such conduct comes only from someone lacking knowledge—in which case, the most that can be said is that he may be excused, not that he must be followed—or from an obstinate person who follows his own desires. He does not accept the truth when it becomes clear to him, nor does he listen to someone stating it in order to understand what he says. Rather, he follows his own desires without guidance from Allah:

“And who is more astray than one who follows his own desires without guidance from Allah?”

For a person either follows a scholar’s authority or exercises independent juristic reasoning. Someone who follows a scholar’s authority must not denounce a view opposed by that scholar as someone declaring it false would, since he does not know that it is false—much less prohibit its adoption and require adherence to the view of his predecessors. Someone exercising independent juristic reasoning examines the matter and engages in debate. Even when his own view has become clear, he does not deem his opponents’ view admissible, although it falls within the scope of permissible independent juristic reasoning—that is, where it has not been shown to contradict a scriptural text or consensus. Whoever, then, goes beyond the bounds of

---

## Page 16104

admissible adherence to scholarly authority and independent juristic reasoning resembles those of whom it is said:

“When it is said to them, ‘Follow what Allah has revealed,’ they say, ‘Rather, we follow what we found our forefathers following.’”

He is among those who follow their own desires without guidance from Allah.

Anyone who claims that a child born to a man after he has followed this fatwa is a child born of unlawful intercourse is guilty of the utmost ignorance, misguidance, and opposition to Allah and His Messenger.

In sum, if what a person has bound himself to perform is an act of devotion to Allah Most High, intended to draw him closer to Allah Most High, he must either perform it or offer expiation. If he binds himself to something that is not an act of devotion—such as divorce, sale, leasing, or the like—he is not obliged to carry it out. Rather, expiation for an oath suffices, according to the Companions and the majority of Muslims. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, one of the two views reported from Abū Hanīfa, and the position of the discerning scholars among Mālik’s followers. For when divorce is invoked as an oath, its taking effect upon fulfilment of the condition is unwelcome, just as falling into disbelief is unwelcome. Thus, the divorce does not take effect, and expiation is required. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning someone who says, “Divorce is binding upon me according to the four schools,” or something similar: does divorce become binding upon him as he said, or what is the ruling?

He replied:

As for the oath-taker’s saying, “Divorce is binding upon me according to the schools of the four imams,” or “According to the school of those who hold that his divorce takes effect, not those who allow expiation for an oath sworn by divorce,” or “I am bound to

---

## Page 16105

perform the Hajj according to the school of Mālik ibn Anas,” or “Such-and-such is binding upon me according to the view of those Muslim jurists who hold it binding,” or “Such-and-such is binding upon me according to the strictest opinion ever expressed in Islam,” or “Such-and-such is binding upon me: I shall not seek a fatwa from anyone who permits expiation for an oath sworn by divorce,” or “Divorce is binding upon me: I shall not do such-and-such, nor shall I seek a fatwa from anyone who allows me release from my oath or a way to retract it”—or similar expressions that intensify the obligation, reinforcing the binding force of the conditional commitment if the oath is broken, so as to prevent him from breaking his oath.

When someone swears an oath, he wishes to reinforce it by every means of emphasis that comes to mind, and to prevent himself from breaking it by every means available to him. None of this, however, takes these commitments outside the category of oaths for which expiation is prescribed. However solemnly he reinforces oaths for which Allah has prescribed expiation, and even if he intends never to break them under any circumstances, this does not change Allah’s law.

People’s oaths do not alter the laws of the religion. Rather, whatever Allah commanded before a person’s oath remains commanded after it; the oath merely reinforces it. No one may issue a fatwa permitting someone to abandon what Allah has made obligatory or to do what Allah has forbidden, even if he has sworn no oath concerning it. How much more so, then, if he has sworn such an oath?

This is like someone swearing to fulfil his obligations: prayer, zakat, fasting, Hajj, dutifulness to parents, maintaining ties of kinship, obeying the ruler and offering him sincere counsel, refraining from rebellion against him or fighting him, repaying his debts, rendering rights to those entitled to them, and abstaining from injustice, shameful deeds, and the like. These matters were obligatory before the oath, and the oath makes their obligation still more emphatic.

---

## Page 16106

Whatever was forbidden before the oath is more emphatically forbidden after it. Thus the Companions would pledge allegiance to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, undertaking to obey him and wage jihad alongside him. This was obligatory upon them even without their pledge; the pledge merely reinforced it. No one may break such a covenant.

Likewise, no one may break the pledge of allegiance to the ruler that Allah has commanded must be fulfilled, even without having sworn an oath. How much more so if he has sworn one! Indeed, if a man enters into a contract with another concerning a sale, a lease, or a marriage, he may not betray him and must fulfil that contract. How much more binding, then, is a covenant with those in authority to fulfil what Allah and His Messenger have commanded: obeying them, offering them sincere counsel, and refraining from rebellion against them!

Whenever a contract must be fulfilled without an oath, swearing an oath to fulfil it reinforces it, even if rescinding such a contract is not permissible. Indeed, it is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever possesses four traits is a pure hypocrite, and whoever possesses one of them has a trait of hypocrisy until he abandons it: when he speaks, he lies; when entrusted, he betrays the trust; when he makes a covenant, he breaks it; and when he disputes, he resorts to wickedness.”

Whatever was permissible before an oath does not become forbidden when a man swears an oath concerning it. Rather, he may do it and offer expiation for his oath. Likewise, an act that was not obligatory does not become obligatory upon him when he swears to perform it. Rather, he may offer expiation for his oath and leave it undone, however solemnly he has reinforced that oath and whatever means he has used to reinforce it. People’s oaths do not alter the laws of the religion. No one may, by his oath, forbid what Allah has permitted or make obligatory what Allah has not made obligatory. This is the law revealed to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 16107

As for the law of those before him, under the law of the Children of Israel, if a man declared something forbidden to himself, it became forbidden to him; and if he swore to do something, it became obligatory upon him. Their law provided no expiation. Allah Most High said:

“All food was lawful to the Children of Israel except what Israel had forbidden to himself before the Torah was revealed.”

Thus, Israel forbade something to himself, and it became forbidden to him. But Allah Most High said to our Prophet:

“O Prophet, why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has already ordained for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

This provision is the one mentioned in His words:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love transgressors.”

“And eat of what Allah has provided for you, lawful and good, and fear Allah, in whom you believe.”

“Allah will not hold you accountable for your unintentional oaths, but He will hold you accountable for the oaths you deliberately bind yourselves by. Their expiation is to feed ten poor people from the average of what you feed your own families, or to clothe them, or to free a slave. Whoever cannot afford this must fast for three days. That is the expiation for the oaths you swear. And guard your oaths. Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may be grateful.”

For this reason, since the law of those before us provided no expiation, and an oath instead obliged them to perform what they had sworn to do, Allah commanded Job to take a bundle of stalks in his hand and strike with it, thereby avoiding breaking his oath. His law provided no expiation for an oath. Had it provided such expiation, that would have been easier for him than striking his wife, even with a bundle of stalks, for Allah had restored his family to him and given him as many again. But since what they made binding by an oath had the same standing as what

---

## Page 16108

the revealed law made obligatory, an oath for them was like a vow. An obligation imposed by the revealed law may admit of a concession when necessary, just as a concession is allowed in administering the lashes prescribed as a legal punishment when the person being struck cannot withstand separate blows. This differs from what someone undertakes by an oath under our law, for the revealed law does not itself require that act. He is bound by his undertaking, but our law provides him with a way out through expiation.

However, some of our scholars thought that oaths left those who swore them no way out, and that they were necessarily bound by their undertakings. Concluding that our law in this respect was like the law of the Children of Israel, they felt compelled to resort to stratagems concerning oaths: either through the wording of the oath, through khulʿ (marital dissolution) to escape the oath, through a circular divorce formula, or by treating the marriage as invalid so that divorce could not take effect within it.

If none of these devices availed them, they resorted to a marriage arranged to make the woman lawful for her former husband again. This resulted from a lack of knowledge of the gracious, upright religion with which Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in this matter, and of the burdens and shackles Allah removed through it. As Allah Most High said:

“My mercy encompasses all things. So I will decree it for those who fear Me and give zakah, and those who believe in Our signs—”

“Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find described in the Torah and the Gospel they possess. He commands them to do what is right and forbids them from what is wrong, makes good things lawful for them and foul things unlawful, and relieves them of their burden and the shackles that were upon them. So those who believe in him, honour him, support him, and follow the light sent down with him—it is they who will succeed.”

Thus, what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed for his community is the truth in reality. What others have introduced can, at most, stand in relation to his law as the law of those before him stood—even though

---

## Page 16109

those who advanced these views through their independent reasoning have made commendable efforts and performed worthy deeds, for which they are rewarded and recompensed.

In every disputed question over which the Muslim community has differed, the more correct of the two positions is the one that accords with Allah’s Book and His Messenger’s Sunnah. Whoever reaches this position receives two rewards, while whoever’s independent reasoning leads him only to the other position receives one reward. Compared with the other position, the view that accords with his Sunnah resembles an easy path through fertile land that leads to the intended destination. Those other views involve long distances, rough terrain, and hudūtha. Their adherents therefore experience greater toil and hardship than those following the way prescribed by the revealed law.

For this reason, they propagated what the Book and Sunnah indicate according to that approach, which entails binding consequences that Allah and His Messenger hate: severed ties, separation, scattered families, ruined homes, and the division between husband and wife that Satan and sorcerers love—corruption evident to every person of sound judgment. They must then either accept this great evil and submit to burdens and shackles, or resort to the reprehensible practices of those who employ stratagems. Allah kept the Prophet and his Companions clear of both groups by providing them with lawful means sufficient for their needs.

“There are three paths”: the way prescribed purely by the revealed law, in accordance with the Book and Sunnah—the path of the foremost and most virtuous early believers and those who followed them in righteousness; and the ways of burdens and shackles, and of scheming and stratagems—even though those who follow them may be among the foremost people of knowledge and faith, obedient to Allah and His Messenger in the independent reasoning they have undertaken

---

## Page 16110

as commanded.

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

This is like exercising independent judgment to determine the direction of prayer. If each group’s reasoning leads it to one of the four directions, they are all obeying Allah and His Messenger and duly performing the prayer. Yet whoever has identified the actual direction of prayer receives two rewards. Scholars are the heirs of the prophets. Allah Most High says:

“And remember David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field in which some people’s sheep had grazed by night. We witnessed their judgment. We gave Solomon understanding of the matter, and to each We gave judgment and knowledge.”

Everyone who exercises independent judgment is correct in the sense that he is obeying Allah; nevertheless, in reality, the truth is only one.

The point here is that any oath for which Allah has prescribed expiation may be expiated, however severely a person has bound himself by it. Even if he has undertaken not to expiate it, he may still do so. His undertaking not to expiate it is a commitment to prohibit what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful. No one may prohibit what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; rather, he must offer expiation for his oath.

The person making such a stringent commitment dislikes its becoming binding upon him. The more stringent he makes it, the more he dislikes being bound by it. He undertook it only to restrain and deter himself, so that its binding force would prevent him from breaking his oath. He did not undertake it with the intention that it should become binding upon him when the condition was fulfilled, for such an intention contradicts the very nature of an oath. A person swears an oath only by undertaking something he dislikes occurring if he breaches it; and he never swears except by undertaking something he wishes to occur if he breaches it. Thus, no one swearing an oath says, “If I do such-and-such, may Allah forgive me,” or, “May He cause me to die as a Muslim.” Rather, he says, “If I do

---

## Page 16111

such-and-such, I am a Jew or a Christian, or my wives are divorced, or my slaves are free, or everything I own is to be given in charity, or I must perform Hajj ten times, barefoot and bareheaded, according to the school of Mālik ibn Anas, or divorce is binding upon me according to the four schools, or such-and-such is binding upon me according to the strictest opinion.”

He may add: “I undertake not to consult anyone who would give me a ruling allowing expiation.” In his anger, he undertakes obligations from which he believes there will be no escape if he breaks his oath, so that their binding force will deter him from breaking it. Yet he never intends any of those consequences to befall him, whether the condition is fulfilled or not. If he believes that they are binding upon him, he accepts them because he believes himself bound by them, despite his aversion to assuming them—not because he wishes to assume them. Such is the person swearing an oath. Believing that the consequence is binding is different from intending it to become binding.

If, however, he intends the consequence to become binding when the condition is fulfilled, it becomes binding upon him in all cases, even if expressed in the form of an oath. Suppose he intends to divorce his wife if she does a particular thing, or if he himself does a particular thing, and says: “Divorce is binding upon me: you shall not do such-and-such.” If he intends that she should do it and thereby become divorced—rather than intending to forbid her from doing it—and is not averse to divorcing her but actually desires it, then she is divorced in this case. He is not, in reality, swearing an oath; rather, he is making divorce conditional upon that act, using the form of an oath. His words have the meaning of a conditional declaration intended to make divorce take effect. Thus, divorce takes effect here upon what constitutes a breach of the wording expressed as an oath, while his intention is that of a conditional declaration. Here, divorce only

---

## Page 16112

takes effect when the condition upon which it was intended to take effect is fulfilled, not upon an actual breach of an oath. What counts is his intention and purpose, not his supposition and belief: it is upon the former that rulings are based. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Actions are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended.”

The early generations—the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness—and the great majority of later scholars among the followers of the four imams and others agree that wording which may signify either divorce or something else constitutes divorce if divorce is intended, but does not constitute divorce if something else is intended. In their view, divorce is not confined to any particular wording. Hence they say that it may take effect through either explicit or indirect wording. Even wording they regard as explicit, such as the word “divorce,” does not effect divorce if it is accompanied by a qualification that gives it a meaning other than divorcing the woman. An example would be saying to her: “You are released from the bonds of imprisonment,” or “from the husband you had before me,” and the like.

If a woman dislikes her husband, she may secure her release from him in return for compensation, as Allah Most High says:

“It is not lawful for you to take back anything you have given them, unless both fear that they cannot keep within the limits set by Allah. If you fear that they cannot keep within the limits set by Allah, there is no blame on either of them concerning what she gives to secure her release. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them. Whoever transgresses the limits set by Allah—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

This release in return for compensation effects an irrevocable separation, so he may not marry her afterward without her consent. It is unlike an ordinary divorce, which is revocable: he may take her back during the waiting period without her consent. However, scholars disagree about this compensated release: does it constitute one irrevocable divorce counted among the three, or

---

## Page 16113

does it effect an irrevocable separation that does not count as one of the three divorces, but is instead an annulment? There are two well-known views.

The first is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and many of the early scholars. It has also been attributed to a number of the Companions, but it has not been authentically established from any of them. Indeed, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ibn Khuzaymah, Ibn al-Mundhir, and others judged all the reports attributed to the Companions on this matter to be weak.

The second view is that it effects an irrevocable separation without counting as one of the three divorces. This is authentically established from Ibn Abbās, as the hadith experts agree. It was also the view of his students, such as Tāwūs and Ikrimah, and is one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two positions. It is the apparent position of Ahmad ibn Hanbal and other hadith jurists, and was also held by Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Abū Thawr, Dāwūd, Ibn al-Mundhir, Ibn Khuzaymah, and others.

Ibn Abbās supported this view by pointing out that Allah Most High mentions compensated release after two divorces, then says:

“If he then divorces her, she is not lawful for him thereafter until she marries another husband.”

If compensated release were itself a divorce, the total number of divorces would therefore be four.

Those who hold this view then disagreed: must compensated release be expressed using wording other than “divorce”? Must it be expressed only in terms of release, annulment, or redemption, with the additional condition that he does not intend divorce? Or does it make no difference whether he intends divorce or not, so that it constitutes compensated release whatever wording is used, whether “divorce” or anything else?

There are several positions on these questions within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. The soundest is the last, which is supported by the statements of Ibn Abbās and his students, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and his early followers: compensated release is separation in return for compensation. Thus, whenever he parts from her in return for compensation, she is

---

## Page 16114

redeeming herself through that compensation, and he is granting her compensated release, whatever wording is used. No one has ever reported that Ibn Abbās and his students, or Ahmad ibn Hanbal, distinguished between compensated release expressed using the word “divorce” and release expressed otherwise. Rather, their statements, in both wording and meaning, encompass all such cases. When al-Shāfi‘ī, may Allah be pleased with him, mentioned the two views on whether compensated release constitutes divorce, he said:

“I think those who said it constitutes divorce were referring to cases in which wording other than ‘divorce’ is used.”

For this reason, Muhammad ibn Nasr and al-Tahāwī stated that there is no disagreement on this point. Yet al-Shāfi‘ī did not report this distinction from anyone; he merely supposed that they drew it. Al-Shāfi‘ī based this on the principle that, even when contracts have the same meaning, their legal effects differ according to their wording. This underlying principle is itself disputed within his school.

As for Ahmad ibn Hanbal, his principles, his explicit statements, and the position of the majority of his followers establish that contracts are judged by their meanings, not their wording. Another view within his school holds that legal effects differ according to wording. This is discussed in connection with using the wording of sale, concluding a sharecropping agreement using the wording of lease, and other such cases.

We have already cited the statements of Ibn Abbās and his students, as well as those of Ahmad and others, and shown that they clearly make no distinction. We have also shown that the principles of Islamic law do not admit such a distinction, nor do Ahmad’s principles. The source of this distinction was al-Shāfi‘ī’s supposition that they drew it.

Elsewhere, we have discussed and demonstrated that the authentic reports on this subject from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Ibn Abbās, and others clearly indicate that it constitutes compensated release even when expressed using the word “divorce.” This separation is irrevocable. The divorce that Allah Most High mentions in His Book, however, is revocable divorce.

---

## Page 16115

These scholars said: “There is no irrevocable divorce in the Book of Allah that counts towards the three divorces at all. Rather, every divorce Allah Most High mentions in the Quran is revocable.”

They also said: “Even if a man tells his wife, ‘You are divorced with one irrevocable divorce,’ only one revocable divorce takes effect.” This is the position of most scholars, including Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī, and is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school.

They said: “Dividing divorce into revocable and irrevocable categories is contrary to the Book of Allah.” This is the view of the jurists of hadith, the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, and the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. Every divorce without compensation takes effect only as a revocable divorce. Even if a man says, “You are divorced with one irrevocable divorce,” or “with an irrevocable divorce,” only one revocable divorce takes effect according to both of them.

As for compensated release, there is disagreement within their two schools. Whoever adopts the correct view applies this principle consistently: his position remains coherent and does not fall into the contradictions found in other positions. An exception is those followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad who say that compensated release expressed using the word “divorce” takes effect as an irrevocable divorce. They thus recognize, in some cases, an irrevocable divorce that counts towards the three, thereby contradicting their own sound principle established by the Quran and Sunnah.

Some of the Zāhirīs said: “If it is effected using the word ‘divorce,’ it constitutes a revocable divorce, not an irrevocable one.” The proponent of this view could not classify it as an irrevocable divorce, since that would contradict the Quran. Yet he thought that using the word “divorce” necessarily made it a divorce, so he classified it as revocable. This is mistaken, for the purpose of a woman redeeming herself can be achieved only through an irrevocable separation. Hence, no disagreement is known among Muslims that compensated release brings about an irrevocable separation. Some, however, regarded the transaction as revocable, saying: “The husband may return the compensation and take her back.”

The position of the four imams and the majority, however, is that the husband has no unilateral right to rescind it. If both spouses agree to rescind it, as when two contracting parties mutually cancel their contract, that is a separate matter of disagreement, discussed in detail elsewhere.

---

## Page 16116

The point here is that the Book of Allah makes clear that divorce after consummation can only be revocable. The Book of Allah mentions no irrevocable divorce except before consummation or after the waiting period has expired. If a man divorces his wife three times, she becomes unlawful to him. This is the major irrevocable separation, which occurs only through three divorces, not through a single, unqualified divorce: the latter produces neither a major nor a minor irrevocable separation.

It is authentically reported that Ibn ‘Abbās was told, “The people of Yemen generally end their marriages through compensated release.” Ibn ‘Abbās replied, “Compensated release is not divorce.” He also returned a woman to her husband after two divorces and one compensated release. This is the apparent position of Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s school and one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two positions.

Those who hold this view, however, disagreed over whether the ruling changes with the wording used. The correct view is that, when the meaning is the same, it is decisive regardless of the wording employed. This is because what matters is the purposes and realities of contracts, not their wording alone. Whatever constitutes compensated release remains compensated release, whatever the wording; whatever constitutes divorce remains divorce, whatever the wording; whatever constitutes an oath remains an oath, whatever the wording; whatever constitutes an oath of marital abstinence remains an oath of marital abstinence, whatever the wording; and whatever constitutes zihār remains zihār, whatever the wording.

Allah Most High mentions in His Book “divorce,” “oaths,” “zihār,” “oaths of marital abstinence,” and “a wife’s redeeming herself”—which is compensated release—and assigns each its own ruling. We must therefore understand the boundaries of what Allah revealed to His Messenger: classify as divorce whatever constitutes divorce; as an oath whatever constitutes an oath; as compensated release whatever constitutes compensated release; as zihār whatever constitutes zihār; and as an oath of marital abstinence whatever constitutes an oath of marital abstinence. This is what is established from the leading Companions and their jurists, and from those who followed them in righteousness. Some scholars, however, have confused some of these categories with others,

---

## Page 16117

thus treating what is zihār as divorce. This increases the incidence of divorce, which Allah and His Messenger detest. People must then either endure what is displeasing or end it through something even more displeasing to Allah and His Messenger: a marriage arranged to make the woman lawful again for her former husband.

The divorce prescribed by Allah and His Messenger is for a man who wishes to divorce his wife to pronounce a single divorce during a period of purity in which he has not had intercourse with her, or when her pregnancy is evident. He then leaves her to wait for three cycles. If he wishes to remain with her, he takes her back during the waiting period; otherwise, he releases her with kindness. If he subsequently decides to marry her again, he does so through a new marriage contract. Thereafter, if he wishes to take her back or remarry her, he may do so; if he wishes to divorce her, he divorces her. This is the prescribed divorce in accordance with the Sunnah. Anyone who divorces only in accordance with the Sunnah has no need to resort to what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, such as a marriage arranged to make her lawful again, or anything else.

Rather, if he divorces her three times, each divorce allowing him to take her back or enter into a new marriage contract, she then becomes unlawful to him until she marries another husband. Under no circumstances may she return to him through a marriage arranged to make her lawful again. Indeed:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the man who marries for this purpose and the man for whose benefit he does so.”

His Companions, including his rightly guided caliphs, and others agreed on this. There is no known instance in Islam of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or any of his caliphs or Companions ever returning a thrice-divorced woman to her husband after such an arranged marriage. Nor was this practice openly conducted during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, anyone who did it acted secretly, and even the woman and her guardian might not know. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed “the man who marries for this purpose and the man for whose benefit he does so.”

Regarding usury, he said:

“Allah has cursed the one who consumes usury, the one who pays it, its two witnesses, and its scribe.”

He thus cursed the scribe and the witnesses because people used to call witnesses to usurious debts, whereas they did not call witnesses to marriages arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.

---

## Page 16118

Moreover, during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the dowry was not recorded in writing as debts were, nor were witnesses called to the marriage for the sake of the dowry. Rather, people would conclude the marriage among themselves, with the marriage being known, and the man would deliver the dowry to the woman, leaving no outstanding debt owed to her. For this reason, when speaking of marriage arranged to make a woman lawful again for her former husband, the Messenger of Allah did not mention the scribe and witnesses as he did concerning usury. Likewise, no hadith concerning the calling of witnesses to marriage has been authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Scholars have differed on this issue, with several views reported within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

One view holds that the marriage must be publicized, whether or not witnesses are called. If it is publicized without witnesses, the contract is valid. This is the position of Mālik and of Ahmad according to one report.

Another view holds that witnesses must be called, whether or not the marriage is publicized. Thus, if witnesses are called and those involved agree to keep it secret, the marriage is not invalidated. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad according to one report.

A third view requires both witnesses and publicity, while a fourth requires either one of the two. Both views are also reported within Ahmad’s school.

As for a “secret marriage,” which those involved agree to conceal and to which they call no witnesses, scholars generally regard it as invalid; it is a form of fornication. Allah Most High says:

“And lawful to you are all women beyond these, provided that you seek them in marriage with your wealth, desiring chastity, not unlawful sexual intercourse.”

These issues are discussed at length in their respective places.

---

## Page 16119

The purpose here is simply to highlight the difference between the views established by the Book and the Sunnah, with their justice, wisdom, and mercy, and the weaker views; and to show that the Book and wisdom with which Allah sent His Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, secure people’s welfare in this life and the Hereafter in the most perfect manner. For he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the Seal of the Prophets, and there is no prophet after him. Allah has brought together in his law the perfections that were dispersed among the laws of earlier prophets. Since no prophet would come after him, the matter was brought to completion through him, just as the religion was perfected through him. The Book he brought is the best of books, his law the best of laws, his way the best of ways, and his community the best of communities. Allah has guaranteed its protection, through the Prophet’s words, from agreeing upon misguidance.

Nevertheless, some members of this community possess knowledge and understanding that others do not. The scholars are the heirs of the prophets. Allah Most High says:

“And David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which some people’s sheep had strayed by night, and We were witnesses to their judgment.”

“We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave sound judgment and knowledge.”

These were two noble prophets who passed judgment in a case. Allah granted one of them a particular understanding, without censuring the other; rather, He praised both for their sound judgment and knowledge. The same applies to scholars who exercise independent judgment—the heirs of the prophets and successors of the messengers who act in accordance with the Book.

Muslim scholars likewise hold two views concerning the case in which David and Solomon passed judgment, and similar cases. Some judge according to David’s judgment, while others judge according to Solomon’s judgment. The latter is correct, yet many scholars—or most of them—do not adopt it, and may not even know of it. We have discussed this at length elsewhere, outside this answer. Allah knows best what is correct.

---

## Page 16120

As for a man who “swears an oath by declaring things forbidden,” saying, “Prohibition shall bind me—I will not do such-and-such,” or, “What is lawful is forbidden to me—I will not do such-and-such,” or, “What Allah has made lawful is forbidden to me if I do such-and-such,” or, “What is lawful for Muslims shall be forbidden to me if I do such-and-such,” or similar words, while having a wife: there is a well-known disagreement on this question among earlier and later scholars. The stronger view, however, is that this constitutes an oath and does not result in divorce, even if he intended it as an oath of divorce. This is Ahmad’s well-known position. Indeed, even if he says, “You are forbidden to me,” intending divorce, no divorce takes effect according to him.

Likewise, if he says, “You are to me like my mother’s back,” intending divorce, this does not effect a divorce according to the great majority of scholars. Allah revealed Quranic verses concerning this, for people used to regard both zihār (declaring one’s wife forbidden like one’s mother) and oaths of abstinence from one’s wife as divorce. Allah abolished all of that, prescribing the major expiation for zihār and treating the oath of abstinence as an oath for which the man is allowed four months. He must then either retain his wife honourably or release her graciously.

Many earlier and later scholars likewise held that if a married man declares his wife forbidden to him, or declares lawful things forbidden to himself without qualification, he has thereby pronounced zihār. This is Ahmad’s position.

If he swears an oath of zihār or prohibition not to do something and then breaks his oath, expiation suffices according to Ahmad’s school. However, it has been said that the required expiation is that for zihār, whether he swore an oath

---

## Page 16121

or pronounced it to take effect. This is the position transmitted from Ahmad.

Another view is that if he uses it as an oath, expiation for an oath suffices; but if he pronounces it to take effect, he must offer the expiation for zihār. This view is stronger and more consistent with the legal principles of Ahmad and others.

Thus, expiation for an oath suffices for someone who swears by declaring something forbidden, just as it suffices for someone who makes an oath in the form of a vow, saying: “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj,” or, “My wealth must be given in charity.” Likewise, if he swears an oath involving manumission, he must offer expiation for an oath according to most of the early scholars among the Companions and Successors. Expiation for an oath also suffices for an oath involving divorce, as a number of earlier and later scholars have ruled. What is authentically reported from the Companions does not contradict this; rather, its meaning accords with it. Every oath that Muslims swear is subject to expiation for an oath, as indicated by the Quran and Sunnah.

If, however, a man intends to effect a divorce, manumission or zihār, he is bound by what he pronounces, whether he makes it effective immediately or conditional. Expiation for an oath does not suffice in that case. Allah knows best what is correct.

---

## Page 16122

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who said:

“I shall be bound to divorce if I ever swear by divorce again, unless I do so absentmindedly or by mistake.”

After quarrelling with someone and finding himself in a difficult position, he said: “The oaths of the Muslims bind me,” or, “Oaths bind me according to Mālik’s school: I shall certainly complain about you to the muhtasib.” He did not remember his first oath, and he follows the Shāfiʿī school. What obligation arises from the oath?

He replied:

If he had forgotten the first oath when he swore the second, then remembered it afterwards, he has not thereby broken his oath. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about a man who said to his wife:

“I shall be bound to divorce: whenever I see such-and-such a woman with you, I shall divorce you.” Does he break his oath if she comes over without his seeing her, or if all three meet somewhere other than the place to which the oath referred?

He—may Allah be pleased with him—replied:

If she comes over without his seeing her, or he meets her in someone else’s house, he has not broken his oath—unless it takes place in his own house, or something [in] (1) the circumstances that prompted the oath entails otherwise. Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
