> (1) The passage in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā against Omissions and Textual Corruption).
> Usāmah ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘ah al-Shāmilah.

---

## Page 16123

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man whose wife went out without his permission. He then said to her:

“I shall be bound to divorce you three times: I shall no longer spare you the rod.”

He intended this to apply if she went out without his permission. Does the divorce take effect immediately, or only if she goes out without his permission? And what if he gives her permission afterwards?

He replied:

No divorce takes effect immediately. Rather, if she goes out without his permission, he breaks his oath. He may give her general permission, provided that neither his intention nor the circumstances that prompted the oath entail otherwise. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked about a man who accused his wife of stealing some dirhams. She said:

“By Allah, I have taken nothing.”

He then said:

“I shall be bound to divorce you three times unless you produce the dirhams.”

Does she remain his wife?

He replied:

If it becomes clear that she did not take the dirhams, he has not broken his oath, according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. This is because what the oath requires is impossible, and because he only intended her to return them if she had taken them. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16124

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who made the following statement concerning his pregnant wife:

“If my wife gives birth to a girl, she is divorced.”

Then, before she gave birth, an exchange took place between them, and he pronounced a single divorce. Afterwards, she gave birth to a girl. Does the conditional divorce take effect or not?

He replied:

If he had separated from her irrevocably through that divorce—either because it was in return for compensation or because he left her until her waiting period expired—then scholars have two well-known opinions on this matter. Al-Shāfiʿī likewise has two opinions. One is that the divorce takes effect; this is also a view derived within Ahmad’s school. But if he had not separated from her irrevocably, and instead took her back during her waiting period, the marriage remains in effect. If the condition upon which he suspended the divorce is fulfilled, the divorce takes effect.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who quarrelled with his wife and was hurt by her. He said:

“I shall be bound to divorce you three times: if you say, ‘Divorce me,’ I shall divorce you.”

She remained silent, then asked her mother:

“What is he saying?”

Her mother replied:

“He is saying such and such. Tell him, ‘Divorce me.’”

The woman then said, “Divorce me.” Does one divorce take effect, three divorces, or none?

---

## Page 16125

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If, by saying, “If you say, ‘Divorce me,’ I shall divorce you,” he did not intend to divorce her at that gathering, then he is instead to divorce her in the presence of witnesses. If he had no particular intention, he does not break his oath if they part without a divorce being pronounced; however, he is to divorce her afterwards, pronouncing the divorce he intended by his oath. If he did not intend to divorce her three times or twice, a single pronouncement of divorce suffices.

This applies if his intention was to comply with her request unconditionally. But if he intended to comply with her request only so long as she wanted a divorce, and she then withdraws her request and says, “I do not want a divorce,” nothing is incumbent upon him if he does not divorce her. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who, while living with his wife somewhere other than her residence, said to her:

“If I stay with you, you are divorced; and if I reside with you, you are divorced.”

Then he also said:

“You are forbidden to me.”

He then moved himself and his belongings elsewhere, without his wife, while she returned to her original residence. If he returns and stays with his wife, does one divorce take effect or two? Is residing the same as staying, or is one broader in scope than the other?

If he did not intend divorce by declaring her forbidden to him, does divorce take effect just as it would if he had intended it? And if there is a legal position that resolves this situation but differs from his own school of law, is he permitted to follow it or not?

---

## Page 16126

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

As for his saying, “If I stay with you, and if I reside with you”: if the oath-taker’s intention regarding staying—once the circumstances that prompted it have ceased—is comparable to that of someone invited to lunch who swears that he will not eat lunch, then the circumstances prompting the oath indicate that he meant that particular lunch. Hence, the correct view is that he does not break his oath by eating another lunch.

The same applies if he and his wife visited some people and he observed circumstances that made him dislike his wife remaining with them, so he swore that he would neither stay nor reside there, intending under those particular circumstances, or if the circumstances prompting the oath indicate that meaning. But if he intended a general prohibition, meaning that he would neither stay with them nor share their residence under any circumstances, then he does not break his oath by staying. If he made the oath without specifying his intention, there is a well-known disagreement among the scholars.

Where staying does constitute a breach of his oath, if by staying he meant residing, no more than one divorce takes effect, unless he intended more than that. Similarly, if someone repeatedly swears by Allah concerning a single act, only one expiation is required, according to the correct view. If staying is included within residing—as the unqualified wording apparently indicates—then this is a case of overlapping conditions. It is like saying:

“If you eat one apple.”

One view is that two divorces take effect because both conditions have been fulfilled. Another view is that only one divorce takes effect. This is the stronger view, since what is understood from these words is, “You are divorced,” whether she eats a whole apple or half of it.

Likewise, if he says, “If I stay”: staying is “a term with more than one meaning”; it can mean residing, encompassing contracts, and he would have

---

## Page 16127

first sworn that he would not stay, then sworn concerning something broader, namely residing. If he resides there, the first is subsumed under the second. Thus, according to the stronger of the two views, no more than one divorce takes effect, if divorce is deemed to take effect in his case.

As for his saying:

“You are forbidden to me,”

if he swore not to do something and then did it, he must offer expiation for an oath. If, however, he did not swear an oath but simply declared her forbidden to him, he must offer the expiation prescribed for zihār. In neither case does divorce take effect.

This is the view of the majority of scholars among the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and the leading scholars of the Muslims. They hold that declaring one’s wife forbidden does not effect divorce unless one intends it. This is reported from Abū Bakr, ʿUmar and ʿUthmān, and is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others. Although some later followers of certain imams have claimed that customary usage has made this expression an explicit pronouncement of divorce, that is not the position of those imams whom they follow.

In the early days of Islam, people regarded the formula of zihār—“You are to me like my mother’s back”—as an explicit pronouncement of divorce. This continued until Aws ibn al-Sāmit pronounced zihār against his wife, the woman who pleaded her case, whose ruling was established in Allah’s revelation:

“Allah has indeed heard the words of the woman who disputes with you concerning her husband and complains to Allah.”

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) initially ruled that she was divorced, until Allah abrogated that ruling and made zihār entail expiation, even if divorce was intended by it.

---

## Page 16128

and declaring one’s wife “forbidden” analogous to zihār, since zihār likens her to a woman forbidden to him, whereas this declaration expressly pronounces her forbidden. Both are objectionable and false speech. Allah’s Book establishes that declaring something lawful forbidden constitutes an oath, in His words:

“Why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you?”

through to His words:

“Allah has already prescribed for you the dissolution of your oaths.”

This, however, is not the place to discuss that matter at length.

As for a person seeking a fatwa following the mufti’s opinion, the position of the four imams and all the other leading scholars is that no one is obliged, nor is it prescribed for him, to adhere to the views of one particular person in everything that person declares obligatory, forbidden or permissible, except the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).

Some scholars, however, say that the person seeking a fatwa must follow the most learned and discerning of those whom he can consult. Others say that he may choose among the muftis. If he possesses some ability to assess their opinions, one view is that he should follow whichever of the two opinions he considers stronger, according to his discernment, since this is preferable to an unrestricted choice. Another view is that he may not exercise independent judgment unless he becomes qualified to do so. The former view is more sound.

Thus, if the person seeking a fatwa considers one of the two opinions stronger—either because its evidence appears stronger according to his discernment, or because the scholar holding it is more learned and scrupulous—he may follow it, even if that scholar’s opinion differs from the position of the legal school.

Shaykh al-Islām (may Allah have mercy on him) was asked about a man who said to his mother-in-law:

“Unless you sell me your slave woman, your daughter is divorced three times.”

They replied, “We will not sell you the slave woman.” He then said, “Your daughter is divorced three times,” although he intended the condition, “If you do not give me the slave woman”?

---

## Page 16129

He replied:

If he intended the condition in his heart and did not intend divorce, he has not broken his oath. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others: as between him and Allah, divorce is not binding upon him. And Allah knows best.

He was asked about a man who said to his wife, “If you enter the house, you are divorced,” and she then entered forgetfully.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he says, “If you enter the house, you are divorced,” and she enters forgetfully, the divorce does not take effect according to the stronger of the scholars’ two opinions. This is the position of the scholars of Mecca, such as ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, Ibn Jurayj and others, and it is one of the two views reported from Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—the brave and intrepid, the lion of battles and lion of the Sunnah, who patiently endured tribulation for Allah’s sake, the eminent scholar and authoritative proof, Ahmad ibn ʿAbd al-Halīm ibn ʿAbd al-Salām ibn Taymiyyah, may Allah, Lord of all creation, have mercy on him—was asked:

A man swore, on pain of threefold divorce, that the Quran is sound and letters and that the Most Merciful rose over the Throne, in accordance with what the apparent wording conveys and what people understand from its apparent meaning. Has he thereby broken his oath or not? (*)

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 240):
> Two points should be noted here:
> First: This fatwa is called al-Futyā al-Dimashqiyya (the Damascus Fatwa). The Shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, included it in al-Tis‘īniyya, 2:547–573.
> Second: There are minor textual corruptions between the two passages, which become apparent upon comparison. The most important are:
> 1 – 33:172: “Likewise, al-Shahrastānī mentioned”; the correct reading is “Therefore, al-Shahrastānī mentioned,” as in al-Tis‘īniyya, 2:551.
> 2 – 33:181: “The discussion that determines one of the expression’s possible meanings will follow”; the correct reading is “The context of the discussion determines one of the expression’s possible meanings,” as in al-Tis‘īniyya, 2:566.
> 3 – 33:185: “The Arabs did not use the word ‘knowledge’ specifically for the ‘urf (custom) subsisting in the human heart”; the correct reading is “… specifically for the ‘arad (accident) subsisting in the human heart,” as in al-Tis‘īniyya, 2:572.
> 4 – 33:186: “Not merely on the basis of the wording [so distinguish—may Allah set you right—between what the wording alone indicates], namely the wording of the verb, and what it indicates through its specific attribution to a particular agent.” The text in square brackets was omitted because the copyist’s eye skipped ahead; it appears in al-Tis‘īniyya, 2:573.

---

## Page 16130

He replied—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all creation.

If the man who swore this oath meant that people’s voices when reciting the Quran, and the ink with which its letters are written, are eternal and without beginning, then he has broken his oath. I know of no one who says this, although discussing the ink in the written Quran and the servant’s voice on their own may be disapproved of, lest this be used as a means of asserting that the Quran is created. Some people have discussed the servant’s voice, although we know that what we recite is truly Allah’s speech, not anyone else’s, and that what lies between the two covers is truly Allah’s speech. Yet I know of no one who has declared that the writing ink and the servant’s voice when reciting the Quran, taken together, are eternal.

However, those among the followers of desires whose hearts harbour deviation understand nothing from the words of Allah, His Messenger, the earliest forerunners, and those who followed them in righteousness concerning “Allah’s attributes” except meanings befitting created beings, not the Creator. Then, when they find such expressions in the words of Allah and His Messenger, they seek to distort the words from their proper places. If they find them in the words of those who follow the early generations, they fabricate lies against them and report their words according to their own false understanding, or add to their wording and alter it in both extent and character, as we hear from their tongues and see in their books.

Then someone who thinks well of these transmitters may attribute this doctrine to those to whom they ascribed it, censuring and debating someone who does not exist. His censure falls upon someone so described who does not

---

## Page 16131

exist—much as Allah diverted abuse from His Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), who said:

“Are you not amazed at Quraysh? They revile Mudhammam (‘the blameworthy’), while I am Muhammad (‘the praised one’).”

This is like the Rāfida’s allegation that the people of the Sunnah—those devoted to hadith, jurisprudence, worship, and knowledge—are Nāsiba (enemies of the Prophet’s household); the Qadariyya’s allegation that they are Mujbira (deniers of human free choice); and the Jahmiyya’s allegation that they are Mushabbiha (those who liken Allah to His creation). Likewise, those who oppose hadith and reject its adherents describe them as Nābita (upstarts), Hashwiyya, scum, and rabble, among other falsely applied names. Whoever examines the books of the speculative theologians who oppose this position will find that, in most or all cases, they argue only against this position, although we know of no one who actually holds it.

If, however, the man who swore the oath meant that the Quran which Allah sent down to Muhammad (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) consists of these 114 surahs, in both their letters and their meanings; that the Quran is neither the letters without the meanings nor the meanings without the letters, but both together; and that our reciting its letters and grasping its meanings does not negate the existence of those meanings and letters before we came into existence—then this is the position of the Muslims, and he has not broken his oath.

Likewise, if he meant that this Quran, which the Muslims recite and write in their copies, is truly—not metaphorically—the speech of Allah, Glorified be He, and that

---

## Page 16132

it is impermissible to deny that it is Allah’s speech. For speech is truly attributed to the one who originally uttered it and whose attribute it is, even if someone else has uttered it by way of conveying and transmitting it. It is the speech of the one who originally spoke it and whose attribute it is, not of the one who conveys it in transmission.

For we know with necessary certainty from the religion of Allah’s Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and that of this ummah’s early generations that, were someone to say:

“These letters—the letters of the Quran—are not part of the Quran; rather, ‘Quran’ refers only to the meanings,”

they would condemn his statement in the strongest terms. They would regard him as they would someone who says:

“The body of Allah’s Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is not included in the designation ‘Allah’s Messenger’ (may Allah bless him and grant him peace); rather, this designates the soul, not the body.”

Or someone who says:

“Prayer does not denote the movements of the heart and body; rather, it denotes only the heart’s actions.”

Likewise, al-Shahrastānī—one of those most knowledgeable about religions, sects, and doctrines—stated in Nihāyat al-iqdām that the view that the Quran’s letters came into existence is a newly introduced view, and that the position of this ummah’s early generations was to deny that they were created. Yet he himself was a leading figure among those who held that they came into existence.

No discerning person should suppose that anything in reason or revelation contradicts this. Rather, whoever has delved deeply into these doctrines and understood their subtleties knows with certainty that sound reason, which never errs, contains nothing that contradicts the position of the early generations and the people of hadith; rather, it contradicts

---

## Page 16133

what their opponents may imagine because of the darkness of their hearts and the desires of their souls, or what they may falsely attribute to them because of their lack of piety and scant religious commitment. Even if one were to suppose—purely hypothetically—that sound reason, which never errs, contradicted some transmitted reports, one of two things would necessarily follow: either the transmitter would have to be judged untruthful, or the transmitted report would have to be interpreted otherwise. Yet, praise be to Allah, this has not happened, nor can it ever happen, for Allah’s preservation of the Book and Wisdom He revealed precludes it. Such contradictions do, however, occur in reports fabricated by heretics to discredit the people of hadith, such as the reports about “the sweat of horses” and “the grey camel,” and other reports that hadith scholars know to be lies.

This is further clarified by the widely transmitted accounts of Islamic scholars—such as al-Shāfiʿī, al-Humaydī (?), Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, and others—condemning anyone who claimed that the utterance of the Quran is created. The reports concerning this are well known in the book of Ibn Abī Hātim, the book of al-Lālakā’ī, the student of Abū Hāmid al-Isfarāyīnī, the book of al-Tabarānī, the book of Shaykh al-Islām, and the works of others whose enumeration would be lengthy. This is not the place to establish the matter through evidence, questions, and answers.

---

## Page 16134

The same applies if, by mentioning voice, the oath-taker means affirming the reports from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), his Companions, and their followers which accord with the Quran and were accepted by the early generations. These include the report from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections:

“Allah calls to Adam with a voice.”

They also include the report cited by al-Bukhārī as evidence on this subject:

“Allah calls to His servants on the Day of Resurrection with a voice heard by those far away just as it is heard by those nearby.”

And the report:

“When Allah speaks revelation—whether the Quran or something else—the inhabitants of the heavens hear His voice.”

In the wording of Ibn Abbās: “They heard the voice of the Compeller.” There is also the report that Allah spoke to Moses with a voice.

These and other reports were stated—either in their own words or as transmissions from others—by such figures as Abd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, Abd Allāh ibn Abbās, Abū Hurayra, Abd Allāh ibn Unays, Jābir ibn Abd Allāh, Masrūq, one of the foremost senior scholars among the Successors, Abū Bakr ibn Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Hārith ibn Hishām, one of the seven jurists, Ikrima, the freedman of Ibn Abbās, al-Zuhrī, Ibn al-Mubārak, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and countless others.

No scholar of Islam before the second century is reported to have denied this or maintained the contrary. Rather, these reports were well known among them and circulated in every age and region. Someone did, however, deny this during the time of Imam Ahmad; this was the first period in which innovations arose denying this in opposition to the scriptural texts. Otherwise, individuals who denied this and other matters had already appeared before him, but the Muslims shunned such a person for as long as he denied this, and he became among them like a mangy camel.

Thus, if the oath-taker intended what has been authentically transmitted from the early generations, he has not broken his oath.

---

## Page 16135

As for his oath affirming that “The Most Merciful rose over the Throne” is to be understood according to what its apparent sense conveys and what people understand from it: the expression “apparent sense” has come to carry different meanings. What is apparent to sound human nature, in the Arabic language, in the upright religion, and in the usage of the early generations differs from what is apparent according to the conventions of many later scholars.

If, by “apparent sense,” the oath-taker means anything peculiar to created beings or anything implying some imperfection—for example, imagining that Allah’s rising over the Throne resembles the settling of bodies upon other bodies, or the settling of spirits, if he does not consider spirits to fall within the category of bodies—then he has broken his oath and spoken falsely. I know of no one who says this, apart from what is reported from such figures as Dāwūd al-Jawāribī al-Basrī, Muqātil ibn Sulaymān al-Khurāsānī, Hishām ibn al-Hakam the Rāfidī, and others like them—if these reports about them are authentic.

For it must be held with certainty that nothing is like Allah, whether in His essence, His attributes, or His actions. His distinction from created beings and His transcendence above any likeness to them are greater and more profound than those among His creation who know Him can comprehend or those who describe Him can express. He must be declared free of every attribute that entails having come into existence or any other imperfection.

Anyone who claims that any follower of Ahl al-Sunnah likened His attributes to those of His creation is either lying or mistaken.

If, however, by “apparent sense,” the oath-taker means what was apparent to the innate nature of Muslims before deviant inclinations emerged and opinions became fragmented—namely, the apparent meaning that befits His majesty, glorified and exalted is He

---

## Page 16136

, just as this is the apparent sense of all the other names and attributes applied to Him, glorified is He, such as life, knowledge, power, hearing, sight, speech, will, love, anger, and pleasure—as in His words:

“What prevented you from prostrating to what I created with My two hands?”

and:

“Our Lord descends to the lowest heaven every night,”

and other such expressions. When applied to us, the apparent sense of these expressions denotes accidents or bodies, because that is the nature of our own essences. When applied to Allah, glorified and exalted is He, however, their apparent sense is only what befits His majesty and His noble Self.

Thus, the terms “essence,” “existence,” and “reality” are applied both to Allah and to His servants, retaining their apparent sense in both usages, while it is firmly understood that their apparent sense with respect to Allah is neither equivalent to their apparent sense with respect to us nor shares with it anything that entails imperfection or having come into existence. This holds whether these terms are considered univocal, equivocal, or applicable in differing degrees. The same applies to His words:

“He sent it down with His knowledge,”

“Indeed, Allah is the Provider, the Possessor of Strength, the Firm,”

“What I created with My two hands,”

and:

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.”

The same principle applies to all of these.

The early Jahmiyyah denied all attributes of Allah that, in us, are accidents, such as knowledge and power, or bodies, such as the hand and face. Their later followers affirmed many of the attributes that, in us, are accidents, such as knowledge and power, while denying some of them, as well as the attributes that, in us, are bodies. Among them were also those who affirmed some of the attributes that, in us, are bodies, such as the hand.

---

## Page 16137

As for the followers of the Salaf, al-Khattābī, Abū Bakr al-Khatīb, and others report their position as follows: The way of the Salaf is to take the hadiths and Quranic verses concerning the attributes according to their apparent sense, without describing their modality or likening them to creation.

Thus, we do not say that “hand” means power or that “hearing” means knowledge. Discussion of the attributes derives from discussion of the essence and follows the same pattern and example. If affirming the essence means affirming its existence, not its modality, then affirming the attributes likewise means affirming their existence, not their modality.

Al-Khattābī and al-Khatīb—two leading scholars of the Shāfiʿī school whose knowledge of transmitted texts is universally acknowledged, as is al-Khattābī’s understanding of their meanings—have thus informed you that the way of the Salaf is to take these texts according to their apparent sense, without describing their modality or likening them to creation. Allah knows that I have investigated the positions of the Salaf extensively, and I know of none among them who differed on this.

As for any later scholar who says that the way of the Salaf is to hold that the apparent sense is not intended, anyone who thinks well of him must understand that by “the apparent sense” he means the sense that befits the creature, not the Creator. There is no doubt that this is not intended. Whoever says that it is intended is a disbeliever once the proof has been established against him.

There are therefore two questions here: one concerning terminology and the other concerning meaning.

As for meaning, there are three possible positions concerning His words:

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne,”

and similar texts.

One is to say: a rising over like that of

---

## Page 16138

a created being; or to interpret it as a rising over that entails having a beginning or an imperfection. This is the view reported from those misguided people who liken Allah to creation and ascribe a body to Him. It is decisively refuted by both the Quran and reason.

Another position is to say: There is no real rising over at all, no God over the Throne, and no Lord above the heavens. This is the position of the misguided Jahmiyyah, who negate the divine attributes. For anyone who has closely studied the prophetic teachings, it is decisively refuted by what is necessarily known of the religion of Islam, and by the innate disposition Allah has given His creatures to acknowledge that He is above His creation, just as they acknowledge that He is their Lord.

Ibn Qutaybah said:

“The nations, Arab and non-Arab alike, have always acknowledged, both in their days of ignorance and in Islam, that Allah is in heaven—that is, above heaven.”

The third position is to say: Rather, He—glory be to Him—rose over the Throne in a manner befitting His majesty and greatness. He is above His heavens, over His Throne, distinct from His creation, even though He—glory be to Him—Himself sustains the Throne and its bearers. His rising over is known, its manner is unknown, belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation, as Umm Salamah, Rabīʿah ibn Abī ʿAbd al-Rahmān, and Mālik ibn Anas said.

This is the position of the Muslims. It is the apparent meaning of “rose over” to Muslims in general whose sound innate dispositions remain intact, having deviated neither into negating the divine attributes nor into likening Allah to creation. This is what Yazīd ibn Hārūn al-Wāsitī—whose standing as a leading scholar, eminence, and merit are universally acknowledged, and who belonged to the generation following the Successors—meant when he said:

---

## Page 16139

“Whoever claims that ‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne’ means something contrary to what is firmly understood by ordinary Muslims is a Jahmī.”

For Allah has instilled in His servants’ innate dispositions, and created them to acknowledge, that their Lord is above His heavens. Thus, Abd Allāh ibn Rawāhah recited these verses to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the Prophet approved them:

“I bear witness that Allah’s promise is true,
And that the Fire is the disbelievers’ abode;
That the Throne floats above the water,
And above the Throne is the Lord of all worlds.”

Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak’s standing as a leading scholar and his eminence were acknowledged by all the groups of the Muslim community. It was even said, “He is the Commander of the Faithful in every field,” and, “Khurasan has produced no one like Ibn al-Mubārak.” He studied under most of the scholars of his time, including al-Thawrī, Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, al-Awzā‘ī, and their contemporaries. When asked, “How do we know our Lord?” he replied, “By knowing that He is above His heavens, over His Throne, distinct from His creation.”

Muhammad ibn Ishāq ibn Khuzaymah—known as the Imam of Imams, to whom the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī turn for his defence of their school, and of whom one might almost say that none among them knew it better—said:

“Whoever does not affirm that Allah is above His heavens, over His Throne, distinct from His creation, must be called upon to repent. If he does not repent, he is to be beheaded and thrown onto a rubbish heap, so that neither the Muslims nor the non-Muslims under their protection are troubled by his foul stench. His property would become fayʾ (communal property).”

Imam Mālik ibn Anas also said, as related from him by Abd Allāh ibn Nāfi‘ in a widely known report

---

## Page 16140

from him:

“Allah is in heaven; His knowledge is everywhere, and no place is beyond His knowledge.”

Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal said the same as Mālik and Ibn al-Mubārak. The reports from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his Companions, and the other scholars of the Muslim community to this effect are mass-transmitted, as anyone who has traced them knows. Scholars have compiled works on them, both short and extensive. Anyone who examines these reports also knows with certainty that not a single word contradicting this can be transmitted from any of them. Rather, they all agree upon one position and one creed, each confirming the others, although some are more knowledgeable than others. Likewise, they all acknowledge the prophethood of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—even though some possess greater knowledge of its distinctive features, merits, rights, implications, reality, and qualities.

Moreover, none of them ever said, “The apparent meaning of this is not intended,” or, “This verse or this hadith is to be understood contrary to its apparent meaning.” Yet they did say such things about verses concerning legal rulings whose general scope or apparent meaning was not intended, and they discussed difficult passages that might be imagined to contradict one another. This is well known to anyone who has examined the matter. They stated these attributes in sound, pure, and clear terms, untainted by obscurity or deceit.

Had this not been the apparent meaning understood by Muslims, the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and then the early generations of the Muslim community would have told the community, “The apparent meaning you understand is not intended.” And some Muslim would have found this verse and others problematic.

---

## Page 16141

If, however, the heart of someone among the later generations has deviated until he comes to see in the verse an unsound meaning implying temporal origination or deficiency, then there is no doubt that what appears to this misguided person is not intended. If we see someone understanding the verse in this unsound apparent sense, we first establish for him that this meaning is not conveyed by the verse’s apparent wording. We then establish, secondly, that the meaning itself is unsound. Even if it were supposed to be the apparent meaning of the verse—although this supposition has no basis in reality—it would be necessary to interpret the verse otherwise, just as with other apparent meanings opposed by evidence requiring that something other than the apparent meaning be intended.

Know that one must master the ways in which expressions convey meaning and understand that a meaning may become apparent from an expression in several ways. Sometimes this is through linguistic convention, customary usage, or usage established by revelation, whether in individual words or combinations of words. Sometimes it is through the construction in which an individual word occurs, which alters what the word itself signifies. Sometimes it is through accompanying verbal indicators that make the expression figurative. Sometimes it is through what is indicated by the circumstances of the speaker, the person addressed, and the subject being discussed. Discussion will follow that specifies one of an expression’s possible meanings or clarifies that its figurative sense is intended. These and other factors give an expression its apparent meaning. Without such understanding, one may flounder in these matters.

Certainly, when an expression has no accompanying contextual indicators clarifying the speaker’s intent, but his intent is known through separate verbal evidence, then something contrary to the apparent meaning is intended. An example is a general expression whose scope is restricted by separate evidence. If, however, the evidence diverting the expression from its apparent meaning is an evident rational consideration, there is a well-known disagreement in the principles of jurisprudence over whether the intended meaning should be described as contrary to the apparent meaning.

---

## Page 16142

In short, once the intended meaning is known, whether we say, “This is the apparent meaning,” or “This is not the apparent meaning,” is a merely verbal disagreement. If, in the oath-taker’s customary usage, the apparent meaning of this verse is something resembling the attributes of created beings, he has broken his oath. If, however, its apparent meaning in his customary usage is what befits Allah Most High, he has not broken it.

If his locality’s customary usage of this expression is unknown, there is no circumstance from which his intent can be inferred, and his intention cannot be ascertained, then he may have intended either a sound meaning or a false one. His oath is therefore not deemed broken on the basis of doubt.

All of this follows from the position that someone who swears to something, believing it to be as he has sworn, has broken his oath if it proves otherwise. According to those who do not consider him to have broken his oath, the ruling concerning his oath is clear.

Know that, if you investigate why most of those who deny this attribute and others like it have denied them, you will find that they have assumed the apparent meaning of this verse to be an istiwā’ like that of created beings, or an istiwā’ that entails temporal origination or deficiency. They then attribute this position to their opponents and proceed to adduce evidence of its falsity. They then say, “It must therefore be interpreted as dominion, appearance and manifestation, or superiority and pre-eminence—that is, loftiness of rank and standing.”

Yet a “third meaning” remains: an istiwā’ befitting His majesty, which this expression signifies just as the words knowledge, will, hearing, and sight signify their respective meanings. This meaning is attested by revelation.

---

## Page 16143

Indeed, anyone who closely studies the reports from the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, knows with inescapable certainty that he conveyed to the Muslim community: “Your Lord, whom you worship, is above everything and over everything—above the Throne and above the heavens.” He also knows that, for the early generations generally, this was as firmly established as their knowledge that Allah knows all things and has power over all things. Not a single statement is transmitted from any of them that indicates the contrary, either explicitly or by its apparent meaning. Nor did any of them ever say, “Our Lord is not above the Throne,” or “He is not over the Throne,” or “His istiwā’ over the Throne is like His istiwā’ over the sea,” or any of the other absurdities of the Jahmiyyah. Nor did any of them liken His istiwā’ to that of a created being or ascribe to Him an attribute entailing temporal origination or deficiency.

What shows you the error of applying the term “apparent meaning” to a meaning befitting created beings is that expressions are of “two kinds.”

“The first” comprises expressions whose meaning is simple, such as the words lion, donkey, sea, and dog. When expressions of this kind are used—as in “the lion of Allah and the lion of His Messenger,” or when a dull-witted person is called a donkey, a scholar, a generous person, or a swift horse is called a sea, or a lion is called a dog—this is figurative usage;

---

## Page 16144

then, if a contextual indication accompanies it, the intended meaning becomes clear, as in the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning Abū Talhah’s horse: “We indeed found it to be a sea”; his words: “Khālid is a sword from among Allah’s swords, which Allah has drawn against the polytheists”; his words to ʿUthmān: “Allah will clothe you in a shirt”; and Ibn ʿAbbās’s words: “The Black Stone is Allah’s right hand on earth. Whoever touches it and shakes hands with it is as though he has pledged allegiance to his Lord”—or words to that effect—and similar statements.

Such wording involves figurative usage, even though the speaker’s intent is evident from the words themselves. It is understood according to this apparent meaning in that speaker’s usage, not according to the apparent meaning of its original lexical sense. Everyone who hears such a statement understands what it means, and that meaning immediately comes to mind, since the original meaning cannot possibly be intended. This makes the statement unambiguous, rather than open to competing interpretations. Understanding the wording in this sense does not in any way constitute the kind of interpretation that diverts an expression from its more likely meaning to a less likely one. This is one source of error among those who go astray in this matter: they imagine that the meaning understood from the expression differs from its apparent meaning and that the expression has therefore been interpreted away from that meaning.

“The second category” consists of expressions whose meanings involve a relation. Their meaning may be purely relational, as with being higher or lower, above or below, and the like. Alternatively, they may convey a positively existing quality that involves a relation, such as knowledge, love, power, incapacity,

---

## Page 16145

hearing, and sight. Expressions of this kind cannot be assigned an independent meaning on the basis of only some of their uses, for two reasons.

First, such an expression has never been used in isolation. Second, doing so would entail either homonymy or figurative usage. Rather, it should be understood as literal in the meaning common to its various uses.

The matter under discussion belongs to this category. The Arabs did not use the word istawā literally and exclusively for a human being sitting on his couch, for example, such that its use in other cases would become figurative. Likewise, they did not use the word “knowledge” literally and exclusively for the cognition residing in the human heart, divided into “immediate” and “inferential” knowledge, while using it figuratively in other cases.

Rather, the verb is sometimes used without a prepositional complement, as in His words:

“When he reached his full strength and maturity.”

Sometimes it takes a preposition denoting an endpoint, as in His words:

“Then He turned toward the heaven while it was smoke.”

At other times, it takes a preposition denoting elevation above. In this usage, it sometimes describes Allah and sometimes describes His creation. It need not therefore be considered literal in one case and figurative in the other. Nor is it permissible to understand Allah’s rising above as implying a characteristic peculiar to the creature rather than the Creator. The same applies to His words, Exalted is He:

“And the heaven We built with might”;

and His words, Exalted is He:

“of what Our hands have made”;

and His words:

---

## Page 16146

Exalted is He:

“The work of Allah, who has perfected all things”;

and His words, Exalted is He:

“And We have certainly written in the Psalms”;

“And We wrote for him on the Tablets concerning all things.”

Would any Muslim deem it permissible to attribute to his Lord the distinctive characteristics of a human builder, maker, writer, or worker? Or would he deem it permissible to deny Him the reality of working and building in a manner unique to Him and befitting His majesty? Or would he deem it permissible to say, “These expressions do not bear their apparent meanings”? Or must he instead say, “Each one’s work accords with his nature. Just as His essence is unlike the essences of His creatures, His working, making, and building are unlike their working, making, and building”?

When we say, “So-and-so built,” or “So-and-so wrote,” we understand the exertion involved in his work and the effects it has upon him only through our knowledge of the builder’s condition—not from the expression alone, namely, the verb and what it signifies through its particular attribution to a specific agent. This will clarify much that many people find difficult and show you where confusion arises in many matters of this kind.

May Allah guide us and our fellow Muslims to the words and deeds He loves and is pleased with. May He unite our hearts upon His religion, which He has chosen for Himself and with which He sent His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful, Master of the Day of Judgment. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon Muhammad, the possessor of the Basin to which people will come, and upon his family and Companions.

---

## Page 16147

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man swore, on pain of triple divorce, that he would not enter his neighbour’s house. He was then compelled to enter and did so. Does divorce take effect in his case or not? If expiation is binding upon him, what is the evidence for that obligation?

He—may Allah be pleased with him—replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If someone swears an oath invoking divorce or emancipation to impel an action or prevent it—for example, saying, “I bind myself to divorce or emancipation as a pledge that I will certainly do such-and-such, or that I will not do such-and-such”; or saying, “If I do such-and-such, my wife is divorced,” or, “My slave is free,” and similar expressions—the scholars hold three views concerning this.

The first is that if he breaks the oath, divorce or emancipation takes effect. This is the view of some of the Successors and is the prevalent view among most jurists.

The second is that neither takes effect and no expiation is due from him. This is reported from some of the early generations and is the position of Dāwūd, Ibn Hazm, and other later scholars. For this reason, Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah, the teacher of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, would not issue a ruling that divorce takes effect, for he narrated

---

## Page 16148

from Tāwūs, from his father, that he did not regard an oath invoking divorce as having any effect. He was asked, “Did he regard it as an oath?” He replied, “I do not know.” Thus, he was certain that he did not consider divorce to take effect, but was unsure whether he regarded it as an oath requiring expiation.

The third view is that expiation for an oath suffices. This is reported from a number of the Companions and others concerning emancipation. It is transmitted from ʿUmar, Hafsah bint ʿUmar, and Zaynab, the stepdaughter of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that they gave a ruling concerning someone who had said to another person, “If I do not separate you from your wife, my wealth is to be given in charity and my slaves are free.” They said, “Expiate your oath and leave the man with his wife, O Hārūt and Mārūt!” This is also the view of Abū Thawr and other jurists concerning emancipation.

Hammād ibn Salamah likewise related in his Jāmiʿ, from Habīb ibn al-Shahīd, that he asked al-Hasan al-Basrī about a man who said that every slave he owned would be free if he went in to see his brother. He replied, “He should expiate his oath.” This is also reported from Abū Hurayrah and Umm Salamah.

Abū Bakr al-Athram said in his Musnad: ʿĀrim ibn al-Fadl narrated to us: Muʿtamir ibn Sulaymān narrated to us, saying: My father said: Bakr ibn ʿAbd Allāh narrated to us: Abū Rāfiʿ informed me, saying:

“My mistress, Laylā bint al-ʿAjmāʾ, said, ‘Every slave I own is free, all my wealth is an offering, and I am a Jew and a Christian if you do not divorce your wife, or I do not separate you from your wife.’”

He said: “I went to Zaynab bint Umm Salamah”—whenever a woman of learning in Medina was mentioned, Zaynab would be mentioned. He said: “I went to her, and she came—meaning, to her—

---

## Page 16149

and said, ‘Hārūt and Mārūt are in the house!’ She said, ‘O Zaynab, may Allah make me your ransom! She said that every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.’ She replied, ‘A Jew and a Christian! Leave the man with his wife’—meaning, ‘and expiate your oath.’

“I then went to Hafsah, the Mother of the Believers. She sent for her, and she came to her and said, ‘O Mother of the Believers, may Allah make me your ransom! She said that every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.’ She replied, ‘A Jew and a Christian! Leave the man with your wife’—meaning, ‘and expiate your oath.’

“She then went to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, and he came—meaning, to her. He stood at the door and greeted her. She said, ‘Sā anta wa-sā abūka.’ He said, ‘Are you made of stone or iron? What are you made of? Zaynab gave you a ruling, and Hafsah, the Mother of the Believers, gave you a ruling, yet you did not accept their rulings!’ She said, ‘O Abū ʿAbd al-Rahmān, may Allah make me your ransom! She said that every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.’ He replied, ‘A Jew and a Christian! Expiate your oath and leave the man with his wife.’”

This report is well known. Humayd and others also narrated it from Bakr ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Muzanī. Ahmad and others narrated it and mentioned that all three instructed her to offer expiation for an oath. However, Sulaymān al-Taymī included in his narration, “Every slave she owns is free,” whereas Humayd and the others did not include this additional wording. This was the response Ahmad gave when he distinguished between an oath involving emancipation and other oaths.

---

## Page 16150

Another report he cited from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās stood in opposition to this. Al-Marwadhī said:

Abū ʿAbd Allāh said, “If a man says, ‘Every slave I own is free,’ they become free when he breaks his oath, because neither divorce nor emancipation admits expiation.”

He also said, “The words ‘Every slave she owns is free’ do not occur in the report concerning Laylā bint al-ʿAjmāʾ. Abū Rāfiʿ’s report states that she consulted Ibn ʿUmar, Hafsah, and Zaynab, mentioning emancipation, and they instructed her to offer expiation for an oath. Humayd and the others, however, did not mention emancipation.”

He continued:

I asked Abū ʿAbd Allāh about Abū Rāfiʿ’s report concerning his wife, and how she consulted Ibn ʿUmar and Hafsah, who instructed her to offer expiation for an oath. I asked, “Does it mention walking?”

He replied, “Yes. My view is that expiation for an oath is required.” Abū ʿAbd Allāh said, “The wording ‘Every slave …’ is not given in it except … (1)”

I asked, “Then if someone swears an oath involving the emancipation of his slave and breaks it?”

He replied, “The slave becomes free. This is what is reported from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās: they said concerning the slave woman, ‘She becomes free.’” He then said, “We have heard it only from ʿAbd al-Razzāq, from Maʿmar.”

I asked, “What is its chain of transmission?”

He replied, “Maʿmar, from Ismāʿīl ibn Umayyah, from ʿUthmān ibn Hādir, from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās.”

He also said, “Ismāʿīl ibn Umayyah and Ayyūb ibn Mūsā were both Meccans.”

Abū Tālib reported that Abū ʿAbd Allāh said:

“If someone swears an oath committing himself to walk to the House of Allah, enter the consecrated state for Hajj, offer a sacrificial animal, or give his wealth in charity to the poor—and likewise any oath for which expiation may be offered, whereby he binds himself by swearing to something—only expiation for an oath is required. This follows Bakr’s report from Abū Rāfiʿ concerning the incident involving Hafsah. She had sworn that she would separate her from her husband, so Hafsah said, ‘O Hārūt and Mārūt! Expiate your oath and free your slave woman.’ He thus treated all of that as expiation for an oath in lieu of emancipation; this is preferable. That is because emancipation admits neither expiation nor an exception clause. And an exception clause

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 241):
> The correct wording, with the blank filled, is: “No one includes ‘every slave’ in this report except al-Taymī.” This means that Sulaymān al-Taymī alone reported this wording, as the shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—related in 35:261.

---

## Page 16151

always applies to an oath that admits expiation. He therefore required emancipation and prescribed expiation in the other cases.”

I say:

This is what Imam Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with him—stated in his answers. Yet his explicit statements in several places imply that expiation for an oath suffices.

He expressly stated in several places that an exception clause applies [only] (1) to an oath that admits expiation. He also expressly stated that, when someone swears an oath involving divorce or emancipation, his position is that an exception clause does not benefit him; for he may append an exception clause, unlike when he directly effects divorce or emancipation, concerning which there is only one position. This was also reported from Ibn ʿAbbās, and it is the position of Mālik and others.

Shaykh Abū Hāmid al-Isfarāyīnī and his followers attributed to Ahmad a distinction between divorce and emancipation regarding exception clauses. This is a mistaken attribution to Ahmad. Rather, this is the position of the Qadariyyah, who maintain that divine will means divine command, and that emancipation is an act of obedience, unlike divorce. Thus, if someone says, “His slave is free, if Allah wills,” emancipation takes effect; but if he says, “His wife is divorced, if Allah wills,” divorce does not take effect. They narrated a hadith to this effect, with a chain of transmission through Syrian narrators from Muʿādh. It is among the reports fabricated by the Qadariyyah who lived in Syria.

The source of this mistake is that Ahmad said concerning someone who says, “If I come to own so-and-so, he is free, if Allah wills,” and subsequently acquires him, that the slave becomes free. He also said, concerning someone who says, “If I marry so-and-so, she is divorced, if Allah wills,”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I did not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā against Omissions and Textual Corruptions).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’—book coordinator for the Shāmila Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 16152

and subsequently marries her, that she is not divorced. Thus, he distinguished between the two conditional declarations. One of his principles is that emancipation may be made conditional upon acquiring ownership, since it is an act of devotion, like a vow. It may therefore validly be made conditional upon acquiring ownership, as in Allah’s words:

“And among them are those who pledged to Allah, ‘If He gives us of His bounty, we will surely give in charity and be among the righteous.’”

Emancipation may itself be the purpose of acquiring ownership. Hence, the sale of a slave on condition that he be emancipated is valid. Divorce, by contrast, is not the purpose of marriage. If divorce were held to take effect upon marriage, the marriage would serve no purpose; and contracts that do not achieve their intended purpose are invalid. When Ahmad distinguished between divorce and emancipation in this matter, someone reporting his position assumed that the distinction arose from the exception clause invoking Allah’s will. This was a mistaken attribution to him.

The point here is that exception clauses in oaths involving divorce and emancipation take different forms. If someone says, “If I do such-and-such, my slave is free (or: my wife is divorced), if Allah wills,” the exception clause benefits him according to the sounder of the two reports from Ahmad. If he says, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do such-and-such, if Allah wills,” a group of Ahmad’s followers, including Abū Muhammad and Abū al-Barakāt, held that the exception clause benefits him here according to Ahmad’s sole reported position.

Others held that both reports apply to the oath formula and the conditional formula alike. This accords more closely with Ahmad’s statements, and it is also the position of Mālik and his followers, who hold two opinions concerning both forms.

Thus, if Ahmad, according to the sounder of the two reports from him, allows an exception clause in an oath involving emancipation—whether expressed in conditional form or in the form of an oath—while also holding that an exception clause applies only to an oath that admits expiation, it necessarily follows that such oaths admit expiation. He said, in the report of

---

## Page 16153

Abū Tālib—when asked about the exception clause, he replied: “The exception clause applies to oaths that admit expiation. Allah Most High says:

‘Allah will not hold you accountable for what is unintentional in your oaths.’

Every oath therefore admits expiation, except divorce and emancipation.”

As for Sulaymān al-Taymī being the one who mentioned, “Every slave he owns is free,” Sulaymān al-Taymī is a thoroughly reliable transmitter, of greater standing than those who did not mention this addition. The reason—and Allah knows best—may be that those who omitted emancipation were reluctant to mention it because of the disagreement surrounding it. This is illustrated by the fact that some transmitters omitted emancipation even when reporting this account from al-Taymī, although it is undisputed that al-Taymī himself mentioned it.

Al-Maymūnī said: “Ahmad and Ibn Abī ʿAdī said: ‘They did not mention emancipation in the report of Abū Rāfiʿ.’”

I say: Muhammad ibn Abī ʿAdī is the most eminent of those who narrated from al-Taymī. Thus, it is clear that some transmitters would omit this addition even though it is established as part of the report. For this reason, when Abū Thawr found it to be established, he adopted it.

As for the other report from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn ʿUmar, Ahmad said: “We have heard it only from ʿAbd al-Razzāq and on the authority of Maʿmar.” As for ʿUthmān ibn Hādir, it has been said that he heard from Ibn ʿAbbās. Abū Zurʿah said: “He is a trustworthy Yemeni of Himyarī descent.” Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah included reports from him.

The first report is more firmly established, and its transmitters are scholars and jurists who understand what they narrate. This latter report contains obfuscation, and its exact wording has not been reliably preserved for us. A detailed discussion has been provided

---

## Page 16154

of the grounds for judging it weak elsewhere. If it is authentic, it would establish disagreement among the Companions on this matter. Al-Bukhārī mentioned a report from Ibn ʿUmar concerning divorce which may or may not fall within this category.

In sum, disagreement on this question is established among the early generations, including ʿAtāʾ, al-Hasan al-Basrī, and others. Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī discussed it in his commentary on al-Khiraqī’s statement:

“Whoever swears an oath pledging the emancipation of the slaves he owns, then breaks it, thereby emancipates all those he owns: his male and female slaves, those contracted to purchase their freedom, those whose emancipation is stipulated upon his death, his slave women who have borne him children, and any share he owns in a slave.”

He explained:

“This means that a man says, ‘If I do such-and-such, every slave I own is free and emancipated,’ or, ‘Everything I own is free.’ If he then breaks his oath, his slaves become free, and expiation does not avert this. This has been reported from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās. It was also the position of Ibn Abī Laylā, al-Thawrī, Mālik, al-Awzāʿī, al-Layth, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ishāq.”

He continued:

“It has also been reported from Ibn ʿUmar, Abū Hurayrah, ʿĀʾishah, Umm Salamah, Hafsah, Zaynab bint Abī Salamah, al-Hasan, and Abū Thawr that expiation for an oath suffices, because this is an oath and thus falls within the general scope of the Most High’s words:

‘Its expiation is to feed ten poor people.’”

He cited the previously mentioned report of Abū Rāfiʿ, then said:

“Our evidence is that he has made emancipation contingent upon a condition. Emancipation admits such conditions, so it yields its effect when its condition is fulfilled, as does divorce. Divorce is excluded from the verse’s general scope, and emancipation is analogous to it. Moreover, emancipation is not truly an oath; it is merely made contingent upon a condition, and thus resembles divorce.”

He said:

“As for the report of Abū Rāfiʿ, Ahmad

---

## Page 16155

said concerning it: ‘Make expiation for your oath and emancipate your slave woman.’ This is an addition that must be accepted. It is also possible that she owned no slave other than that woman.”

I say: Their stated analogy is undermined by all the other commitments made contingent upon a condition: giving property in charity, walking to Mecca, making sacrificial offerings, saying, “If I do such-and-such, I shall be obliged to emancipate a slave or grant a divorce,” or saying, “If he does such-and-such, he is a Jew or a Christian,” and similar statements. These all take the form of a condition, yet they regard them as oaths on account of their meaning. The basis upon which he proceeds is itself disputed, for there is disagreement concerning divorce. Indeed, if they do not deem emancipation to take effect, even though it is an act of devotion, then all the more reason not to deem divorce to take effect. Abū Thawr did not concede the ruling concerning divorce, but said:

“If there is consensus on this, consensus is most deserving of being followed. Otherwise, analogy dictates that it is like emancipation.”

Yet it is known that there is no consensus on this matter.

As for the addition he mentioned in the report of Abū Rāfiʿ—that they said, “Emancipate your slave woman”—this is an error. No one who narrated this report mentioned that they said, “Emancipate your slave woman.” Ahmad, al-Jūzajānī, al-Athram, Ibn Abī Shaybah, Harb al-Kirmānī, and several other compilers narrated it, but none mentioned this. Ahmad’s statements in the general body of his responses show that he did not report these words from them. Rather, his response was that only al-Taymī mentioned the oath involving the slave’s emancipation. Abū Muhammad quoted this from al-Khallāl’s al-Jāmiʿ, and al-Khallāl mentioned it within the account of a question transmitted by Abū Tālib

---

## Page 16156

as we have explained. This is an error in what is attributed to Ahmad. Abū Tālib sometimes misunderstood what he transmitted, and this is one such instance.

As for his report from Ahmad that qualifying an oath with “if Allah wills” applies only to oaths whose breach can be expiated, several others also transmitted this from Ahmad. Moreover, Abū Tālib is trustworthy, and his reports are generally accurate, although he occasionally made mistakes in the wording. His report that this qualification applies where expiation is possible contains no mistake; rather, he transmitted it just as others did.

Hārūn ibn ʿAbd Allāh said:

“Abū ʿAbd Allāh was asked, ‘Did Ibn ʿAbbās not hold that this qualification could be added after some time had elapsed?’

“He replied, ‘That concerns a statement, not an oath. He was referring to the words of Allah, Almighty and Majestic:

“‘Never say of anything, “I will do that tomorrow,” without adding, “If Allah wills.”’”

Abū ʿAbd Allāh said: “That concerns a statement, not an oath. Such a qualification is valid only where expiation is possible. If someone swears an oath involving divorce or emancipation, he does not make expiation.”

Thus, he explicitly stated that this qualification applies only to oaths whose breach can be expiated. If, alongside this, he also explicitly stated that such a qualification is valid when someone swears an oath involving divorce or emancipation, he must consequently recognize expiation in those cases. What he said is what the Book and Sunnah entail, for Allah Most High said:

“But He will hold you accountable for the oaths you bind yourselves by. The expiation for this is to feed ten needy people with the average food you provide for your families,”

up to His words:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

He thus prescribed this expiation for binding oneself by an oath without restriction, and made it the expiation for our oaths when we swear.

---

## Page 16157

and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ may act if he wishes or refrain if he wishes.”

Whatever falls within the scope of the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings be upon him) also falls within the scope of Allah Most High’s words. Divorce and emancipation pronounced with immediate effect do not fall under the terms ‘oath’ or ‘swearing,’ by scholarly consensus. This is unlike swearing to urge or deter someone, or to affirm or deny something, which constitutes an oath by agreement of the imams.

As for a purely conditional pronouncement, such as saying, “If the sun rises, you are divorced,” they hold two well-known positions. According to al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two positions among Ahmad’s followers, it is not an oath; this was the position preferred by al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā. According to Abū Hanīfa and the other position among Ahmad’s followers, it is an oath; this was the position preferred by Abū al-Khattāb.

The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that something else is better should do what is better and expiate his oath.”

This is a general statement, implying that this applies to every oath. Whatever does not admit of this, therefore, is not an oath.

The purpose here is to establish precisely what has been transmitted from the early generations and the imams on this issue. The evidence will be discussed later, if Allah Most High wills. Al-Bukhārī related in his Sahīh that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“There is no divorce except out of a desire for it, and no emancipation except that by which one seeks the Face of

---

## Page 16158

Allah.” It is well known that someone who swears by divorce or emancipation does not seek divorce, nor does he intend emancipation as an act of devotion; rather, he is using them to swear an oath.

As for an oath involving divorce, some have held that it requires expiation, while others have held that it requires none. This second position is that of Dāwūd and his followers. The Shia hold that divorce does not take effect through it and that no expiation is required. This is a weak position, although the position that divorce takes effect without expiation is also weak—and weaker still. The requirement of expiation is what has been transmitted from Tāwūs and others, and it follows from the statements of the Companions. A number of Mālikī muftis and others have issued fatwas to this effect.

There is no doubt that there are stronger grounds for holding that divorce does not take effect than for holding that emancipation does not take effect. Thus, if the Companions ruled that emancipation does not take effect, this applies all the more to divorce. However, no report concerning divorce had reached Abū Thawr, so he said: “Analogy dictates that divorce likewise does not take effect, unless there is a scholarly consensus on the matter, in which case that has greater claim to be followed.”

If, however, someone says, “If I do such-and-such, I must free my slave or divorce my wife, my wealth shall be given in charity, and I must perform Hajj, or I must fast for such-and-such a period,” or something similar, then expiation for an oath suffices according to the schools of Ahmad and al-Shāfiʿī. This is also one of the two positions reported from Abū Hanīfa—the report transmitted by Muhammad—and it is said that Abū Hanīfa adopted this position in the end. It is also the position of a group of Mālik’s followers, and it is what has generally been transmitted from the Companions and Successors. The jurists call this “a vow of obstinacy and anger.”

This applies when the act vowed is an act of devotion, such as emancipation and the like. If it is not an act of devotion, as in the case of divorce, then nothing is required according to Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and one report from Ahmad. The well-known position reported from Ahmad, however, is that expiation for an oath is required.

---

## Page 16159

A “vow of devotion” is, for example, when the person making the vow wishes the condition to be fulfilled and undertakes to perform the consequent act out of gratitude to Allah Most High. He might say: “If Allah heals my sick relative, I undertake to fast for such-and-such a period, or give such-and-such in charity,” or something similar. He must fulfil this vow, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Narrated by al-Bukhārī.

As for a “vow of obstinacy and anger,” the person making it intends neither the condition nor its consequence to occur. For example, someone tells him, “Travel with so-and-so,” and he replies: “If I travel, I undertake to fast for such-and-such a period, or to perform Hajj.” His intention is to do neither the conditional act nor the consequent act.

This is like someone declaring himself a Jew or a Christian if he does such-and-such, or saying, “If I do such-and-such, I am an unbeliever,” and similar statements. The imams agree that if the condition is fulfilled, he does not thereby become an unbeliever. Rather, according to Abū Hanīfa and the well-known position reported from Ahmad, he must offer expiation for an oath. According to Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, nothing is required of him.

This differs from saying: “If you give me the money, I will disbelieve.” He becomes an unbeliever by this statement; indeed, his disbelief takes effect immediately, because he intends to disbelieve when the condition is fulfilled.

A group of jurists considered the wording used by the person making the vow and said: “He has made the ruling conditional upon something, so it must take effect when that condition is fulfilled.” They did not distinguish between a “vow of obstinacy” and a “vow of devotion.”

The Companions, the majority of the early generations, and the scholars of discerning judgment, however, said: “What matters is the meaning of the words.” Here, the person laying down the condition intends both the condition and its consequence to occur, whereas in the other case he intends to avoid

---

## Page 16160

either the condition or its consequence. This is why he sometimes swears using a conditional formula and sometimes an oath formula. For example, he says: “On pain of having to perform Hajj, I shall certainly do such-and-such,” or “…I shall not do such-and-such”; or: “I undertake to free a slave if I do such-and-such,” or “…if I do not do such-and-such.” This is the argument of those who require him to offer expiation in cases involving emancipation, and likewise divorce.

For if someone tells him, “Travel,” and he declares, on pain of freeing a slave or divorcing his wife, that he will not do so, or says, “If I do such-and-such, my slave is free,” or “…my wife is divorced,” his intention is that neither the condition nor its consequence should occur. He is thus using this as an oath, not effecting emancipation or divorce.

They said: This person has sworn an oath undertaking that divorce will take effect. This is like undertaking to pronounce it, by saying: “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to free a slave or divorce my wife.” If he said this, the imams agree that he would not be obliged to divorce his wife. There are, however, two views on whether he would be obliged to free the slave. According to al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others, neither divorce nor emancipation takes effect through this declaration. However, according to the well-known position of his school, al-Shāfiʿī requires expiation if he does not free the slave, but not if he does not divorce his wife. This is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. According to the apparent position of his school, Ahmad requires expiation in both cases; this is also a view held within al-Shāfiʿī’s school.

For when the act vowed is not an act of devotion, there is agreement that he is not obliged to perform it. The well-known position of al-Shāfiʿī and others is that no expiation is required if he does not perform it. Ahmad’s well-known position is that he must offer expiation for an oath.

Those scholars said: His undertaking that divorce will take effect is like his undertaking to disbelieve. If he undertook the latter, he would not thereby become an unbeliever, by agreement. Rather, according to one of the two views, he must offer expiation for an oath, as explained above.

---

## Page 16161

Those who hold that divorce and emancipation take effect said:

The distinction between the two cases is that here he has undertaken a legal consequence—namely, that divorce or emancipation takes effect—whereas there he has undertaken an act of his own—namely, bringing it about. This is like saying, “I am bound to perform Hajj,” or “…to fast,” or “…to give charity.” With an act, he has the choice either to perform it or to refrain and offer expiation. A legal consequence, by contrast, rests with Allah Most High.

They said:

The permissibility of khulʿ (divorce in return for compensation) is established by explicit texts of the Quran and Sunnah. Thus, if a man tells his wife, “If you give me such-and-such, you are divorced,” and she gives it to him, the divorce takes effect. All other conditions are analogous: when divorce is made conditional upon them, it takes effect. Likewise, the permissibility of a contract of emancipation in return for payment is established by the Quran and Sunnah. The same principle applies when a man tells his slave, “If you give me a thousand, you are free,” and likewise when emancipation is made contingent upon any other condition.

This is the sum of their argument. The opposing scholars, however, say:

Your assertion that what becomes binding here is a legal consequence, whereas there it is an act, is mistaken. Rather, in both cases, what is made binding upon fulfilment of the condition is a legal consequence. In one case, however, it is something taking effect; in the other, it is an obligation. Thus, when someone says, “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to perform Hajj,” what follows is only the obligation to perform Hajj, not the actual performance of it.

It may further be said:

There is no difference between the consequence being a legal ruling and its being inseparably connected with one, as with a cause and the effect that necessarily follows from it. If someone declares that he is a Jew or a Christian if he does such-and-such, he has undertaken a legal consequence. Yet there is no disagreement that this does not become binding upon him when the condition is fulfilled.

Moreover, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to fast,” or “…I am bound to perform Hajj,” the consequence is the obligation to fast and perform Hajj. Then, if performing it becomes binding upon him by virtue of its obligatory status, the obligation

---

## Page 16162

is what is made conditional, not the act itself. For if the act itself were made conditional, it would occur as soon as the verbally stipulated condition was fulfilled. Rather, what is made conditional is the obligation to emancipate a slave, perform Hajj, or the like. He then has the choice between accepting this obligation and offering expiation.

When he says, “If I do such-and-such, my slave is free,” the consequence is freedom itself, which entails the prohibition of keeping him enslaved. Likewise, divorce taking effect entails the prohibition of marital intimacy with her. Here, the prohibition follows from the consequence; it is not itself the consequence. This belongs to legal discourse establishing and declaring legal effects, whereas the former belongs to legal discourse imposing duties.

The same applies when he says, “If I do such-and-such, my wealth is charity.” He has undertaken that the wealth should become charity. This is a legal consequence, not an act. Yet once it becomes charity, he must give it away.

If he says, “Then my slave is free,” he has undertaken that the slave should become free. If, instead, he says, “Then I am bound to emancipate this slave,” what he has undertaken is the obligation of emancipation. Once it becomes obligatory, he must perform it. Nevertheless, he may have that obligation lifted.

When he says, “Then he is free,” he has undertaken freedom itself. Once the slave becomes free, he must let him go, just as, when a woman has been divorced three times, he must let her go and must neither be alone with her nor have intercourse with her. Thus, the person making the vow in this case has undertaken a legal consequence, and the act follows from it.

Then, when he performs what he has made obligatory upon himself—namely, bringing about the divorce or emancipation—it takes effect. Thus, the conditional stipulation entails an obligation, followed by the act that brings it about and its taking effect. If, however, he intends this conditional stipulation as an oath, it becomes an oath: the obligation does not bind him, he is not required to bring it about, and it does not take effect. If intending an oath prevents all three, then all the more should it prevent just one of them—namely, its taking effect.

---

## Page 16163

They said:

Moreover, when a man likens his wife to his mother’s back or declares her forbidden to him, saying, “You are to me like my mother’s back,” or, “You are forbidden to me,” he has undertaken only a legal consequence, not an act. Nevertheless, expiation applies in this case.

They said:

Just as someone who undertakes the obligation of emancipation has the choice between accepting that obligation and offering expiation, someone who undertakes that emancipation itself should take effect likewise has a choice. He may accept its taking effect, emancipating the slave and letting him go, so that his emancipation—his release of the slave—fulfills the vow. Alternatively, he may neither emancipate him nor let him go; he does not offer expiation in fulfillment of the vow, but rather owes expiation.

This is like someone who says, “If I do such-and-such, this wealth is charity,” or, “This camel is a sacrificial offering,” and then breaks his oath. He has the choice between giving the wealth in charity and sending the camel as a sacrificial offering—thereby accepting the consequences of its being charity or a sacrificial offering—and offering expiation while retaining the wealth and the sacrificial animal rather than sending it away.

If, however, he undertakes something forbidden, such as saying, “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to desecrate a copy of the Quran,” or the like, then, by scholarly agreement, he is not permitted to do so. Whether expiation is obligatory is subject to the disagreement mentioned earlier.

The same applies if he undertakes a legal consequence that he is not permitted to undertake, as when he says, “If I do such-and-such, I am a Jew or a Christian.” He is not permitted to commit himself to disbelief in any way whatsoever. If he intended that, he would become a disbeliever by that very intention.

The point is that neither in revealed law nor in customary usage is there any distinction between undertaking a legal consequence that obliges him to perform an act, which in turn entails another legal consequence requiring or prohibiting an act, and undertaking the legal consequence requiring or prohibiting that act.

Undertaking the obligation to perform the act that entails that legal consequence is illustrated by saying, “Then I am bound to divorce or emancipate.” For he

---

## Page 16164

has undertaken the obligation of divorce, emancipation, and pronouncing divorce. This is an act on his part that produces a legal consequence: divorce or emancipation taking effect.
