It is clear that undertaking the obligation to perform the act that entails the second legal consequence—its taking effect—is a stronger commitment than merely undertaking that it should take effect. In the former case, he has undertaken two legal consequences and two acts, whereas here he has undertaken only one of those consequences and one of those acts. Thus, what he undertakes in the disputed cases is only part of what he undertakes in the cases of consensus. If he need not be bound by the fuller undertaking, then all the more by the lesser one.

In the cases of consensus, if he intends the conditional statement as an oath, he has the choice between breaking his oath and offering expiation for it, and fulfilling what he has undertaken by effecting emancipation, divorce, or charity. The same applies, with even greater force, to what he undertakes in the disputed cases. Breaking this oath requires that the condition be fulfilled without the stipulated consequence occurring. He does not break it unless both requirements are met.

Thus, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I am bound to perform Hajj, emancipate a slave, or pronounce divorce,” he does not break his oath unless he does that thing and the consequence stipulated upon it does not occur. If he brings about the stipulated consequence, he has not broken his oath, just as he would not have broken it had the condition not been fulfilled.

Even supposing that he has undertaken an act by saying, “If I do such-and-such, my slave becomes free, or my wife is divorced,” there is no difference between that and saying, “Then I am bound to emancipate my slave or divorce my wife.” Undertaking either of the two entails undertaking the other. Obligation requires that he perform the obligatory act, and prohibition entails that he may perform the forbidden act. Imposing an obligation entails its being obligatory; declaring something forbidden entails its being forbidden. Obligation requires performance; bringing a legal consequence into effect entails its taking effect and requires prohibition; and prohibition requires abstention. Thus, there is no difference between undertaking to impose an obligation

---

## Page 16165

and its being obligatory and performing the act, or declaring something forbidden, or its being forbidden, or bringing a legal consequence into effect, or its taking effect, or the prohibition resulting from it.

These scholars said:

As for the argument based on divorce for compensation and contractual manumission, and making these conditional upon compensation, the response of the Zāhirī scholars—Ibn Hazm and others like him—is that no conditional emancipation or divorce takes effect. They base this on the absence of a revealed text authorizing it: in their view, any contract or condition whose permissibility is not established by a revealed text is invalid. They do not consider the general evidence requiring the fulfilment of conditions and covenants, prohibiting treachery, and so forth sufficient in this regard, because they believe these texts have been abrogated. This position is weak, as explained at length elsewhere.

The terms divorce and emancipation in the Quran encompass both immediate pronouncements and those made conditional, provided that the intention is for them to take effect upon fulfilment of the condition. Both fall within the meaning of pronouncing divorce. This differs from cases in which its taking effect upon fulfilment of the condition is undesired, for this is an oath included within the meaning of pronouncing divorce.

Accordingly, the response based on the position of the leading scholars and the majority rests on distinguishing between a condition whose fulfilment is intended and one whose nonfulfilment is intended, together with the nonoccurrence of the consequence made dependent upon it. The latter is intended as an oath, not to bring about the stipulated consequence upon fulfilment of the condition. Distinguishing between these two cases is the position of the Companions, among whom no disagreement on this matter is known. It is also the position of the great majority of the early generations

---

## Page 16166

and the jurists. It is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, one of the two positions within the school of Abū Hanīfah, and one position within the school of Mālik.

The response is:

Here, he intended both the condition and its consequence, just as the other person intended a vow of devotion. Just as he distinguishes, in vows contingent upon conditions, between cases in which fulfilment of the conditions is intended and those in which their nonfulfilment is intended, the same distinction applies here. All these cases belong to a single category: rulings contingent upon conditions. If religious law, or reason and custom, distinguish—in rulings contingent upon conditions expressed in language—between what is intended to occur and what is intended not to occur, as the Companions and the majority of jurists agree, then the two cannot be treated alike.

The argument based on divorce for compensation and contractual manumission is appropriate only against those who reject conditional divorce altogether, as is the position of Ibn Hazm and the Imāmiyyah, or some of them. They maintain that divorce contingent upon a condition never takes effect, because they recognize as effective only such divorce as is established to have been authorized by the Lawgiver.

They say:

It has not been established that He authorized this.

They do not accept analogical reasoning. They have also treated the reports from the Companions and Successors concerning oaths by divorce and emancipation as evidence for their position. Yet these reports do not support their position. What is reported from Tāwūs is that he did not consider an oath by divorce to have any effect. This does not entail that he held divorce contingent upon conditions to be ineffective in every case. Rather, he may distinguish between a condition whose fulfilment is intended and one whose nonfulfilment is intended, just as this is the position of Tāwūs, ʿAtāʾ, and others concerning “vows of contention and anger.”

---

## Page 16167

For this reason, when al-Shāfiʿī arrived in Egypt, someone asked him about a person who says, “If I do such-and-such, I shall be obliged to perform Hajj or to fast.” Al-Shāfiʿī ruled that he should offer expiation for an oath. The prevailing view among the people of Egypt was Mālik’s position: that he was obliged to perform Hajj or to fast. Nevertheless, when the son of ʿAbd al-Rahmān al-Qāsim broke such an oath, ʿAbd al-Rahmān al-Qāsim—whose authority is central to Mālik’s school—ruled that he should offer expiation for an oath. He said, “I have given you a ruling according to the position of al-Layth ibn Saʿd. If you do this again, I shall give you a ruling according to Mālik’s position.”

The discerning scholars among the later followers of Mālik favour ruling that expiation for an oath is required. This is also the position that Abū Hanīfah ultimately adopted. The majority of the early generations, among the Companions and Successors, hold that expiation for an oath suffices, as is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. The well-known position of both is that the person may choose between offering expiation and performing the act he undertook. Another position reported from Ahmad is that expiation itself is obligatory; a similar opinion is also mentioned within al-Shāfiʿī’s school.

Likewise, a number of muftis who follow Mālik rule that an oath by divorce requires expiation for an oath. They cite as evidence the report they transmit from ʿĀʾishah, who said, “Every oath, however weighty, has the same expiation as an oath sworn by Allah.” This is the position of Tāwūs and those of the early generations who agreed with him, and it is the meaning of the Companions’ position.

These are important questions requiring an extensive discussion, for which this is not the place. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16168

Section

It is generally unnecessary to base fatwas on this principle. Rather, most questions concerning oaths by divorce or manumission, oaths sworn by Allah Most High, vows, declarations of prohibition, and the like require certain governing rules.

The First Rule

If someone swears not to do something, then does it while forgetting his oath or without knowing that it is the very thing covered by his oath, the scholars hold three positions concerning him.

The first position is that he does not break his oath in any such case, regardless of the type of oath. This is the position of the Meccan scholars, such as ʿAtāʾ, Ibn Abī Najīh, ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, and others, as well as that of Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh. It is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions—indeed, the stronger of the two—and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

I examined Ahmad’s response as transmitted in this report and found that its transmitters were as numerous as those who transmitted his response in the second report, which was adopted by his follower al-Khallāl, al-Khiraqī, al-Qādī, and others of his school. This second position distinguishes between oaths that admit of expiation—such as oaths sworn by Allah Most High, zihār declarations, and declarations of prohibition—and oaths that, according to his explicit ruling, do not admit of expiation, namely oaths by divorce or manumission.

The third position is that he breaks his oath regardless of its type. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, and of Ahmad according to the third report from him.

---

## Page 16169

The first position is the soundest, because the inducement and restraint involved in an oath are analogous to obedience and disobedience in relation to commands and prohibitions. Someone who swears an oath concerning himself, his slave, a relative, or a friend whom he expects to obey him is seeking the performance of whatever he has sworn should be done and preventing whatever he has sworn should be left undone. By his oath, he reinforces his request or prohibition, making it equivalent to an emphatic command or prohibition.

The evidence of the Quran and Sunnah establishes that anyone who does something forbidden out of forgetfulness or by mistake incurs no sin and is not considered disobedient or in breach of the command. Likewise, someone who performs the act covered by an oath out of forgetfulness or by mistake does not thereby break or contravene his oath. This includes someone who acts on the basis of an interpretation, follows someone who has given him a fatwa, follows a deceased scholar, or exercises independent scholarly judgment, whether correctly or mistakenly. Whenever he does not deliberately contravene his oath, but believes that what he has done does not contravene it, he does not break his oath.

This includes someone who concludes a khulʿ (marital dissolution in exchange for compensation) and then performs the act covered by his oath, believing that his oath does not extend to acts performed after the khulʿ. According to those who hold that this oath-related khulʿ is invalid—which is the soundest scholarly position—this case falls under the ruling on an oath sworn by someone acting in ignorance on the basis of an interpretation. Those who consider it valid, however, say that he performed the act covered by his oath during a period of irrevocable separation. If the woman performs the act covered by the oath after irrevocable separation and the expiry of her waiting period, the man does not break his oath, by scholarly agreement. The same applies if she performs it during the waiting period following an irrevocable divorce, according to the majority, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, who hold that a subsequent divorce cannot take effect against a woman whose marriage has been dissolved through khulʿ.

Abū Hanīfah, however, holds that a subsequent divorce can take effect against her. According to him, therefore, the man breaks his oath if the stipulated condition is fulfilled during the period of irrevocable separation. And if the man were a layman and were told, “Conclude a khulʿ with your wife and perform the act covered by your oath,” but did not understand the meaning of khulʿ and assumed it to be merely a divorce

---

## Page 16170

and so divorced her, then performed the act covered by his oath, believing that this would not break it, no further divorce would take effect according to those who hold that a person acting in ignorance on the basis of an interpretation does not break his oath. Likewise, if he were told, “Put her away with a single pronouncement of divorce,” and did so, then performed the act covered by his oath, that act would not bring about a second divorce, even if the first divorce were revocable. However, in cases of forgetfulness, mistake, or ignorance, although he does not break his oath, it remains binding according to the vast majority of scholars. There is no disagreement on this apart from a weak view held by some later scholars.

The Second Principle

If someone swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, but it turns out otherwise, there is even stronger reason not to deem him to have broken his oath than in the case of someone who performs the act covered by his oath forgetfully or in ignorance. For this reason, Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and others distinguished this case from that of someone acting forgetfully or in ignorance. They held that, in this case, he does not break an oath sworn by Allah Most High, whereas in the other case he does.

They explained that, here, the oath concerned the past and therefore did not become binding. For someone who swears about the past either knows the facts—in which case he is either truthful and true to his oath, or deliberately lying, making his oath a deliberately false oath—or is mistaken, believing matters to be as he has sworn. In the latter case, he incurs no sin and does not bear the sin of a liar. This is what these scholars regard as an inadvertent oath. Such a mistake is possible for prophets and others, just as forgetfulness is possible for them.

Thus, in the report of Dhū al-Yadayn, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “I have not forgotten, nor has the prayer been shortened.” Yet he had forgotten, and Dhū al-Yadayn said to him, “Indeed, you have forgotten.” He then asked, “Is it as Dhū al-Yadayn says?” They replied, “Yes.”

An authentic hadith also relates that, when he led them in five units of prayer, they asked him after the prayer, “Have more units been added to the prayer?” He asked, “Why do you ask?” They replied, “You prayed five.” He said, “I am only human; I forget as you forget. When I forget, remind me.”

---

## Page 16171

They said:

An oath concerning the future, however, becomes binding; error and forgetfulness affect the act, not the making of the oath. This is why they distinguish between past and future in oaths sworn by Allah. As for divorce, they give the same ruling for both past and future, corresponding to one of the reports from Ahmad concerning the future.

As for the positions of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, according to their view that someone acting in ignorance or forgetfulness does not break his oath concerning the future, neither does someone who was mistaken when making his oath—someone who swore to something he believed to be as he had sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise.

According to their view that he breaks his oath concerning the future, he also breaks it concerning the past, treating past and future alike. Likewise, he does not break his oath.

This is the approach adopted by those followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad who take this line, such as Abū al-Barakāt in his al-Muharrar.

Those who follow this approach say that anyone who holds that a person does not break his oath when he swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, but it turns out otherwise, must also hold that he does not break his oath by performing the act covered by it forgetfully or in ignorance. They regard the distinction drawn by Mālik and Abū Hanīfah as weak.

Another view is that he does not break his oath concerning the past, with no disagreement on this point, whereas there are two views concerning the future.

This is the approach of a group of Ahmad’s followers who followed the reasoning of the followers of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik. They distinguished between past and future, saying: If someone swears by Allah to something he believes to be as he has sworn, but it turns out otherwise, he does not break his oath. But if he swears not to perform the act covered by his oath, then performs it forgetfully or in ignorance, then in his case there are

---

## Page 16172

two reports. This is the approach of al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā, Ibn ʿAqīl in al-Fusūl, Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and others. They thus confined the disagreement to the future, excluding the past.

Some of them said: An “inconsequential oath” is when someone swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise; there is no disagreement about this. But if he inadvertently utters an oath concerning the future, there are two reports. This is the approach of al-Qādī and Ibn ʿAqīl in al-Fusūl. In his Khilāf, al-Qādī preferred the view that saying “No, by Allah” or “Yes, by Allah” concerning the future is not inconsequential. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and others.

Others among them said: An oath that slips off the tongue is inconsequential, with no disagreement among the scholars. But when someone swears to something that turns out otherwise, there are two reports. This is the approach of Abū Muhammad.

The correct view is that there is disagreement concerning both cases. According to al-Rabīʿ’s report from al-Shāfiʿī, al-Shāfiʿī requires expiation from someone who swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise. Al-Shāfiʿī’s other view, however, is that this is inconsequential, in agreement with the majority. This is also the view of Muhammad ibn al-Hasan. Likewise, the apparent position of Ahmad is that both kinds are inconsequential and require no expiation. This is the view of the majority of scholars.

For this reason, most of Ahmad’s followers stated categorically that neither case requires expiation, mentioning no disagreement. This is because, in his response, he explicitly declared both kinds inconsequential, as al-Khiraqī, Ibn Abī Mūsā, and other early scholars related.

A group transmitted two reports from him concerning inconsequential oaths: one corresponding to the view of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, and another corresponding to the view of

---

## Page 16173

al-Shāfiʿī, as related by a group of scholars, including Ibn ʿAqīl, Abū al-Khattāb, and others. Some of them, such as Ibn ʿAqīl, explicitly stated that if an inconsequential oath is defined as an oath uttered inadvertently, without intent, then someone who swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise, has broken his oath. Consequently, several approaches emerged within Ahmad’s school.

“The approach of the early scholars”: Both cases are inconsequential, with no disagreement.

“Al-Qādī’s approach”: An oath concerning the past is inconsequential, with no disagreement, while there are two reports concerning an oath inadvertently uttered about the future. This approach accords with the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik.

“Abū Muhammad’s approach”: An inadvertently uttered oath is inconsequential, with no disagreement, while there are two reports concerning an oath about the past. This approach accords with al-Shāfiʿī’s position.

“The fourth approach”—the weakest of these approaches—is that, according to one report, one case is inconsequential but the other is not, while according to the other report, the reverse is true.

“The fifth approach,” which reconciles the various approaches, is that there are three reports within Ahmad’s school, as stated by the author of al-Muharrar. Thus, if someone inadvertently says, “No, by Allah” or “Yes, by Allah,” believing the matter to be as he has sworn, this is inconsequential by agreement of the imams

---

## Page 16174

four.

If someone inadvertently utters an oath concerning the future, or deliberately swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise, there are three positions concerning these two cases, corresponding to the three reports from Ahmad.

“The first” is that both are inconsequential, as held by the majority. This is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school and, according to one of the two approaches, is his undisputed position. According to this approach, he explained inconsequential oaths in these terms. This is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions.

“The second” is that he breaks his oath concerning the past, but not an oath uttered inadvertently. This, too, is one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions.

“The third” is the reverse, in accordance with the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik.

It is thus clear that someone whose mistake occurs when swearing the oath—who swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise—is treated, according to one of the two approaches, like someone who forgets or acts in ignorance. According to the other approach, he does not break his oath, without disagreement. The latter approach is the one well known among the leading scholars of Ahmad’s school.

Accordingly, someone who swears an oath invoking divorce concerning a matter he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise, does not break his oath if someone who forgets or acts in ignorance does not break an oath concerning the future. This follows either by treating the two cases alike or with even greater reason, depending on which of the two approaches is adopted. This is likewise how the discerning jurists have stated the matter.

---

## Page 16175

Some later jurists, such as al-Sāmirī, the author of al-Mustawʿib, have supposed that someone who swears an oath invoking divorce or manumission concerning something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise, breaks his oath without disagreement, because oaths invoking divorce cannot be inconsequential. This is mistaken. Those who say that oaths invoking divorce cannot be inconsequential are precisely those who hold that someone who forgets or acts in ignorance breaks an oath invoking divorce. Those who hold that someone who forgets or acts in ignorance does not break his oath do not maintain that oaths invoking divorce cannot be inconsequential—if an inconsequential oath is understood to mean swearing to something one believes to be as one has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise. For the grounds for not regarding the oath as broken in this case are either stronger than in that case or equally strong, as we have explained.

No one can maintain that someone who swears an oath invoking divorce and manumission, with regard to his wife, that he will not do something, and then does it forgetfully or without knowing that it is the act covered by his oath, does not break his oath, yet also maintain that someone who swears to something he believes to be as he has sworn, only for it to turn out otherwise, does break his oath. This is because ignorance at the time the oath is sworn is less serious than ignorance at the time the act covered by the oath is performed; at most, it is equivalent. Moreover, the first oath is unanimously held to be binding, whereas they disagree as to whether the second is binding at all. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“If someone swears an oath invoking divorce concerning some matter and then breaks his oath, does divorce take effect or not?”

He replied:

“This question is disputed among the early and later scholars, who hold three positions.”

---

## Page 16176

“The first view” is that divorce takes effect if he breaks his oath. This is the well-known position among most later jurists, some of whom even believed that it was a matter of consensus. Most of them therefore cited no proof for it. The proof they do offer is exceedingly weak: that he undertook a commitment contingent upon a condition, and so, when that condition is fulfilled, he becomes bound by his commitment.

This reasoning is contradicted by numerous cases, some of which are matters of consensus, such as vows to divorce, to commit a sin, or to do something permissible, and pledges to commit disbelief made by way of an oath. Moreover, no precedent can be adduced for an analogy without there being a legally significant difference between the two cases. Nor is this position supported by the general wording of any scriptural text or by consensus.

However, since a contract entails being bound by what one has undertaken, one might initially suppose that this, too, is a binding contract. This accords with the practice in the earliest days of Islam, before Allah revealed expiation for oaths requiring or forbidding an action. It is also said that this was the law prescribed for those before us. But the law of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, abrogated this: it prescribed for Muslims a means of release from their oaths and allowed them to dissolve the bond of an oath through the prescribed expiation.

If, however, he does not break his oath, divorce undoubtedly does not take effect, except according to a weak opinion reported from Shurayh and mentioned as a narration from Ahmad, concerning cases in which the pronouncement of divorce precedes the condition.

According to the view that divorce does take effect, if he intended the second oath merely to reinforce the first—not to establish another oath—only one divorce takes effect. If he left his intention unspecified, three divorces take effect; another opinion holds that only one takes effect.

---

## Page 16177

“The second view” is that no divorce takes effect and no expiation is required. This is the position of Dāwūd and his followers, as well as several groups among the Shia. Reports suggesting this view are cited from a number of the early generations; indeed, it is explicitly transmitted from some of them, such as Abū Ja‘far al-Bāqir, a transmitter from Ja‘far ibn Muhammad.

Their underlying principle is that oaths invoking divorce, emancipation, zihār (likening one’s wife to a forbidden female relative), declarations of prohibition, or vows are nonbinding, like oaths sworn by created beings.

A number of jurists belonging to the schools of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī, such as al-Qaffāl and the author of al-Tatimma, issue this ruling concerning an oath made by undertaking an obligation to divorce. It is also transmitted as an explicit statement from Abū Hanīfa. Their reasoning is that saying, “Divorce is binding upon me,” “I am bound to divorce,” or similar expressions constitutes a vow, not a pronouncement effecting divorce. It is like saying, “I owe it to Allah to divorce.” There is no disagreement that someone who vows to divorce is not obliged to do so. There are, however, two views on whether he must offer expiation.

“The first” is that he must. This is the expressly stated position of Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and it is also reported from Abū Hanīfa—either without qualification or only when the vow was intended as an oath.

“The second” is that he need not. This is the position of a number of the Khurasanian jurists of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school, such as al-Qaffāl, al-Baghawī, and others. Thus, whoever regards this as a vow and does not require expiation

---

## Page 16178

for a vow to divorce rules that nothing is required of him, as a number of jurists of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school and others have ruled. Whoever holds that expiation is required must, according to his own position, require the expiation prescribed for an oath, as a number of Hanafī and Shāfi‘ī jurists rule. The Hanafīs base this on Abū Hanīfa’s principle that whoever swears an oath by vowing to perform sinful or permissible acts must offer expiation for an oath. Those jurists of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school who hold this view likewise distinguish between saying, “I am bound by a vow,” which entails no obligation, and saying, “If I do it, I am bound by a vow,” which requires expiation for an oath. Thus, they distinguish between a vow to divorce and an oath made by vowing to divorce.

According to the apparent meaning of Ahmad’s expressly transmitted position, both a vow to divorce and an oath made by vowing to divorce require expiation for an oath. Some Khurasanian jurists of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school agree with him on this. Al-Rāfi‘ī, al-Nawawī, and others regard it as the preferred position in al-Shāfi‘ī’s school, mentioning it in connection with vows to perform any permissible act. However, the statement “Divorce is binding upon me” has the form of a pronouncement effecting divorce in Ahmad’s school. If the speaker intends it as a vow, then, according to Ahmad, expiation for an oath is required.

“The third view”—the soundest of these views, and the one supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and reasoning—is that this is an oath like other oaths sworn by Muslims. It is therefore subject to the same rules as their other oaths, namely, expiation upon breaking it;

---

## Page 16179

unless the person who swore the oath chooses to effect the divorce, in which case he may do so without expiation.

This is the view of a group of earlier and later scholars, including Tāwūs and others. It is also what the reports from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) imply on this subject. Many Mālikī scholars and others issue fatwas accordingly. Indeed, it is said that leading Mālikī scholars in many parts of the Maghreb issue fatwas to this effect. This is also what Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s explicit statements and principles imply in several places.

According to this view, if someone repeats an oath that may be expiated twice or three times concerning a single act, must he offer one expiation or several? Scholars hold two views, both of which are reported from Ahmad. The better-known report from him is that one expiation suffices.

Ibn Hazm and others have related these three views concerning oaths involving divorce, just as they have related them concerning oaths involving emancipation, vows, and other matters. Thus, if someone says, “If I do such-and-such, my slaves are free,” the same three views apply. Here, however, none of the jurists of Abū Hanīfa’s or al-Shāfi‘ī’s schools has said that he is not bound to emancipate them, as they have said concerning divorce. A vow to emancipate is therefore valid, unlike a vow to divorce.

What is reported from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is that expiation for an oath suffices. This is established from Ibn ‘Umar, Hafsa, and Zaynab, and they also reported it from ‘Ā’isha.

---

## Page 16180

and Umm Salama, Ibn ‘Abbās, and Abū Hurayra. This is also the view of leading Successors, such as Tāwūs, ‘Atā’, and others. No contrary view is established from any Companion, whether concerning oaths involving divorce or oaths involving emancipation. Indeed, if the Companions hold that someone who swears an oath involving emancipation is not bound to emancipate, then, in their view, someone who swears an oath involving divorce is even less bound to divorce.

This is like swearing an oath by means of a vow, such as saying, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj,” or “fast for a year,” or “give a third of my wealth in charity.” According to Companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), such as ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, ‘Ā’isha, and Ibn ‘Umar, this is an oath for which expiation suffices. This is also the view of the great majority of the Successors, including Tāwūs, ‘Atā’, Abū al-Sha‘thā’, ‘Ikrima, al-Hasan, and others. It is al-Shāfi‘ī’s explicitly stated position and Ahmad’s position, with no disagreement over its attribution to him. It is also one of the two views reported from Abū Hanīfa, and was adopted by Muhammad ibn al-Hasan. A group of Mālik’s followers, such as Ibn Wahb and Ibn Abī al-Ghamr, likewise held this view, and Ibn al-Qāsim gave his son a fatwa to this effect.

The position known from the majority of the early generations—the Companions, the Successors, and those after them—is that there is no distinction between swearing an oath involving divorce, emancipation, or a vow: either expiation suffices for every such oath, nothing is required of the person, or he is bound to carry out what he swore to do.

Indeed, if saying, “If I do such-and-such, I must free a slave,” with the intention of swearing an oath does not oblige him to emancipate a slave, but expiation for an oath suffices instead—even though saying it as a vow would make it binding upon him

---

## Page 16181

by unanimous agreement—then there is all the more reason why his saying, “Then my slave is free,” should not be binding. For if the intention of swearing an oath prevents both the duty to emancipate and emancipation itself from becoming binding, then it should all the more prevent emancipation alone from becoming binding.

Moreover, a person may incur liabilities in a broader range of circumstances: a minor, an insane person, and a slave may incur liabilities even though their legal dispositions are not valid. If, then, the intention of swearing an oath prevents conditional emancipation from becoming a binding liability, there is all the more reason for it to prevent emancipation itself from taking effect. And if emancipation, which becomes binding through a vow, does not become binding when intended as an oath, then divorce, which does not become binding through a vow, should all the more not become binding when intended as an oath.

The consequence of a conditional statement becomes binding only when the speaker intends that consequence to become binding upon fulfilment of the condition, as when he says, “If you release me from the obligation to pay your dowry, you are divorced,” or, “If Allah heals my sick relative, a third of my wealth is to be given in charity.” But the case is different when he dislikes the consequence taking effect even if the condition is fulfilled, and undertakes it only to urge himself or someone else to do something, or to deter himself or someone else from doing it. An example is saying, “If I do such-and-such, then I am a Jew or a Christian, my wealth is to be given in charity, my slaves are free, my wives are divorced, and I must perform Hajj ten times and fast.” Such a person is swearing an oath, by agreement of the Companions, the jurists, and all other groups.

Allah Most High has said:

“Allah has already ordained for you the dissolution of your oaths.”

And He Most High has said:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn. But guard your oaths.”

It is established through several chains in the authentic collections that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that something else is better should do what is better and expiate his oath.”

This encompasses

---

## Page 16182

the oaths of all Muslims, both in wording and in meaning. No scriptural text, consensus, or analogy restricts its scope; rather, the revealed proofs confirm its general applicability.

Oaths in the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger are of two kinds: one is legally recognized, binding, and admits of expiation, such as swearing by Allah. The other is neither legally recognized nor binding and does not admit of expiation: namely, swearing by created beings. If the oath in question is among the oaths of Muslims, then expiation applies to it, and it belongs to the first kind. If it is not among the oaths of Muslims, it belongs to the second. Asserting the existence of a binding oath that does not admit of expiation has no basis in the Quran or the Sunnah.

Dividing the oaths of Muslims into those that admit of expiation and those that do not is like dividing intoxicating drinks into wine and non-wine, journeys into long and short, or gambling into prohibited and permissible forms. Rather, the foundational principles require the contrary. A detailed discussion belongs elsewhere. However, this third position—that expiation applies to all oaths of Muslims—is the position supported by mutually consistent revealed proofs. It is also what has been transmitted from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and the leading Successors:

---

## Page 16183

whether concerning all oaths or only some of them. The rationale given is that it is an oath, and this rationale entails that the ruling applies to all oaths of Muslims.

There are three kinds of formulation. The first is an immediately effective pronouncement, such as saying, “You are divorced.” This is not an oath, and no expiation applies to it, by agreement of the Muslims.

The second is an oath formulation, as when someone says, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I shall certainly do such-and-such.” This is an oath by agreement of linguists and jurists.

The third is a conditional formulation. If it is intended as an oath, it has the same ruling as the second, by agreement of the scholars.

If, however, he intends divorce to take effect when the condition is fulfilled—for example, he chooses to divorce her if she gives him compensation, saying, “If you give me such-and-such, you are divorced”; or he chooses to divorce her if she commits a major sin, saying, “You are divorced if you commit adultery or steal”—and his intention is to effect divorce when the specified condition is met, rather than to swear an oath, then divorce takes effect thereby, by agreement of the early generations.

For divorce made contingent upon a specified condition, the ruling that divorce takes effect has been reported from several Companions, including ‘Alī, Ibn Mas‘ūd, Abū Dharr, Ibn ‘Umar, and Mu‘āwiyah, as well as many of the Successors and those who came after them. More than one scholar has reported consensus on this.

---

## Page 16184

and I know of no one who has reported from any of the early generations that divorce made contingent upon a condition does not take effect. Disagreement on this is known only from some of the Shi‘a and from Ibn Hazm of the Zāhirī school.

These Shi‘a received legal rulings from some jurists of the Prophet’s household concerning someone whose intention was to swear an oath. They therefore assumed that every conditional pronouncement was of this kind. Likewise, some scholars of the majority received rulings from certain Companions and Successors stating that divorce made contingent upon a condition takes effect when that condition is fulfilled. They assumed that this was an oath, treating every conditional pronouncement as an oath, just like one intended as an oath. They did not distinguish between a conditional pronouncement intended as an oath and one intended to effect divorce, just as the former did not distinguish between the two with respect to divorce itself.

I know of no Companion who ruled that an oath makes divorce binding. Likewise, I know of none of them who issued a ruling concerning a conditional pronouncement intended as an oath. This is the position known from the majority of the early generations, and even Dāwūd and his followers adopted it. They distinguished between making divorce conditional with the intention of swearing an oath and doing so with the intention of effecting divorce, just as they distinguished between these two intentions when making a vow or something else conditional.

The distinction between them is clear. Someone swearing an oath does not want the consequence to occur, even if the condition is fulfilled. An example is a Muslim saying:

“If I do such-and-such, then I am a Jew or a Christian.”

He abhors disbelief even if the condition is fulfilled. He undertakes this commitment only so that it will not become binding and to deter himself from fulfilling the condition—not because he intends the consequence to occur when the condition is fulfilled.

The same applies to an oath involving Islam, as when a non-Muslim under Muslim protection says:

“If I do such-and-such, then I am a Muslim.”

Likewise, someone swearing an oath involving a vow, declaring something forbidden to himself, zihār (declaring his wife as forbidden to him as his mother), divorce, or emancipation may say:

“If I do such-and-such, then Hajj is incumbent upon me, my slaves are free, my wives are divorced, and my property is given in charity.”

---

## Page 16185

then he dislikes these consequences even if the condition is fulfilled. He made them conditional only to deter himself from fulfilling the condition, not with the intention of bringing them about. When the condition is fulfilled, a conditional pronouncement intended to bring about its consequence falls under the rulings governing such pronouncements, whereas one intended as an oath falls under the rulings governing oaths. Allah has explained in His Book the rulings governing divorce and those governing oaths.

If he says:

“If you steal or commit adultery, you are divorced,”

he may intend this as an oath: he would prefer her to remain with him despite this conduct rather than divorce her. His sole intention is to deter and frighten her so that she does not do it. This is an oath and does not cause divorce to take effect. Alternatively, he may intend to effect divorce: he would prefer to separate from her rather than remain with her despite such conduct, and therefore chooses that she should be divorced from him if she does it. In this case, divorce takes effect. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About someone who swore not to speak to his brother’s relative by marriage, and swore by triple divorce that this man would not enter his house, but the man then entered without his consent.

He replied:

If the person swearing believed that the man whose conduct was the subject of the oath would obey him and honour his oath by not entering once he had sworn, but matters turned out otherwise, and he would not have sworn had he known this, then the scholars disagree as to whether he has broken his oath. The stronger view is that he has not broken it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16186

He was asked:

About a man who swore by triple divorce that he would not live in the place where he was residing. He then moved out and vacated it. Is he permitted to return or not?

He replied:

If the reason for which he swore has ceased to exist, he may return. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who swore by triple divorce that his wife would not put her hand into his bag or take anything from it. He said this was for a period of four months. Afterwards, he swore a second oath that she would not pass on what she had heard to anyone.

She subsequently told people, so her husband said to her:

“Did I not swear by divorce that you would not pass it on to anyone? Yet you have passed it on!”

She said:

“I passed it on, but I did not know that I was bound by an oath.”

He said:

“Now the divorce has taken effect. Get up, give me my bag, and give me the thread from it. No oath remains binding on me, and the divorce has taken effect.”

---

## Page 16187

She said:

“I did not know that we were bound by an oath indefinitely; I thought the oath was only for five or six days.”

He said to her:

“I do not know. You are now divorced from me three times.”

Does divorce take effect against her by virtue of the first oath or the second?

He replied:

If she believed that his oath was no longer binding and then did what he had sworn she would not do, he has not broken his oath. If he said, “You are now divorced from me three times,” because he believed that the divorce had already taken effect, no divorce takes effect by that statement. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who entered into a contract of manumission with his slave. The slave caused him distress, prompting him to swear by triple divorce that he would not stop beating him and keeping him in custody until he produced his accounts or returned to him the amount sought from the stipend. Is there any lawful way for him to be released? Please give us a ruling.

He replied—may Allah be pleased with him:

If the person concerning whom the oath was sworn is unable to produce the required accounts or return the requested amount from the stipend, neither may be demanded of him. Rather, the authority must compel the man who swore the oath to leave him alone. If he is compelled to do so, he does not break his oath, according to the correct one of the scholars’ two opinions, and no divorce takes effect. This applies whether he is compelled by the sultan’s military governor or a comparable official, by a judicial governor, or by a secretary superior to him whose just orders are binding upon him. The same applies if

---

## Page 16188

the slave is not obliged to produce either of them. For if this is not obligatory under the law with which Allah sent His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the man who swore the oath must be compelled to leave him alone. If he leaves him alone under these circumstances, he does not break his oath.

The same applies if the man who swore the oath believed the situation to be a certain way, but it proved otherwise. For example, he might believe that the accounts would reveal matters that must be disclosed, only for this to prove otherwise. According to many scholars, he does not break his oath if he leaves him alone. Likewise, if he believed that the other man was obliged to return the stipend and swore an oath to that effect, but it then became clear that this was not obligatory, he does not break his oath according to many scholars.

Likewise, if he believed that the person concerning whom the oath was sworn could perform the required act, but it became clear that he was unable to do so, he does not break his oath according to many scholars. This is the better of the two opinions and the one more strongly supported by Islamic law. Similarly, if he believed that the other man had misappropriated or stolen money and swore an oath demanding its return, but it then became clear that he had neither misappropriated nor stolen it, he does not break his oath according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who, while angry, swore by triple divorce that his wife would not enter her paternal aunt’s house. His wife was blessed with a child. After that, the woman concerning whom the oath had been sworn entered her paternal aunt’s house. Some people had told the man who swore the oath that if his wife gave birth and then entered, he would not have broken his oath. Please give us a ruling.

---

## Page 16189

He replied:

If the man who swore the oath believed that he would not break it once his wife had given birth, and she entered on the basis of that belief, then he has not broken his oath. His oath nevertheless remains binding: if the act covered by it is subsequently committed knowingly and deliberately, he breaks his oath. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a man who swore an oath concerning his wife, saying to her, “If you go out while I am away, you are divorced three times.” When he returned from his journey, she said to him, “By Allah, I needed to use the bathhouse and could not bathe at home.”

He replied:

If she believed that these circumstances were not covered by his oath and that she would not be contravening it by doing so, then the man who swore the oath has not broken it.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man with two wives whose house was found to be missing a sum of money. He swore, on pain of divorcing his new wife three times, that unless he located the sum missing from his house, he would not allow his first wife to remain in his house. He believed that his first wife had misappropriated the sum concerning which he had sworn the oath.

---

## Page 16190

He—may Allah strengthen him—replied:

If he believed that his first wife had misappropriated his money and, for that reason, swore that he would expel her unless she produced it, but it subsequently became clear that she had not misappropriated it, he is not obliged to expel her, nor has he broken his oath. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a man who swore, on pain of triple divorce, that he would not marry his daughter to a particular man. He then married her to someone else, who subsequently divorced her irrevocably with three pronouncements. May he now marry her to the man concerning whom he had sworn the oath, or not?

He replied:

If the oath-taker’s intention or the circumstances that prompted the oath indicate that it concerned only that particular proposed marriage, he may marry her to him on the second occasion. For example, he may have refused to marry her to him because the suitor had demanded a large trousseau, whereas on the second occasion he was content to marry her without one. But if the reason remains, he breaks his oath. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who had performed Hajj and had two wives, and who swore, on pain of triple divorce, that he would not give them any food.

---

## Page 16191

He replied:

If his intention was that he was refraining for a particular reason, as indicated by the circumstances giving rise to the oath, and that reason has ceased to exist, his oath is no longer binding according to the stronger of the two scholarly views. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who swore, on pain of triple divorce, that his wife must not leave his house without his permission. She then said, “Today, your mother and I are having lunch together.” He understood that his mother would come to her, while his wife understood that he had given her permission, so she went to his mother’s home.

He replied:

In these circumstances, divorce does not take effect according to the sounder of the two scholarly views. This is one of the two views of al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. This concerns the case of someone acting in ignorance or forgetfulness, about which the disagreement is well known: does the oath-taker break his oath, or not? Or should a distinction be drawn between oaths whose breach can be expiated and other oaths? The correct view is that he does not break his oath in either case. For keeping or breaking an oath is analogous to obeying or disobeying a command, when the statement to which the oath relates is a directive.

The statement to which an oath relates is either a “declarative statement,” in which case the oath-taker’s purpose is to affirm or deny its truth, or a “directive,” in which case the oath-taker’s purpose is

---

## Page 16192

to urge and restrain. He urges himself or the person concerning whom he swears, and restrains himself or that person. This is thus a command or prohibition reinforced by an oath. Breaking such an oath is like disobeying a command given without an oath.

It is well established in Islamic law that someone who does a prohibited act forgetfully, or mistakenly believing that it is not the prohibited act—as with those who adopt a tenable interpretation—is neither sinful nor disobedient. Allah has answered the believers’ supplication:

“Our Lord, do not take us to task if we forget or make a mistake.”

Likewise, someone who forgets the oath, or believes that what he has done is not what the oath concerned, owing to an interpretation or a mistake, such as mishearing or the like, has not contravened the oath and therefore has not broken it. In this respect, there is no difference between an oath sworn by Allah Most High and other kinds of oaths. The rulings governing oaths differ according to what is sworn by, but not according to what is sworn about. The discussion here concerns what is sworn about, not what is sworn by.

It is also well known that someone who swears on pain of divorce or emancipation has not made a purely conditional declaration, such as one contingent upon sunrise. Nor does he intend both the condition and its consequence to occur, as with a vow of pious devotion or a declaration conditional upon compensation, such as a divorce granted in return for compensation. Rather, his purpose is to urge himself or restrain the person concerning whom he swore, and to restrain himself or that person, just as someone making a vow out of obstinacy or anger intends to do.

For this reason, the jurists agree on calling this an oath. The correct position in the school of Ahmad and others is that an exception clause may validly be appended to it. This differs from a purely conditional declaration, which is a pronouncement with deferred effect and, according to the correct view, is not an oath. An exception clause is of no avail in such a declaration according to those who do not allow such clauses in pronouncements that themselves bring about a legal effect, such as Mālik, Ahmad, and others. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16193

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who found his maternal aunt’s son with his wife and swore on pain of divorce that his maternal aunt’s son had been with his wife, as was indeed the case.

He replied:

If the man is truthful in what he swears, he has not broken his oath. Likewise, if he believes himself to be truthful, he has not broken his oath, even if the reality is otherwise, according to the sounder of the two scholarly views. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who swore on pain of divorce that he would not marry a particular woman, but then changed his mind and wished to marry her. May he do so?

He replied—may Allah illuminate his resting place and his tomb:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. He may marry her, and no divorce takes effect with respect to her when he marries her, according to the majority of the early generations. This is also the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others.

---

## Page 16194

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man’s wife refused to have intercourse with him. Hurt by her refusal, he swore on pain of divorce, while she was pregnant, that he would not have intercourse with her after she gave birth. Would divorce take effect if he had intercourse with her after childbirth? Should the circumstance that provoked the oath be taken into account?

He replied:

If he has intercourse with her after childbirth, consideration must be given to his intention when swearing and to the reason for his oath. If he swore for a particular reason and that reason has ceased to exist, he has not broken his oath, according to the sounder of the two scholarly views within Ahmad’s school and others.

For someone may swear an oath concerning a particular matter for a specific reason: for example, he may swear not to enter a town because of injustice he witnessed there, and that injustice subsequently ceases; or he may swear not to speak to a certain person, whose sinful conduct subsequently ceases; and so forth. There are “two views” within Ahmad’s school and others as to whether he breaks his oath in such circumstances. The sounder view is that he does not, because urging and restraining through an oath are comparable to commanding and forbidding.

Thus, one who swears an oath to restrain himself or someone else is like one who forbids an act. If someone forbids entering a town or speaking to a particular person for a reason, and that reason subsequently ceases to exist, the prohibition ceases. This is like refraining from greeting a man first because he is a disbeliever, after which he embraces Islam; or refraining from entering a land because it is a land of war, after which it becomes a land of Islam; and similar cases. When a ruling is based on a particular cause, it ceases when that cause ceases.

---

## Page 16195

Thus, if a man swears not to have intercourse with his wife, intending to punish her because she puts him off and defies him when he requests it, then, if she repents of this and becomes obedient and agreeable, the reason for the abstention he imposed upon her no longer applies—just as if he had abstained from her because of her defiance and that defiance then ceased. But if he intended to refrain from intercourse with her forever because of her earlier sin, whether or not she repented—such that even if he knew she would sincerely repent, his purpose would still be to punish her for what had passed, just as a man punishes another for a past sin whether or not he has repented of it, not to deter him from future wrongdoing, but merely to appease his own anger or the like—then this is a different case. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man swore on pain of divorce that he would not have intercourse with his wife for six months, with only one divorce remaining. His intention was not to have intercourse with her until that period had elapsed. What should he do once it has elapsed?

He replied:

Once the period has elapsed, he may have intercourse with her and incurs no liability, provided she did not demand intercourse from him when four months had elapsed. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad, al-Shāfi‘ī, and the majority of scholars. He is termed a “mūlī” (a husband who has sworn to abstain from intercourse with his wife).

---

## Page 16196

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had a wife and a slave woman. He took the slave woman as a concubine, and his wife became jealous. He therefore swore never again to have intercourse with the slave woman, then freed her. She married and lived with her husband for a time, after which he died. May the man who freed her marry her?

He replied:

If his intention or the circumstances that prompted the oath indicate that he meant not to have intercourse with her as her owner, he may marry her and have intercourse with her. But if they indicate that he meant not to have intercourse with her under any circumstances, whether through ownership or marriage, he breaks his oath if he does what he swore not to do. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man owed a sum of money to two people and said, “My wife is divorced three times over if the month ends before I pay them the sum.” Had he not sworn, he would have been imprisoned. Now he has not obtained the money, and today is the last day of the month. He fears that he will break his oath. If he divorces his wife once in return for compensation, will this protect him from the threefold divorce taking effect, or not?

He replied:

If he was wrongfully coerced into taking the oath, as would be the case if he was unable to repay the debt

---

## Page 16197

and was forced to swear on pain of imprisonment and beating, his oath is not binding, and he incurs no liability for breaking it. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man buys vegetables, paying a levy on some purchases but not on others. Someone whom he fears came to him, so he swore an oath on pain of divorce that he would pay the levy on whatever he bought. May he purchase al-falt?

He replied:

If he was wrongfully coerced into taking the oath, it is not binding, and he incurs no liability for breaking it. If he has not been placed within the power of the tax-farmer’s agents, the latter has no claim against him, whether under Islamic law or by custom. If he has no claim against him, the man does not break his oath by withholding payment. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man left a legal document in his brother’s house, and it went missing. Several days later, he asked for it but could not find it. Believing that it still existed, he swore an oath on pain of divorce that he would not enter his brother’s house until the document was given to him. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If the document had already ceased to exist before he took the oath, but he believed that it still existed, then, according to the majority of scholars, he does not break his oath, for two reasons:

First, he swore to something impossible in itself, just as if he had sworn that he would drink the water in a jug when there was no water in it. According to most scholars, such a person does not break his oath.

Second, he believed that the document still existed and could be handed over. Thus, he swore concerning something that he believed possessed a particular characteristic, but it proved to be otherwise.

---

## Page 16198

Chapter: Making Divorce Conditional

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man swore an oath on pain of divorce, then added the qualification “if Allah wills” after a brief pause, long enough for words to be spoken. What is the ruling?

He replied:

In these circumstances, no divorce takes effect, and no expiation is required of him.

Even if someone prompted him, saying, “Say: ‘If Allah wills,’” this would still avail him, even if adding the qualification had not occurred to him until he was prompted. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man became angry with his wife and said, “You are divorced three times.” His wife said to him, “Say ‘now.’” He said, “Now,” intending an exception. What is the ruling?

---

## Page 16199

He replied:

If he believed that saying, “Divorce shall be binding upon me, if Allah wills,” would not cause divorce to take effect, and his purpose was to frighten her with these words rather than to effect a divorce, then no divorce took effect.

If he said, “If Allah wills,” on that occasion, then according to the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, divorce made conditional upon Allah’s will does not take effect. According to the position of Mālik and Ahmad, it does take effect, as is also reported from Ibn ʿAbbās. However, since this man intended and believed that no divorce would take effect, he understood these words as words that did not effect a divorce. Thus, he did not intend to pronounce divorce.

When a speaker deliberately utters words which he does not believe will effect a divorce—as when a non-Arabic speaker utters an expression without understanding its meaning—no divorce takes effect. By contrast, divorce pronounced in jest takes effect because the speaker intended to pronounce divorce, even though he did not intend to bring it into effect. This man intended neither.

His case resembles that of a man who sees a woman and says, “You are divorced,” thinking her to be a woman other than his wife, only to discover that she is his wife. According to the correct view, no divorce takes effect thereby. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16200

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man adopted the doctrine of “circularity” attributed to Ibn Surayj. He then swore, on pain of divorce, that he would not do something, but subsequently did it. He later abandoned that doctrine and took his wife back. After that, he swore, on pain of threefold divorce, that he would not do something. He subsequently said to his wife, “You are divorced.” Does a threefold divorce take effect in his case, or may he invoke the first doctrine mentioned?

He replied:

The “Surayjī doctrine” is invalid in Islam. It is an innovation on which none of the Companions, the Successors, or their followers ever issued a fatwa. It was only put forward by a group of jurists after the third century AH, and the majority of Muslim jurists rejected it. Their rejection is correct, for the invalidity of what its proponents said is evident in several respects.

One is that it is a self-evident teaching of Islam that Allah has permitted divorce just as He has permitted marriage, and that the religion of the Muslims differs from that of the Christians, who do not permit divorce. If there were anything in the religion of the Muslims that made divorce impossible, their religion would become like that of the Christians.

---

## Page 16201

“Their specious argument” is that they say: If a man tells his wife, “If my divorce of you takes effect, then you are divorced three times before it,” and subsequently pronounces an immediate divorce, the conditional divorce must take effect. If the conditional divorce took effect, the immediate divorce would take effect. Thus, its taking effect would entail its not taking effect, and so it does not take effect. This is mistaken.

Their statement, “If the immediate divorce took effect, the conditional divorce would take effect,” would be correct only if the conditional stipulation were valid. If the stipulation is invalid, however, the conditional divorce need not take effect. The stipulation is indeed invalid, because it entails a divorce taking effect after three prior divorces, which is invalid in the religion of the Muslims. It also entails saying, “If my divorce of you takes effect, my divorce of you does not take effect.” This combines two contradictories: if the condition is not fulfilled, its consequence does not follow; if the condition is fulfilled, the divorce necessarily takes effect. To say that it nevertheless does not take effect would entail its both taking effect and not taking effect, which combines two contradictories.

Furthermore, once a divorce has taken effect, it cannot subsequently be undone. Since the divorcing man’s words entail something impossible under Islamic law—a divorce taking effect after three prior divorces—and something rationally impossible—divorce both taking effect and not taking effect—the proponent of the Surayjī doctrine contradicts both reason and religion.

However, if the man who swore the oath believed it to be valid, whether through independent legal reasoning or by following a scholar, and subsequently pronounced divorce believing that it would not take effect, no divorce takes effect. This is because he did not intend to utter anything that he believed would constitute divorce. His case is therefore like that of a non-Arabic speaker who utters the word of divorce without understanding it. The same applies if he addresses words of divorce to someone he believes is not his wife, only to discover that she is his wife: according to the correct view, the divorce does not take effect.

Even if it subsequently became clear to him that the Surayjī doctrine was invalid and that the immediate divorce does take effect,

---

## Page 16202

his subsequent discovery of the truth would not cause the divorce to take effect.

Likewise, if, as a precaution, he took his wife back, fearing that the divorce might have taken effect or believing that it had, it would not take effect. Even if, after discovering the invalidity of the Surayjī doctrine, he acknowledged that the divorce had taken effect, no divorce would take effect through this acknowledgment.

If he believed that the divorce had taken effect, took his wife back, and then did what he had sworn not to do, believing that he had already broken his oath once and therefore could not break it a second time, no divorce would take effect through that act. That act is one matter, while his oath not to do that thing remains in force. If the reason for the oath persists, the oath remains in force; if that reason has ceased to exist, he may, on that basis, do what he swore not to do without breaking his oath.

Likewise, if he married her again and then did what he had sworn not to do, believing that an irrevocable separation had occurred and that the original oath was no longer binding, he would not have broken his oath, because he believed it was no longer in force. Similarly, according to the more correct of the scholars’ two views, a person does not break his oath if he does not know that what he has done is the very thing he swore not to do.

As for subsequently saying to his wife, “You are divorced,” this divorce does take effect. If he believes that this pronouncement brings the total to three divorces and acknowledges that he has divorced her three times, neither this belief nor this acknowledgment causes any divorce to take effect.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“What is your view on acting upon the ‘Surayjī doctrine,’ whereby a man says to his wife, ‘If I divorce you, you are divorced three times before it’? This is known as ‘the question of Ibn Surayj.’”

---

## Page 16203

Answer:

None of the early generations of this community or its imams issued a ruling endorsing this doctrine: neither the Companions, nor the Successors, nor the imams of the established schools—Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad—nor their contemporary disciples, such as Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad, al-Muzanī, al-Buwaytī, Ibn al-Qāsim, Ibn Wahb, Ibrāhīm al-Harbī, Abū Bakr al-Athram, Abū Dāwūd and others. None of them issued such a ruling. Only a group of later jurists endorsed it, and the majority of the Muslim community rejected their position, including the followers of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and Ahmad, and many followers of al-Shāfiʿī. Al-Ghazālī initially upheld it, but later retracted his position and demonstrated its invalidity. It is well established in the religion of the Muslims that Muslim marriage cannot be like Christian marriage.

The circularity they imagined here is unfounded. They assumed that if the unconditional divorce took effect, the conditional divorce would also take effect. Yet the latter would take effect only if the condition were valid, whereas the condition is invalid because it entails something impossible under Islamic law: a divorce taking effect after having been preceded by three divorces. This is impossible under Islamic law, and since the Surayjī device entails this legal impossibility, it is invalid.

If a man has sworn an oath involving divorce, believing that he would not break his oath, and later discovers that this is impermissible, he should keep his wife. No divorce takes effect on account of what has already occurred, and he must repent going forward.

In sum, if a man says to his wife, “If I divorce you, you are divorced three times before it,” and then divorces her, the unconditional divorce takes effect according to the stronger view, while the conditional divorce does not take effect alongside it. For if the conditional divorce—which consists of three divorces—were to take effect, the unconditional divorce would not take effect, since it would exceed the permitted number of divorces. And if the unconditional divorce does not take effect,

---

## Page 16204

the conditional divorce does not take effect either.

Another view holds that neither divorce takes effect, because the unconditional divorce taking effect entails the conditional divorce taking effect, while the conditional divorce taking effect entails the unconditional divorce not taking effect. It is not permissible to follow this opinion. Ibn Surayj is free of what has been attributed to him on this matter, as Shaykh ‘Izz al-Dīn stated.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is the “Ibn Surayj device” valid or not? If we say that it is invalid, what of someone who followed his authority on this matter and acted upon it, then sought Allah’s forgiveness upon learning that it was invalid?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. This device is an innovation in Islam. None of the Companions, the Successors or the four imams issued a ruling endorsing it. Only a group of later scholars endorsed it, and a body of Muslim scholars rejected their position. Whoever followed someone’s authority on this matter and then repented, Allah has pardoned what is past. He need not separate from his wife, even if he had married her, provided that he was acting on what he believed to be a valid interpretation. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married a woman and had a child by her. The witnesses or others advised him that, when he went in to his wife, he should say to her, “If I divorce you, you are divorced three times before that divorce.” Is that marriage contract valid or not?

---

## Page 16205

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The marriage is valid and need not be contracted anew. The “Ibn Surayj device,” when its formula is not uttered, does not invalidate the marriage, by scholarly consensus. However, if he subsequently divorces her, that divorce takes effect according to the overwhelming majority of scholars, including the followers of Mālik, Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah, and many—if not most—of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man’s wife asked him for a divorce, so he divorced her and said, “I will never return to her again.” A friend of his met him and said, “I will not believe you about this unless you say, ‘Whenever I marry this woman, she is divorced according to Mālik’s school.’” He did not understand the rulings of Islamic law. May he take her back?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If by “Whenever I marry her” he intended either taking her back or entering into a new marriage contract—as his words apparently indicate—then whenever he takes her back before her waiting period expires, she is divorced a second time. If he then takes her back again, she is divorced a third time.

If, however, he leaves her until her waiting period expires, she becomes separated from him without his retaining the right to take her back. If he subsequently marries her, those who hold that a divorce made conditional upon marriage takes effect in such a case—such as Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and Ahmad according to one reported opinion—say that she is divorced when he marries her.

As for those who do not hold this view—such as al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad according to his well-known opinion—she was revocably divorced when he made her divorce conditional, and a revocably divorced woman is treated as a wife in such matters. But does the intervening separation terminate

---

## Page 16206

the legal effect of the condition? The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that it does not. He explicitly distinguished, when divorce is made conditional upon marriage, between a woman who is still in her waiting period and one who is not. According to his school, therefore, she is divorced when he marries her. This is also one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two opinions.

According to al-Shāfi‘ī’s other opinion—that an irrevocable separation terminates the legal effect of the condition—which is also one opinion reported from Ahmad, his saying, “If I marry her,” is like his saying, “If you enter the house.” Once she becomes irrevocably separated from him, this oath ceases to bind. He may therefore marry her without any divorce taking effect as a result. This is the view preferred by many of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers.

As for his saying, “According to Mālik’s school,” this amounts to committing himself to a particular school, but that commitment is not binding. Rather, he may follow al-Shāfi‘ī’s school. If the divorce was irrevocable in exchange for compensation, and he subsequently pronounced the conditional divorce, whether during her waiting period or otherwise, then the condition concerns a woman who is no longer his wife. According to al-Shāfi‘ī’s school, no divorce therefore takes effect if he marries her.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man who follows al-Shāfi‘ī’s school became irrevocably separated from his wife through three divorces. She subsequently married another man and became separated from her second husband. She then wished to reconcile with her first husband because she had children by him. He told her, “I cannot afford to maintain you or provide you with clothing.” She would not accept this, so he said to her, “Whenever you become lawful for me, you become forbidden to me.” Does she thereby become forbidden to him? And is that permissible?

---

## Page 16207

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. She does not become forbidden to him by that statement. There are, however, two views concerning it:

The first is that he may marry her without incurring any further obligation. The second is that he must offer expiation: either the expiation for zihār, according to one view, or the expiation for breaking an oath, according to another.

Likewise, the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others is that he may marry her and that no divorce takes effect through this statement, although there is disagreement over expiation. Only those who allow divorce to be made conditional upon marriage, such as Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, hold that divorce takes effect through such a statement—and then only if a declaration that she is forbidden to him is regarded as divorce, as Mālik holds, or if he intends divorce by it, as Abū Hanīfah holds.

As for al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, according to them, even if he says, “Whenever I marry you, you are divorced,” no divorce takes effect. How much less, then, through a declaration that she is forbidden to him! Ahmad, however, according to the well-known view reported from him, allows a declaration of zihār made before marriage to be deemed valid and binding upon him, unlike al-Shāfi‘ī. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> End of Volume Thirty-Three.
